An independent, original study guide

This book is a self-contained preparation aid for a foundations-level associate exam on using an AI assistant effectively, accurately, and responsibly in business and technical work. It is written to be read cover to cover, to be used as a reference during revision, and to be printed or exported as a PDF.

Important: This is an independent, original study guide. It is not affiliated with, sponsored by, endorsed by, or approved by any certification body, cloud provider, or AI vendor. It does not reproduce, summarize, or replace any paid course, lecture recording, or official training material. Every chapter was written from first principles for this project, with its own titles, ordering, examples, explanations, scenarios, and questions. The exam code used in the title is used descriptively; no official status is claimed.

Trademarks: "Claude" and other product names mentioned in this book are trademarks of their respective owners. They are used only for identification and explanation. No affiliation or endorsement is implied.

Why this book is organized differently

Most course outlines follow the order in which topics were recorded. This book does not. The material has been deliberately rearranged so that ideas build on one another: you meet the interface first, then learn to write instructions, then learn to check what comes back, and only then choose models, design workflows, and study governance. Practice chapters come last, when you have enough grounding to reason through scenarios rather than guess.

Part Chapters What you will be able to do
I — Orientation and the Exam 1–2 Navigate the workspace and read a scenario question
II — Prompting Foundations 3–5 Write instructions, chains, and task-family briefs
III — Verification and Responsible Output 6–8 Detect errors, bias, and shape output for an audience
IV — Models, Features, and Context 9–10 Choose a model and manage a long conversation
V — Business Analysis and Workflow Design 11–13 Turn needs into use cases, pilots, and workflows
VI — Projects, Data, and Governance 14–16 Configure projects, handle data safely, and escalate
VII — Exam Practice 17–18 Work through scenarios and two full practice papers
VIII — Final Preparation 19–20 Consolidate with clinics and a final revision sheet

How to use each chapter

Each chapter follows the same rhythm:

  1. Learning objectives tell you what should be different after reading.
  2. Key concepts carry the explanation, with tables and examples where useful.
  3. Worked scenario models the reasoning an exam question is testing.
  4. Common mistakes flags the traps that appear in distractor options.
  5. Chapter summary gives you a revision sheet.
  6. Checkpoint questions let you test yourself immediately; every answer has a rationale, including why the other options are wrong.

A suggested approach: read the objectives, skim the summary, read the chapter, then answer the checkpoint questions without looking at the answers. Write down any option you got wrong and revisit it the next day.

A six-week preparation plan

Week Focus Chapters
1 Workspace, interface, and exam habits 1–2
2 Prompting and verification 3–8
3 Models, features, and context 9–10
4 Business analysis and workflow design 11–13
5 Projects, data, and governance 14–16
6 Scenario practice, clinics, and review 17–20

If you have less time, prioritize Chapters 3, 6, 7, 9, 10, 14, 15, and 16. Those topics generate the largest share of scenario questions.

Conventions used in this book

  • Exam note — a point that is frequently tested.
  • Common mistake — a wrong answer that looks plausible.
  • Try it — a small exercise you can perform in a scratch session.
  • Code and prompt blocks use plain text, JSON, Python, or bash, and are illustrative; they are not meant to be used unchanged in production.
  • Where a product or plan is discussed, the principle is stated first and the product name second. The exam rewards principle-level reasoning.
  • Role names are used throughout. No individual is named in this book.

A note on scope

A foundations exam tests judgment: which surface, which model, which level of human review, and which verification step fits the constraint you were given. It does not reward memorizing interface trivia or pricing tables. When two answers both sound reasonable, ask yourself which one actually satisfies every constraint in the scenario — especially the constraints about privacy, safety, and honesty about what can be verified. That habit is the single most useful preparation strategy this book can offer.

End of front matter.

Welcome to the workspace. Before you can use an AI assistant well, you need to know what you are looking at, what the system may do with your material, and what the exam is really asking. This chapter lays that groundwork in a practical way: it treats the assistant as a tool for real work, and the exam as a test of judgment rather than a memory contest.

Many candidates expect to recite product details. That expectation is understandable, and it is the fastest route to a poor score. A foundations-level associate exam cares far more about whether you can spot the one fact in a scenario that changes the answer, whether you know when a polished reply still needs checking, and whether you can communicate a limit honestly. Details matter, but as support for decisions, not as ends in themselves.

Keep one idea in mind: nearly every exam question is a small workplace story with a constraint hidden inside it. Your job is to find that constraint. The rest of this chapter gives you the vocabulary and habits to do it consistently.

Learning objectives

After working through this chapter, you should be able to:

  • Distinguish judgment-based exam questions from trivia recall, and explain why the former dominate a foundations assessment.
  • Read a scenario question methodically and identify the constraint that determines the correct choice.
  • Name the main parts of a typical assistant interface and describe each part's purpose without depending on a particular product's labels.
  • Compare individual, team, and enterprise plan tiers by administrative control, data handling, and collaboration rather than by price.
  • Explain the difference between a casual chat and a work product that carries an obligation to verify.
  • Run a short orientation checklist when starting a first session in a new workspace.
  • Manage exam time, flag uncertain questions, and return to them without losing your place.

Key concepts

What a foundations exam actually measures

A foundations exam sits at the start of a learning path. It establishes that you understand the shape of the tool, the boundaries of safe use, and the habits that make output trustworthy. It does not establish that you have memorized every control in every version of a product. Interfaces change; good judgment does not.

Think about the decisions a person makes on the job with an assistant. They decide whether a task suits the tool at all, what context to supply, which feature fits, whether an answer needs checking, and who should sign off before it is shared. They decide what to disclose to a colleague and what to keep private. These are decisions under constraints, and scenario questions simulate them.

The exam rewards three broad capabilities. The first is judgment: choosing a reasonable action given the stated goals and limits. The second is safe use: recognizing when data is sensitive, when a claim needs a source, and when a request should be declined or escalated. The third is clear communication: shaping a prompt or response so another person can act on it. None of these is "recall the name of the setting." Recognition may appear, but usually as scaffolding around a decision, not the decision itself.

Compare the two question styles below. The left looks like trivia; the right looks like the exam.

Question style What it asks What it really tests Likely exam emphasis
Recall "Which control sets response length?" Memory of a label Low on its own
Recognition with purpose "An answer is too long for a briefing note; which control should change?" Matching a need to a control Moderate
Scenario judgment "A customer draft cites no sources and includes a number the analyst cannot confirm. What next?" Verification, risk, and sign-off High
Scenario with constraint "The same draft is needed in ten minutes and the source is confidential. Which path is fast and compliant?" Weighing two competing constraints Highest

The more a question embeds a goal, an audience, and a limit, the more it tests judgment. A long question is not a burden; its length is where the clues are.

Exam note: A foundations exam is not trying to catch you on trivia. It is asking whether you would make a sound decision on a normal workday. Answer as a careful professional would act, not as a manual's index might read.

A second reason the exam favors judgment is durability. Products evolve; buttons get renamed and features appear. An assessment tied to such details would age badly, while one built on principles — context quality, verification, privacy, appropriate use — stays meaningful. That is why this book teaches principles and uses generic labels for controls: learn the principle, and you can map it onto any interface.

Reading a scenario question: finding the deciding constraint

A scenario question is a short story with a decision at the end. Most of the story is context; one or two details carry the answer. Find those details before you look at the options. Reading the options first anchors you on a plausible choice and then makes you read the story to confirm it — exactly backwards.

Use a consistent routine. Read the stem and identify who acts, what they want, and for whom. Look for the constraint: a deadline, audience, data classification, permission, policy, or requirement such as "must be verifiable." Then, before reading the options, state the answer in your own words. Compare that statement to the options last. This protects you from distractors that are true but irrelevant.

Constraints fall into a few recurring types. Spotting them matters more than memorizing question text.

Constraint type Typical wording What it decides
Audience "for a customer," "for the board," "for peer review" Formality, detail, and verification needed
Data sensitivity "confidential," "contains personal data," "internal only" Whether a tool or connector may be used at all
Time or latency "needed immediately," "high-volume," "user is waiting" Model tier, reasoning depth, or whether to defer
Verification need "must be cited," "number must be confirmed," "will be relied on" Whether to ground, source, or escalate
Permission or role "read-only access," "team member," "no admin rights" What the person can change or approve
Policy or rule "per organizational policy," "must be disclosed" Which action is compliant regardless of convenience

One constraint can flip an answer. Speeding up a summary of public material might favor a faster model. Add "the source is a confidential contract," and data handling becomes the deciding factor instead. The action changes though the task looks identical. Candidates who skim for keywords get tripped up: "summary" suggests one path, while "confidential" demands another.

Two traps deserve mention. The attractive but unsafe option solves the visible task and ignores the constraint: "paste the document into a public tool" may be fast and produce a good summary, but if the document is confidential the choice is wrong regardless of quality. The perfect but impractical option honors the constraint but ignores a stated goal such as a hard deadline. The correct answer satisfies the constraint first, then serves the goal as well as possible within it.

When two options both seem defensible, ask which respects the strictest reading of the stated constraint. Examiners intend one clear deciding factor. If you cannot find it, you may be inventing a constraint the scenario never stated. Stay literal: use only what the text says.

Try it: Cover the options. Write one sentence beginning "The key constraint is…" Then uncover the options and choose. If your sentence matches none of them, re-read the stem — you missed the constraint or over-weighted a minor detail.

The assistant interface: a generic tour

Products differ, but the underlying parts are strikingly similar. Learn the generic map and you can orient yourself in any workspace within a minute.

Interface area What it is What you use it for
Conversation area The central thread of messages and replies Asking, reading, refining, keeping a working record
Model and feature controls Selectors for capability, reasoning depth, and tools Matching the engine and features to the task
Navigation panel The list of chats, projects, and spaces Finding prior work and switching contexts
Project space A persistent container with instructions and knowledge Holding recurring work with stable background
Artifact space A separate rendering area for documents, code, or visual drafts Iterating on an output apart from the chat
Attachment controls Ways to add files, images, or links Supplying source material for grounding
Entry points Text box, voice input, and dictation Getting words into the assistant
Scheduling controls Options to run a task at a set time or interval Recurring or deferred work

The conversation area is the primary working surface. Each new turn is informed by earlier ones, which is why a clean thread is valuable: the assistant can see decisions you already made. A thread mixing five unrelated topics dilutes that context. The conversation is active context, not an archive — old messages still influence new answers.

The model and feature controls sit near the conversation, often as a selector or small menu. They choose a capability tier, request longer reasoning, or enable tools such as research or code execution. These are not the same as your prompt. The prompt says what you want; the controls shape how the assistant works. In the exam, a question about "which control" asks you to reason from the task: routine and repetitive suggests a lighter setting; ambiguous and high-stakes suggests a more capable one.

The navigation panel lists chats and project spaces. Its value is retrieval, so work that matters should be easy to find — name threads clearly and keep related work together. A user who cannot find last week's analysis has effectively lost that context.

A project space persists across conversations. It can hold background instructions and uploaded knowledge, so you need not repeat setup. Think of it as a dedicated desk for a recurring kind of work: a team maintaining a product FAQ keeps policy documents and house style in one project rather than pasting them into every chat. This distinction matters in questions contrasting a one-off question with recurring work.

An artifact space renders certain outputs apart from the chat — a document to open, code to inspect, a visual draft, or a simple interactive view. The point is iteration: you refine the artifact without losing the conversational record of how you got there. A short factual reply lives in the chat; a deliverable someone will edit and reuse often benefits from an artifact.

Attachment controls bring external material in — this is how grounding works. Supply the source and ask the assistant to work from it. Check what was actually attached and whether it was used. A confident answer that ignores the supplied file is a warning sign, not a success.

Entry points include the text box, voice input, and dictation. Voice captures ideas quickly but introduces transcription risk: names, numbers, and technical terms are easy to get wrong. Check a dictated figure before sending it, and treat a spoken answer like any other draft.

Scheduling controls let a task run later or on a recurring basis. Scheduling suits stable inputs and predictable outputs — a daily digest from a known source, for instance. It is a poor fit when each run needs fresh judgment or the data changes in ways the schedule cannot detect. Treat scheduled output as unattended output: it still needs a review path.

Common mistake: Assuming the model selector changes correctness. It changes capability and speed trade-offs. Accuracy still depends on the context you provide and the checking you do; a more capable model is no substitute for a good prompt or a verification step.

Accounts and plan tiers: principles, not prices

Products package features into tiers, and names change. What does not change is the logic. Understand the logic and you can answer any tier question by reasoning rather than recalling a price list.

An individual account is the simplest case. One person signs in, sees their own chats and projects, and controls the settings. Administrative control is personal and minimal — no central owner manages others. Data handling follows standard provider terms in the individual's own workspace. Collaboration is ad hoc: share an output or invite someone into a space when needed.

Above it sits a team or business tier. An organization gains administrative control: it can manage membership, set some policies, and often choose how submitted data is handled under an organizational agreement. Data handling may include stronger commitments about not using content for model training, plus configurable retention and access controls. Collaboration improves because members share projects, knowledge, and settings. The key shift is that the workspace is now governed, not merely personal.

At the top sits an enterprise tier. Administrative control is broad — identity integration, detailed roles, auditing, and enforced policy. Data handling commitments are strongest, with clearer boundaries around storage, access, and regional or regulatory requirements. Collaboration spans many teams under central oversight. Cost and complexity rise, but the deciding factor in a scenario should rarely be cost; it should be what control or protection the situation requires.

Read the table below as principles, not a catalog. Prices are absent because they are unstable and conceptually uninteresting.

Dimension Individual tier Team or business tier Enterprise tier
Administrative control Personal settings; no central owner Org manages membership and some policies Central identity, roles, auditing, enforced policy
Data handling Standard provider terms; individual responsibility Org agreement; stronger content commitments; configurable retention Strongest commitments; regulatory and regional controls; detailed oversight
Collaboration Ad hoc sharing of outputs Shared projects and knowledge among members Cross-team work under central governance
Typical fit Personal exploration and solo work A department standardizing its practice A large organization with compliance duties
Exam cue "one person," "personal workspace" "a team," "shared project," "org policy" "auditing," "role assignment," "regulated data"

A question mentioning a shared project and organizational policy points to a team tier even if it names none. One about auditing and enforced roles points higher. One about a single person trying a tool points lower. The deciding cue is the need for governance, not a product label.

Upgrading a plan does not remove personal responsibility. A stronger data agreement does not make it wise to paste regulated records into a chat without a purpose. Administrative controls give you tools; they do not make decisions. In the exam, "upgrade the plan and proceed" is often a distractor when the real problem is a policy or verification step.

Exam note: When a scenario mentions roles, permissions, or who may see content, treat it as an account-and-plan question even if it looks like a feature question. Governance cues outrank convenience cues.

Casual chat versus checked work product

Not every use carries the same obligation. Asking an assistant to suggest a newsletter name is a casual chat. Asking it to draft a figure for a customer report is a work product. On screen they look identical — same box, same reply — but they are not the same thing.

A casual chat is exploratory. It helps you think, brainstorm, or understand a concept. Errors are cheap because nothing leaves the conversation; loose prompts and quick judgments are fine.

A work product is anything relied on, shared, or acted upon: emails to stakeholders, summaries used in decisions, code that will run, figures that will be quoted, documents carrying a name or brand. It carries three obligations — accurate enough for its purpose, traceable enough to see where claims came from, and approved by the right person before release.

The distinction matters because fluency blurs it. A well-written answer feels finished; the cleaner the prose, the less we question it. That is a dangerous instinct — polish is a property of style, not evidence of truth. Ask "where will this go?" before you accept an answer. If it is into someone else's inbox, verification enters the picture.

A practical habit is to tag outputs by confidence as you work: draft, needs source, ready for review, approved. This is not bureaucracy; it prevents accidentally promoting a casual answer into a shared one. The exam rule is simple: if a scenario mentions an external audience, a decision, or a published artifact, verification belongs in the correct answer.

A first-session orientation checklist

Knowing the theory is one thing; walking into a new workspace confidently is another. A short routine removes guesswork and prevents early mistakes — the wrong model, the wrong attachment, or sharing something you should not have. Copy this into a note the first time you open an unfamiliar assistant and adjust it to your organization's rules.

FIRST-SESSION ORIENTATION CHECKLIST

1. Confirm the account context
   [ ] Personal or organization-managed account?
   [ ] Which plan features are available to me?
   [ ] Who is the administrator for policy questions?

2. Check the data boundaries
   [ ] What data classification does my work involve?
   [ ] Is submitted content covered by an org agreement?
   [ ] What may not be entered (personal, regulated, secret)?

3. Map the interface
   [ ] Find the conversation area and model selector.
   [ ] Find navigation, projects, and artifact spaces.
   [ ] Find attachment, voice, and scheduling controls.

4. Set up a clean start
   [ ] Name the thread or project clearly.
   [ ] State task, audience, and constraints in the first prompt.
   [ ] Attach only the sources needed and permitted.

5. Plan the verification step
   [ ] Decide how facts and figures will be checked.
   [ ] Know who approves output before it is shared.
   [ ] Keep a place to record decisions and open questions.

The checklist front-loads governance and boundaries before features. That ordering is intentional: it is easier to set a safe context at the start than to repair an unsafe one after content has been entered. The same priority appears in exam questions — establish what is allowed before deciding what is efficient.

Managing exam time and flagging questions

The exam itself is a timed event, and time management is a learnable skill. Divide the available time by the number of questions for an average. Treat it as a guide, not a rule: recognition items should take less, multi-step scenarios more. What matters is noticing when you drift. If you spend twice the average on one question, either you missed the constraint or you are arguing with the options.

Use two passes. On the first, answer everything you can decide confidently and flag what is genuinely uncertain. Do not stare. A flag is a promise to return, and most interfaces provide a marker for it. Once done, you have banked the certain points and reduced the hard set.

On the second pass, revisit flagged questions with fresh eyes. A later question often contains a clue that helps an earlier one. If two options still seem equal, return to the stem and find the constraint you under-weighted. If you remain stuck, choose the option reflecting safe, careful practice and move on. Leaving a blank is almost never better than a reasoned choice.

Keep a steady pace rather than sprinting early and guessing late. Read the last line of a long question first if you get lost — the actual ask is often there. Watch for "NOT," "EXCEPT," and "least," which flip the logic. Change an answer only when you can name the new evidence that justifies it.

Exam note: Flagging is a scheduling tool, not a sign of weakness. The candidate who flags three hard questions and returns with time left usually outperforms the one who spends ten minutes forcing the first.

Worked scenario

Apply the chapter's routines to a realistic question. Read the scenario before the options and practice finding the constraint.

Scenario: An analyst at a consultancy is helping a client team prepare a briefing for senior leadership tomorrow morning. The analyst holds a document of internal client figures. The briefing must include a projected figure the client has not yet published. The analyst has thirty minutes, a team plan with shared projects, and a manager who must approve anything sent outside the client team. The assistant produces a polished draft.

Question: What should the analyst do before the briefing is shared?

  • A. Send it immediately, because the thirty-minute deadline leaves no time for checks.
  • B. Move the work into a project space so the shared context is preserved for later.
  • C. Verify the projected figure against an approved source and obtain the manager's approval, then share.
  • D. Ask the assistant to use a more capable model to confirm the projected figure.

Reasoning: The goal is a briefing for senior leadership. Now the constraints. The figures are internal client data, raising a handling concern. The projection is unpublished, raising a verification concern. A manager must approve anything sent outside the team, raising an authorization concern. The deadline is real but is a pressure, not a permission.

Option A sacrifices accuracy and authorization for speed — the attractive-but-unsafe pattern, solving the visible goal while ignoring stated limits. A deadline does not cancel approval. Option B is good practice in general, preserving context in a project, but addresses none of the three constraints: true but irrelevant. Option D misunderstands model controls. A more capable setting may reason better but cannot confirm an unpublished figure against reality; it has no privileged access to that number's truth.

Option C alone addresses all three: it verifies the claim, respects approval, and only then shares. It also honors the deadline as far as possible because the checks are targeted, not exhaustive. This is what a careful professional would do, and what the exam wants.

The question was solvable not through a feature detail but through listing constraints and testing options against them. That discipline is the most transferable skill in this exam.

Try it: Change one fact: the figures are public and already published. With verification and confidentiality removed, the deadline becomes decisive and a fast path is defensible. The story barely changed, but the answer did — because the constraint did.

Common mistakes

  • Treating the exam as a recall contest and memorizing labels instead of practicing judgment.
  • Reading answer options before identifying the constraint, anchoring on the first plausible choice.
  • Assuming a more capable model setting guarantees accuracy; it does not replace verification.
  • Confusing a prompt with a control. The prompt states the task; controls shape execution.
  • Forgetting that voice and dictation introduce transcription errors, especially in numbers and names.
  • Sharing an output because it reads well, without checking whether it is accurate or approved.
  • Assuming an upgraded plan removes the need for careful data handling or a review step.
  • Spending too long on one hard question and leaving easy points uncollected at the end.

Chapter summary

  • A foundations exam tests judgment, safe use, and clear communication more than product trivia.
  • Scenario questions contain a deciding constraint — audience, data classification, deadline, verification need, permission, or policy — that determines the answer.
  • Identify the constraint before reading the options, then test each option against it.
  • A typical interface has a conversation area, model and feature controls, a navigation panel, project and artifact spaces, attachments, entry points, and scheduling controls.
  • Plan tiers differ mainly in administrative control, data handling, and collaboration; governance cues point to the appropriate tier.
  • A casual chat is exploratory; a work product must be accurate, traceable, and approved before being shared.
  • Begin a new workspace with a short checklist settling account context, data boundaries, interface map, clean setup, and verification.
  • In the exam, use two passes with flags, keep a steady pace, and change an answer only when new evidence justifies it.

Checkpoint questions

  1. A polished draft will be emailed to a customer. It contains a statistic the analyst cannot source. What is the best first action?
  • A. Send it, because writing quality suggests the assistant checked the figure.
  • B. Verify the statistic against an approved source before sending.
  • C. Switch to a faster model and regenerate the draft.
  • D. Move the conversation into a project space.
  1. Which cues most strongly suggest an enterprise plan tier?
  • A. One person exploring the tool on a personal device.
  • B. A short chat with no files attached.
  • C. Central identity management, role assignment, and auditing requirements.
  • D. A request for a creative newsletter name.
  1. What is the main purpose of an artifact space?
  • A. To store the list of previous conversations.
  • B. To render an output separately so it can be iterated on.
  • C. To select a model tier.
  • D. To schedule a recurring task.
  1. A user dictates a prompt containing an account number and sends it without checking. What is the primary risk?
  • A. The assistant will refuse to answer.
  • B. Voice input is always slower than typing.
  • C. Transcription may have altered the number, producing a wrong or unsafe request.
  • D. The conversation will automatically become a project.
  1. In a timed exam, what is the best use of a flag?
  • A. To mark a question for return after confident answers are banked.
  • B. To end the exam early.
  • C. To indicate the answer was verified by an external source.
  • D. To skip the question without answering it under any circumstances.
  1. Which statement best captures the difference between a prompt and a response control?
  • A. They are identical; the terms are interchangeable.
  • B. A prompt states the task; controls shape how the assistant works.
  • C. Controls write the prompt for you.
  • D. A prompt can only be used in team accounts.
  1. A scenario mentions a confidential document and a tight deadline. Which constraint should normally decide the answer?
  • A. The deadline, because speed is always the priority.
  • B. The document's confidentiality, because data handling limits what may be done.
  • C. The document's length, because longer files take more time.
  • D. The model's name, because capability overrides policy.

Answers and rationale

  1. Correct: B. A customer-facing statistic that cannot be sourced is a verification failure; a work product must be checked before sharing. Option A wrongly treats fluency as evidence — polish is not verification. Option C changes the model, which cannot confirm a fact it has no way to check. Option D preserves context but does nothing about the figure; useful yet irrelevant to the question.

  2. Correct: C. Central identity management, role assignment, and auditing mark a governed, large-scale deployment, which is what an enterprise tier provides. Option A describes a personal context. Option B gives no governance cue. Option D is a casual creative task with no administrative or data signal.

  3. Correct: B. An artifact space renders an output apart from the chat so it can be opened, inspected, and refined. Option A describes the navigation panel or chat history. Option C describes the model selector. Option D describes scheduling controls.

  4. Correct: C. Voice and dictation can mis-transcribe numbers and proper names; an unchecked account number can produce a wrong request or send data to the wrong place. Option A is not guaranteed — the assistant usually proceeds. Option B is false; voice is often chosen for speed. Option D confuses dictation with deliberately creating a project.

  5. Correct: A. Flagging is a scheduling device: mark uncertainty, collect certain points first, return with remaining time. Option B misuses a flag as an exit signal. Option C gives it a meaning it lacks. Option D is wrong because a flagged question should still get an answer, ideally on the second pass.

  6. Correct: B. The prompt carries the task, context, and constraints; controls such as model tier or response settings shape execution. Option A collapses a real distinction. Option C overstates controls — they do not write your request. Option D is false; prompts work in all account types.

  7. Correct: B. When confidentiality and speed conflict, data handling governs: some actions are simply impermissible with confidential material, however urgent. Option A treats a deadline as permission, which is unsafe. Option C introduces a factor the scenario did not make decisive. Option D wrongly assumes capability can override policy.

End of chapter 1.

Chapter 1 oriented you to the workspace. This chapter turns that orientation into working practice. An assistant is not one single thing: it is a conversation, a place to keep recurring context, a panel where outputs take shape, and a set of controls that change how answers are produced. A candidate who understands only the chat box will handle simple requests but will struggle with scenario questions that hinge on where work should happen or which control should be used.

The organizing idea is simple. Different kinds of work belong on different surfaces, and different controls adjust different parts of the result. A short question belongs in a chat. A recurring responsibility with a stable body of background material belongs in a project space. A deliverable that you expect to revise several times belongs in an artifact. Once you can make that match, the rest of the chapter's features become tools you reach for deliberately rather than switches you flip at random.

Learning objectives

After studying this chapter, you should be able to:

  • Explain the conversation as a working surface, including turns, attachments, and why a clean thread improves results.
  • Distinguish a one-off chat, a persistent project space, and an iterable artifact, and choose among them for a given task.
  • Describe what response controls change and how they differ from the prompt itself.
  • Recognize the accuracy limits of voice and dictation entry and the role of scheduled or recurring tasks.
  • Explain research-oriented modes, memory features, sandboxed code execution, and packaged skills at a principle level.
  • Identify privacy and verification implications of each feature so you can answer judgment-based exam questions.

Key concepts

The conversation as a working surface

The conversation is the most familiar surface, but it is worth examining precisely. A turn is one exchange: a message you send and the assistant's reply. Each turn is not isolated. The assistant reads the current message together with earlier turns in the thread, so a conversation accumulates context as it grows. This is why a thread behaves like a shared workspace rather than a stack of independent questions.

That accumulation is useful and risky in equal measure. It is useful because you can refer back to earlier decisions without restating them: "apply the same tone as the first draft" works when the first draft is in the thread. It is risky because irrelevant early material stays present and can pull later answers off course. A thread that began with a discussion of pricing and later turns into a drafting task may carry pricing assumptions into the draft unless you explicitly narrow the scope.

Attachments are files or other material you add to a turn. They give the assistant source content to work from: a spreadsheet to summarize, a policy document to interpret, a set of notes to reorganize. An attachment changes what the assistant can see, but it does not automatically change what the assistant should do. The instruction in your message still has to say what to produce from that material. A common beginner error is to upload a document and write only "thoughts?" — the attachment supplies content while the prompt supplies no task.

A clean thread is one whose accumulated context supports the current request rather than competing with it. Three habits keep a thread clean:

  • Start a new conversation when the subject genuinely changes. Carrying unrelated history forward is rarely helpful.
  • Restate the essential constraints near the point where they matter, even if they appeared earlier.
  • Remove or ignore side threads that no longer apply, and say so explicitly when continuing a long conversation.

Exam note: Scenario questions often contrast "continue the existing thread" with "start a fresh conversation." The deciding factor is whether the earlier context helps or contaminates the new task. There is no universal rule that continuing is better.

Consider an analyst who spends twenty turns refining a budget memo, then asks the assistant to draft a customer-facing announcement. The budget figures and internal cautions in the thread are not appropriate for the announcement. A fresh conversation, or an explicit instruction to disregard prior material, is the safer choice.

Project spaces: persistent context with uploaded knowledge

A chat is built for a conversation. A project space is built for an ongoing responsibility. It persists beyond a single thread and can hold several things a chat cannot hold well: a stable set of background documents, standing instructions that apply to every conversation inside it, and a history of related work.

Think of a project as a container with three layers. The first layer is purpose — a plain statement of what this space is for. The second is knowledge — uploaded files that the assistant can draw on when working inside the project. The third is instructions — standing guidance about role, scope, format, and boundaries that should apply without being repeated in every message.

A support team maintaining a product FAQ might keep the current help articles, the tone guide, and the escalation policy in one project. New conversations inside that project inherit the same background. A coordinator does not need to re-upload the policy each time or remind the assistant of the house style. That is the practical value of persistence: consistency across many sessions with less repeated setup.

Persistence is not the same as perfection. Uploaded knowledge can become stale, two documents can disagree, and standing instructions can drift away from current policy. A project needs an owner who decides what belongs inside it and when material should be updated. If the FAQ changes but the uploaded copy does not, the project will confidently reproduce outdated guidance. Chapter 14 treats project maintenance in depth; for now, the exam-relevant point is that a project is a governed space, not a dumping ground.

A short set-up sketch makes the structure concrete:

PROJECT: Quarterly customer briefing
PURPOSE: Produce a consistent briefing note from approved sources.

KNOWLEDGE TO UPLOAD
- Current product overview (approved version, with date)
- House style and terminology guide
- Prior briefing notes marked "approved"

STANDING INSTRUCTIONS
- Role: assistant to a briefing coordinator
- Use only uploaded knowledge for factual claims
- Format: short executive summary, then three sections, then risks
- Flag anything the sources do not support; do not fill gaps
- Do not include pricing figures unless they appear in an uploaded source

TEST BEFORE USE
- Ask one real request and check: scope, format, sourcing, omissions

Try it: After sketching a project, write one representative request and one deliberately awkward request. If the standing instructions hold up under both, the set-up is probably sound. If they collapse under the awkward one, the instructions need sharper boundaries.

Artifacts: separately rendered, iterable outputs

An artifact is an output that is rendered as its own object rather than living only inside a message. It can be opened, viewed, and revised as a distinct thing. Common forms include a document, a block of code, a visual draft, and a simple interactive view that a user can click through.

The distinction matters because a chat reply and an artifact support different working styles. A reply is excellent for an answer you will read once. An artifact is excellent for a deliverable you will iterate. When the assistant produces a document as an artifact, you can ask for a change to a specific section, compare versions, and keep the surrounding conversation as the record of decisions. The object evolves while the thread explains why.

Artifact types vary in what they are good for:

  • Documents suit structured prose that will be edited, reviewed, and shared.
  • Code suits a script or snippet that will be run, inspected, or pasted into a repository.
  • Visual drafts suit layouts, diagrams, and mockups where shape and arrangement carry meaning.
  • Simple interactive views suit small tools or demonstrations where a user needs to try inputs and see results.

An artifact is not automatically correct. Rendering something neatly can create a false sense of finish. A polished document artifact that contains an unsupported claim is still an unsupported claim. Treat an artifact as a convenient workspace for refinement, not as evidence of quality. The verification habits in Chapters 6 and 7 apply to artifacts every bit as much as to chat replies.

Common mistake: Asking for "a better version" of an artifact without naming what should change. Iteration works when feedback is specific: shorten the summary, replace the generic closing, align the table headings with the source. Vague feedback produces vague revision.

Choosing the right surface

Many exam items are really matching problems. The candidate must notice the shape of the work and select the surface that fits. The table below maps common task types to a recommended surface and the reason behind the choice.

Task type Best surface Why this surface fits
One quick question about a term Chat The need is a single answer; setup would cost more than the task.
A long discussion that changes direction midway A new chat for the new direction Fresh context prevents earlier assumptions from leaking into the new topic.
Weekly report built from the same approved sources Project space Persistent knowledge and standing instructions keep output consistent across sessions.
A proposal you expect to revise five times Artifact (document) The output is a distinct object that supports targeted iteration and version comparison.
A small calculation or file transformation Chat plus sandboxed code execution The tool does the mechanical work; the chat explains the result.
A mockup of a simple screen flow Artifact (visual or interactive draft) Shape and interaction carry information that prose cannot convey.
Research that must show where claims came from Chat with a research-oriented mode Source gathering and citation are the point of the task.
A snippet that will be pasted into a codebase Artifact (code) Code is easier to inspect, copy, and revise when rendered on its own.

Two patterns are worth memorizing. First, recurrence points toward a project: if the same background and the same rules will be needed again, persistence saves effort and improves consistency. Second, iteration points toward an artifact: if the output will be shaped repeatedly, give it a surface of its own. A chat remains the default for everything else.

Response controls: shaping the answer without rewriting the prompt

Response controls are settings that influence how the assistant presents an answer: length, verbosity, tone, and format. They differ from the prompt itself in an important way. The prompt says what to produce; controls say how to present it. A prompt might ask for a comparison of two options. A control might make that comparison terse and formal rather than long and conversational.

This distinction is tested because controls are often treated as a substitute for clear instructions. They are not. If a prompt is vague about the decision to be made, no length setting will make the answer useful. Controls tune delivery; they cannot repair missing substance. Conversely, a well-specified prompt can still arrive in an unhelpful shape if the delivery does not fit the reader. Used together, they work well.

Control What it changes What it does not change
Length or verbosity Amount of detail and expansion in the reply Whether the underlying facts are correct or complete
Tone Register and style, such as formal, neutral, or conversational Authority; a confident tone is not accuracy
Format Structure such as bullets, table, or short sections Whether the structure matches the reader's actual next step
Reasoning depth How much step-by-step explanation is surfaced Access to facts the assistant does not have

A useful way to think about controls is as a second filter. First write a prompt that states the task, constraints, and required content. Then set controls to match the audience. A briefing for a busy reviewer might be short and structured. The same findings prepared for a training session might be longer and more explanatory. The underlying analysis is unchanged; the packaging differs.

Exam note: If a scenario asks how to make an answer shorter without changing its meaning, the answer is usually a response control or a revision instruction. If the scenario asks how to make an answer correct, the answer is verification against sources, not a control adjustment.

Voice, dictation, and scheduled tasks

Not all input arrives as typed text. Voice entry and dictation let a user speak a request or transcribe spoken words into text. These entry points improve speed, especially on mobile devices or while a user is away from a keyboard. They also introduce accuracy limits worth naming on an exam.

Speech recognition can mishear domain terms, numbers, product names, and homophones. Punctuation and structure often disappear, which can turn a carefully phased request into an ambiguous run of words. Background noise, accents, and fast speech raise the error rate. The practical response is to treat dictated input as a draft: check names, figures, and technical terms before sending, and add structure if the transcription flattened it. A dictated prompt containing the wrong number is still the wrong number when the assistant answers it faithfully.

Scheduled or recurring tasks run a request at a set time or on a repeating schedule. At a principle level, they automate routine information work: a morning digest, a weekly summary of changes, a periodic check that a source has been updated. The value is consistency and the removal of a repeated manual step.

Scheduling also raises the stakes of setup. A one-off prompt can be corrected after you read the answer. A recurring task repeats whatever instruction it was given, including any flaw. Before scheduling, confirm three things: the source is stable and authorized, the instruction is unambiguous, and someone will review the output. A recurring task that pulls from a stale document will keep producing stale results until someone intervenes. An output that no one reads is automation in name only.

SCHEDULED TASK SKETCH
Name: Monday source freshness check
Trigger: weekly, Monday morning
Request: list items in the uploaded source index whose "verified" date
         is older than 30 days; return a table with item, date, owner role
Review: coordinator reads output before any update is made
Stop condition: pause if the source index cannot be read

Research-oriented modes

A research-oriented mode is a mode in which the assistant is directed to gather material from sources and present claims with references rather than answer purely from its own generated content. The defining features are source gathering and citation, not speed or cleverness.

Used well, such a mode changes the kind of answer you get. Instead of a fluent summary with no traceable basis, you receive claims attached to sources you can inspect. That makes verification possible. It also makes verification necessary: citations must be checked, because a source marker is not proof that the source says what the answer claims. Chapter 6 covers source triangulation in detail.

Three exam-relevant limits are worth keeping in view. First, a cited answer can still misrepresent a source through a wrong attribution or an overbroad summary. Second, a source can be current in one area and outdated in another. Third, a research mode does not decide what counts as evidence for the decision at hand — that judgment remains with the person framing the request. When the stakes are high, the mode is a starting point for review, not a replacement for it.

Memory features and privacy implications

Memory features let an assistant retain certain facts or preferences across conversations. At their simplest, they record preferences such as a preferred format or level of detail. At their broadest, they may store information about a user's role, projects, or recurring constraints.

The convenience is obvious. A user does not need to restate a preference in every thread. The privacy implications are equally real. Memory turns a preference into stored data, and stored data raises questions: what was saved, who can see it, how long it is kept, and how it can be removed. In an organizational setting, a preference recorded in one context may be inappropriate in another. A detail shared in a casual drafting session should not quietly follow a user into a confidential workflow.

A few principles keep memory use defensible:

  • Save preferences that genuinely generalize, not one-off details tied to a single task.
  • Treat anything sensitive as unsuitable for memory, even if it would be acceptable inside a single controlled conversation.
  • Assume that retained material may be reviewed under organizational policy, and act accordingly.
  • Know how to inspect and delete stored items; convenience without control is a poor trade.

Privacy note: A frequent exam distractor suggests that because a conversation feels private, its contents are automatically protected. Feeling private is not a control. Policy, permissions, and retention rules decide how data is handled.

Code execution as a sandboxed tool

Some assistants can run code in an isolated environment, often called a sandbox. This gives the assistant a way to do exact mechanical work: calculate a figure, sort a list, transform a file, or test a small piece of logic. The sandbox is separated from your own machine and from organizational systems, which limits the damage a mistake can cause.

The key insight is the division of labor. Language models are not reliable arithmetic engines; a sandbox can add, aggregate, and transform with precision. But the sandbox only does what the generated code tells it to do. The instruction still has to describe the correct operation, and the result still has to be checked against the input. A beautifully executed wrong formula is still wrong.

Good candidates for code execution include recalculating totals, converting formats, deduplicating records, and checking a rule against a list. Poor candidates include tasks where the decision depends on judgment, context, or information the sandbox cannot access. Code execution is a tool for computation and transformation, not a source of truth about the world.

Two limits matter for the exam. First, execution in a sandbox does not imply access to external systems unless a connector or tool explicitly grants it. Second, a successful run is not validation: the code may run cleanly while implementing the wrong rule. Ask for the assumptions behind the calculation and spot-check a small input before trusting a large output.

Skills: packaged instructions the assistant can draw on

A skill is a reusable package of instructions that gives the assistant a defined way to handle a class of tasks. Rather than pasting the same detailed guidance into every prompt, a user can make that guidance available as a packaged capability the assistant draws on when it fits.

The analogy to a checklist is useful. An organization might have a standard way to review a contract, format a release note, or structure a weekly report. Encoding that approach as a skill makes the method repeatable. When a request matches, the assistant applies the packaged steps and conventions without the user restating them each time.

Skills differ from a project's standing instructions in scope and portability. Project instructions belong to one space and apply to everything inside it. A skill is typically invoked for a particular kind of task and can be used wherever that task arises. They also differ from a response control: a skill changes the procedure followed, while a control changes how the answer is presented.

Three cautions keep skills in their proper place. A skill does not guarantee correctness; the underlying instructions may be flawed or outdated. A skill should be checked against the current request, because a method that fits one situation may mislead in another. And a skill does not transfer authority: if a task requires human approval, packaging it as a skill does not remove that requirement.

Worked scenario

An operations coordinator needs a dependable way to produce a monthly service summary. Each month, the same three approved source files are reviewed, a short summary is written for a leadership audience, and a table of open issues is maintained. The first attempt was a single long chat that mixed source questions, drafting, and formatting requests. By the third month, the thread had grown unwieldy, some figures reflected an older source version, and the summary's tone varied from month to month.

The coordinator considers four options and reasons through each.

Option A — keep extending the original chat. This is tempting because the history contains useful decisions. But the thread also contains outdated figures and abandoned drafts. Continuing would carry stale context into a task that depends on current sources. Rejected.

Option B — create a project space with the three approved files, standing instructions for audience and format, and a separate conversation for each month. This matches recurrence: the same background and rules are needed every cycle. Uploaded knowledge can be version-labelled, and standing instructions keep the tone consistent. Each monthly conversation stays clean because it begins fresh inside a stable container. Selected.

Option C — rely only on memory features. Memory could retain a format preference, but it would not hold the approved source files or enforce a sourcing rule. It also risks storing details that should not persist. Rejected as insufficient.

Option D — use a scheduled task with no review step. A recurring task could assemble a draft automatically, but the source files change and the summary goes to leadership. Without a named review step, an outdated source would reach the audience unchecked. Rejected.

The resolution combines surfaces rather than relying on one. A project space holds the approved files and standing instructions. Each month begins a new conversation inside that project. The summary itself is produced as a document artifact so it can be revised section by section after review. A scheduled task sends a reminder to check source dates; it does not publish anything. Response controls are set to keep the leadership summary short and neutral, while the project instruction requires every figure to be traceable to an uploaded file.

The result is a workflow whose structure encodes the lessons of the chapter: persistent context where the work recurs, a clean thread for each cycle, an artifact where iteration happens, controls for presentation, and a human review point before anything is sent.

Exam note: When a scenario presents several features, the strongest answer often combines them in a way that assigns each feature a clear job. Be wary of options that solve one problem while creating a new risk, such as automation without review.

Common mistakes

  • Treating a project space as a larger chat and uploading documents without labelling versions or assigning an owner.
  • Expecting response controls to fix factual errors; controls shape presentation, not accuracy.
  • Continuing a long thread after the subject changes, allowing irrelevant context to influence the new task.
  • Uploading an attachment without stating what should be done with it.
  • Assuming that a neatly rendered artifact has been verified.
  • Trusting dictated input without checking names, numbers, and technical terms.
  • Scheduling a recurring task before confirming that its source is stable and that someone reviews the output.
  • Storing sensitive details in memory because they were convenient in one conversation.
  • Believing that a citation in a research-mode answer proves the claim; the source still needs checking.
  • Assuming that code running successfully in a sandbox means the correct operation was performed.

Chapter summary

  • The conversation is a cumulative working surface; turns build context, attachments supply material, and clean threads protect against contamination.
  • Project spaces persist knowledge and standing instructions, making them the right choice for recurring work with stable background.
  • Artifacts render outputs as distinct objects suited to iteration: documents, code, visual drafts, and simple interactive views.
  • Match surface to task: chat for one-offs, project for recurrence, artifact for iteration, and a fresh thread when the subject changes.
  • Response controls adjust length, verbosity, tone, and format; they do not fix weak prompts or verify facts.
  • Voice and dictation are fast but error-prone with names and numbers; scheduled tasks repeat instructions faithfully, flaws included.
  • Research modes gather and cite sources but still require checking; memory stores preferences with privacy consequences.
  • Sandboxed code execution handles calculation and transformation precisely, provided the rule is right; skills package reusable instructions without transferring authority.
  • Across every feature, the exam favors the answer that names the risk and keeps a human review point where it matters.

Checkpoint questions

  1. An analyst has discussed pricing for twenty turns and now wants a short internal announcement that must not mention pricing. What is the best first step?

A. Continue the same thread and ask the assistant to ignore earlier pricing.

B. Start a fresh conversation and state the restriction explicitly.

C. Lower the verbosity control and reuse the thread.

D. Move the work into an artifact without changing the thread.

  1. Which combination best fits a weekly report built from the same approved sources and revised section by section after review?

A. A chat with a short prompt and high verbosity

B. Memory features with a scheduled task and no review

C. A project space for knowledge and instructions, plus a document artifact for the report

D. A research mode with no sources supplied

  1. What does a response control such as tone change?

A. The facts available to the assistant

B. The register and style in which the answer is presented

C. The permissions granted to a connector

D. The amount of memory retained across chats

  1. A dictated request contains a misheard figure. The assistant answers using that figure. Who is responsible for catching the error before the answer is used?

A. The sandbox, because it validates all input automatically

B. The response control, because it checks numeric accuracy

C. The user, who should review dictated input before relying on it

D. The project space, because it rejects incorrect numbers

  1. Which statement about research-oriented modes is most accurate?

A. A citation guarantees that the source supports the claim.

B. They remove the need to judge what counts as evidence.

C. They gather sources and present claims with references, which still require checking.

D. They are useful only for tasks that need no verification.

  1. A recurring task pulls from a document that is updated monthly. What is the safest setup at a principle level?

A. Schedule the task and publish its output with no review.

B. Include a freshness check, a clear instruction, and a named review of the output.

C. Store the document's contents in memory and stop checking the source.

D. Use a longer response so errors become less likely.

  1. What is the main difference between a project's standing instructions and a skill?

A. Standing instructions apply within one space; a skill packages a reusable method for a task type.

B. Skills control tone; standing instructions control length.

C. Standing instructions run code; skills cannot contain instructions.

D. There is no difference; the terms are interchangeable.

Answers and rationale

  1. B. A fresh conversation removes the pricing material that could contaminate the announcement, and stating the restriction explicitly makes the boundary unambiguous. A is weaker because asking a model to ignore material it can still see is less reliable than removing that material from context. C changes presentation only; lower verbosity does not remove outdated assumptions. D changes the output surface but leaves the contaminated thread in place.

  2. C. The project holds recurring knowledge and instructions, while the artifact supports section-by-section revision after review. A lacks persistence and treats verbosity as a quality control. B introduces exactly the risk the scenario must avoid: no review and memory used as a substitute for source files. D describes research without supplying sources, which defeats consistency.

  3. B. Tone governs register and style. A is wrong because controls do not add facts or grant knowledge. C confuses response controls with connector permissions, which are a separate access question. D confuses presentation settings with memory retention policy.

  4. C. Dictation is error-prone with figures, so the user must check input before relying on the result. A is wrong because a sandbox executes code; it does not validate the intent or the numbers typed into a prompt. B is wrong because response controls never check numeric accuracy. D is wrong because a project space stores context, not a numeric validation gate.

  5. C. Research modes gather and cite sources, and the citations still require verification against the source text. A is a classic distractor: a citation marker is not proof of support. B is wrong because judging evidence remains a human responsibility. D reverses the purpose of the mode, which exists precisely for tasks where verification matters.

  6. B. Freshness checks, unambiguous instructions, and human review address the two failure modes of recurring work: stale sources and unchecked output. A automates the risk by publishing without review. C replaces a living source with retained data that will age. D mistakes length for accuracy.

  7. A. Project instructions are scoped to a space; a skill packages a method that can be reused when the matching task appears. B assigns control-like roles to both and is incorrect. C misdescribes both features. D ignores a real distinction in scope and portability that scenario questions often test.

End of chapter 2.

A request to an assistant is not a command in the calculator sense. It is a brief: a description of what you want, for whom, under what limits, and what a good answer would look like. When that brief is thin, the assistant fills the gaps with the most plausible continuation. The result may be fluent and still be wrong for your situation. Prompting is the craft of closing the gaps that matter.

This chapter explains why context changes output quality, sets out the five parts of a useful instruction, and shows how to give background without drowning the task in it. It then covers placement of critical rules, prohibitions versus demonstrations, how to choose examples, specificity versus over-specification, a draft-critique-revision rhythm, and the separation of untrusted text from your own instructions. A before-and-after rewrite ties the ideas together.

Learning objectives

After studying this chapter, the candidate should be able to:

  • Explain why the information available at generation time changes the usefulness of an answer.
  • Identify the five parts of a useful instruction and write each for a given task.
  • Supply relevant background while excluding material that distracts or misleads.
  • Place critical constraints where they are least likely to be overlooked.
  • Distinguish a negative instruction from a positive example and know when each applies.
  • Select a small set of few-shot examples that teaches the desired behavior.
  • Recognize under-specification and over-specification and correct both.
  • Use a draft, critique, and revision sequence to improve an output deliberately.
  • Separate instructions from untrusted source text and respond safely to embedded commands.
  • Rewrite a weak prompt into a strong one and justify each change.

Key concepts

Why context changes output quality

An assistant does not look up your intent. It works with the text it can see when it generates a reply: the current message, earlier turns, project instructions, and attached or retrieved material. That is the context, and every answer is conditioned on it.

If you write "Summarize this report," the range of reasonable continuations is enormous: a one-line gist, a chapter-by-chapter digest, a critical review, a list of statistics. The assistant must guess. If you write "Summarize this report for an operations lead who needs the three decisions requiring approval this week, in a table," the range collapses and the answer becomes useful on the first try.

Two ideas matter here. The first is signal-to-noise. Relevant context steers the answer toward your goal; irrelevant context adds material the assistant must sift through and may treat as meaningful. Pasting a history of the company above a question about one policy clause does not merely waste space — it introduces names and dates that can pull the answer off target. More context is not automatically better context.

The second is that the assistant mirrors assumptions present in the context. If your background says "the new policy reduced errors," the summary will probably repeat that claim rather than test it. The assistant is not being deceptive; it is continuing a pattern you supplied. Neutral wording in the background is therefore as important as the task verb.

Context also has structure. Instructions near the start and end of a message tend to carry more weight than those buried in the middle. Material in an attachment may be treated differently from typed material, and earlier turns remain visible, so an abandoned approach from twenty messages ago can still influence the answer. Context is an ordered resource, not a bag of words.

Before sending, ask: "If a colleague received only this text, could they produce exactly what I need?" If not, the missing piece is context you still have to supply.

Exam note: Context quality is not context quantity. When a scenario contrasts a long, unfocused prompt with a shorter one that states audience, decision, and format, the shorter one is usually better designed.

The five parts of a useful instruction

A dependable instruction has five parts: a role or frame, a task, constraints, a format, and examples. A failing prompt is usually missing one of them, so treat the list as a diagnostic checklist rather than a template.

The role or frame sets the perspective: "advise a small operations team," "write for a first-time reader," "respond as a reviewer checking a claim." It focuses attention on the vocabulary and priorities that fit the situation. It is not a switch that grants expertise; it works by making certain patterns more relevant.

The task names the action and object. Use a specific verb and deliverable: "list the missing fields," "compare the options against four criteria." Verbs such as "handle" leave the assistant to invent the deliverable. A useful test is whether the task can be checked.

Constraints define boundaries: what to include and exclude, which sources are allowed, how uncertainty should be labeled, and what must never be stated. Separate hard constraints from preferences. "Do not include personal data" is hard; "keep the tone friendly" is a preference. Hard constraints belong near the top and should be restated near the end.

The format fixes the shape for the next step: a table with named columns, a numbered list, a short paragraph, structured fields. This is not decoration. A table supports scanning; bullets support action; prose supports narrative; structured data supports import.

Examples show the mapping you want. A short sample can communicate tone, granularity, and edge-case handling more reliably than several sentences of description, especially how to treat a blank field or how much caveat to attach to a claim.

Part Purpose Self-check question
Role or frame Sets perspective and priorities Who is addressed, and in what decision setting?
Task Names the action and deliverable What artifact will exist when this is done?
Constraints Defines boundaries What must be included, excluded, or never claimed?
Format Fixes the shape for the next step How will a person or tool consume this?
Examples Demonstrates the mapping What does a correct answer look like in miniature?

Try it: Take a recent prompt and mark which of the five parts are present. Add only the missing parts, then compare the two answers. The improvement is usually traceable to a specific addition.

Giving background without dumping irrelevant material

Background bridges your situation and the answer. Too little leaves the assistant guessing; too much buries the task. Aim for the smallest set of facts that changes the answer.

Use the "would change the answer" test: include a detail only if removing it could alter the reply. The audience, the decision supported, and definitions of internal terms pass. A paragraph about company founding does not, unless the task concerns company history.

With a large source, resist pasting all of it by reflex. Identify the relevant section and supply that, noting what the rest contains. If you do not know which part matters, supply a short index of headings and offer detail on request. This keeps the task visible and lets the assistant ask a focused follow-up.

Irrelevant background carries three costs: it dilutes the instruction, can anchor the answer in accidental details, and may expose information that did not need to leave its original system. A concise brief is both a quality choice and a prudent one.

Background item Include when Leave out when
Intended audience Vocabulary or depth depend on it Output is truly audience-neutral
Decision supported The assistant must prioritize facts Task is a mechanical transformation
Definitions of terms Terms are ambiguous or internal Terms are ordinary and clear
Excerpt of a source It contains the needed evidence Only the document's existence matters
Organizational history The task is about that history It is colour that changes nothing
Personal or restricted details A verified need and authorization exist Curiosity or completeness is the reason

Background selection is about appropriateness as well as relevance. Pasting a full customer record to ask a formatting question creates a data-handling problem that no prompt clarity fixes.

Placing the most important instructions where they are least likely to be overlooked

Instructions are not weighted equally by position. In long messages, material in the middle can receive less attention than material at the edges. A critical rule hidden in the third sentence of a dense paragraph is more likely to be missed than one on its own line near the start and repeated as a short closing line.

Use three habits. Put the frame and most important constraint at the top, before detailed background. Format constraints as separate lines or bullets rather than folds in prose. Close with a one-line restatement of the rule that matters most — the "final check."

Consider a rule requiring only supplied figures. Buried mid-paragraph, it invites the assistant to supplement with general knowledge. Placed first and repeated at the end, an invented figure becomes much less likely. Neither placement guarantees correctness, but the second makes the boundary harder to miss.

Placement also interacts with surfaces. Project instructions act as defaults for every chat in a project, which suits standing rules on tone and terminology. They are not a substitute for restating a task-specific rule today. If this deliverable must exclude a section, say so in this prompt; relying on a general project default is a common source of silent failure.

Exam note: When a scenario emphasizes one non-negotiable requirement — a source restriction, a privacy rule, a strict format — choose the answer that states it early and restates it late, not one that mentions it in passing.

The difference between a negative instruction and a positive example

A negative instruction tells the assistant what to avoid. A positive example shows what to produce. Both have a place, and confusing them causes stubborn output problems.

"Do not write long paragraphs" rules one thing out without saying what should replace it; the assistant may substitute terse fragments that are technically not paragraphs and still unhelpful. "Write two or three sentences per bullet, each naming one action and one owner" describes the target. Better still, show one bullet with exactly that shape.

Negative instructions remain essential for guardrails: "do not invent citations," "do not include personal data," "do not claim a feature not in the supplied material." The mistake is relying on prohibitions alone to define style or structure. Pair each important prohibition with a positive demonstration of the desired alternative.

Situation Negative-only version Better version
Tone too formal "Do not sound stiff." "Use short, direct sentences. Example: 'Approve the request by Friday.'"
Answers too long "Do not be verbose." "Use at most five bullets, one line each."
Citations invented "Do not make things up." "Cite only supplied sections; write 'not stated' otherwise."
Structure inconsistent "Do not be messy." "Use exactly these headings: Summary, Gaps, Next steps."
Jargon leaks in "Avoid jargon." "Replace each technical term with a plain phrase, as in the sample row."

Prohibitions protect the boundary; demonstrations define the target. A strong prompt usually contains both.

Few-shot examples and how to choose them

A prompt with no examples is zero-shot; with one it is one-shot; with several it is few-shot. Examples work by demonstrating the mapping from input to output, including details awkward to state in words: how terse to be, whether to include a caveat, how to treat a missing value.

Choose for quality, not quantity. A useful example resembles inputs you actually expect. The set should vary in surface details while keeping the behavior constant, so the assistant learns the pattern rather than copying a feature. Include one hard case — a blank field or a conflicting instruction in source text — where you most want the handling shown.

Examples must be correct; a wrong label becomes a pattern the assistant reproduces faithfully. They must agree with each other; conflicting formats invite inconsistent answers. Use synthetic or sanitized content, since real customer records and internal secrets survive into outputs. Keep the set small — often two to five examples suffice for a foundational task. A long stack consumes context, crowds out the request, and can cause overfitting to the sample.

Example choice Why it matters What to avoid
Representative inputs Teaches the real pattern Exotic cases that never occur
Varied surface details Prevents copying superficial features Identical wording that invites memorization
One edge case Shows handling of blanks and conflicts Only clean, easy cases
Correct and consistent Establishes a reliable mapping Wrong labels or conflicting formats
Sanitized content Protects sensitive information Real personal or confidential records

Common mistake: Assuming examples replace rules. State the constraint in words, then show it. If the two disagree, the assistant receives mixed signals and the output becomes unpredictable.

Specificity versus over-specification

Under-specified prompts leave too much to chance. Over-specified ones try to control too much and can be worse. The middle ground is to specify the outcome and leave the method open unless the method is part of the requirement.

An under-specified prompt says "Write something about our service." There is no audience, decision, deliverable, length, or definition of success, so the assistant invents all five. The remedy is to add the missing parts of the instruction framework.

Over-specification dictates every sentence or imposes overlapping rules, such as "be exhaustive" alongside "keep it under three lines." When rules conflict, the assistant tries to reconcile them and may satisfy neither. Heavy specification also suppresses useful judgment: prescribing each section's wording prevents the assistant from adapting phrasing to what the evidence supports.

The test is whether each added instruction removes a genuine ambiguity. "Write for a non-technical reader" does. "Use the word 'streamline' exactly once in the second sentence" usually does not. Keep instructions that change the answer predictably; drop those that merely express a process preference.

Method detail is legitimate when it is part of the outcome: a regulatory formula, a format fixed by a downstream system, a required house style. Specify those precisely. The aim is not to avoid detail but to spend it where it removes real uncertainty.

Exam note: If two prompts produce similar answers and one is much shorter, the shorter is often better. Check whether the extra text changed anything.

Asking for a draft, then a critique, then a revision

A single request asks the assistant to understand, plan, and execute at once. Splitting it into three passes makes each easier and gives you a chance to steer.

In the draft pass, ask for a complete first version. The aim is not a final answer but something concrete to react to. People find it easier to judge an existing draft than to specify every requirement in advance, and the draft often reveals what they actually wanted.

In the critique pass, request an evaluation against explicit criteria: coverage of requested parts, accuracy relative to supplied source, clarity for the named audience, and compliance with format.
