Try it: Before forwarding any response, write the original request at the top of your notes, list its requirements underneath, and tick each one off. If you cannot tick a requirement, the response is not ready to forward.

The mechanism of confident fabrication

An assistant generates an answer by predicting what text is likely to follow the prompt, guided by patterns learned from training material. It does not look up a fact in a database, and it has no built-in sense of certainty about each statement it produces. The result is a system very good at producing text that looks like a well-formed answer, with no automatic mechanism for guaranteeing it is true.

Fabrication arises from this design. With strong evidence in its training or supplied context, output is likely to align with reality. With weak evidence, the model still produces the most plausible continuation it can. Plausibility and truth overlap often enough to be useful, but they are not the same. A fabricated detail is not random nonsense; it is the kind of detail that would fit naturally in that position. That is what makes it dangerous.

Confidence is a feature of writing style, not a measurement of correctness. The model has no separate uncertainty signal that it reliably translates into hedging. The opposite can happen: a made-up figure is often stated more cleanly and decisively than a careful, qualified truth, because the training signal rewards complete, authoritative-sounding answers. "The figure is 47 percent" can be entirely invented, while "I could not verify the figure from the material provided" may be the more accurate statement.

That is why fluency is not evidence. A smooth, well-structured answer gives the reader a feeling of competence, and that feeling is regularly transferred from the writing to the facts. The transfer is a human reaction, not a property of the output. The reader must interrupt it with a deliberate verification step.

Fabrication is likelier for niche or recent information, very specific detail, prompts inviting the model to fill a gap, or answers with no supplied source material. It is less likely when the answer is grounded in inspectable material and the model is permitted to say it does not know.

Common mistake: Assuming that a detailed answer with precise numbers must be based on real data. Precision in phrasing is not proof of precision in the underlying information.

Categories of hallucination and how to detect them

For verification purposes, it helps to sort fabrications into practical categories. These are not a theory of the model's inner workings; they are a checklist of what commonly goes wrong and what to look for.

Hallucination type What it looks like Detection tactic
Invented source A citation to a report or document that does not exist Search the exact title; check publisher and date; treat unfindable sources as fabricated
Wrong attribution A real quote or idea credited to the wrong person, organization, or document Verify the quote against the original; confirm who actually said or published it
Plausible number A statistic that sounds reasonable but has no basis in the supplied data Recompute from source figures; check whether the number appears in the material
Incorrect date or name A specific year, deadline, or name that is wrong Cross-check against a calendar, timeline, or authoritative record
Fabricated detail An invented example or case used to illustrate a point Ask whether the instance appears in the source; demand a citation for it
False synthesis A conclusion presented as following from sources that do not support it Read the cited passages directly and test whether they justify the conclusion

The first four rows are the classic exam categories; the last two are common in practice and easy to miss. A false synthesis is especially slippery: every source may be real and accurately quoted, yet the conclusion drawn from them is not warranted.

Nearly every detection tactic goes back to something external — the source document, a search result, a calendar, a dataset. You cannot reliably verify an answer using only itself. Internal consistency is a useful first filter, but a fabrication consistent with the rest of a fabricated answer will pass it.

Warning signs in a response

Some answers carry signals that warrant a closer look. No single sign proves fabrication, but each lowers the verification threshold.

Vague sourcing is common. Phrases such as "studies show" without a named source shift the burden of proof onto the reader. A related sign is a citation that looks specific but cannot be located: an unusual title, a missing publisher, or a page number that leads nowhere.

Uneven specificity is another tell. An answer may give exact figures in one paragraph and wave vaguely in the next, or state precise percentages for familiar facts while becoming vague exactly where the question was hardest. Fabricated details cluster at the point of maximum difficulty, where grounding is weakest.

Over-completeness deserves attention too. If an answer supplies a number for every metric and a date for every event, ask whether the source really contained all that. Real documents are patchy; a suspiciously exhaustive answer may be filling gaps with inventions.

Watch for answers that never acknowledge limits: no hedging and no note about what could not be confirmed presents a uniform confidence the situation rarely justifies. Finally, note internal contradictions — if the summary says costs fell while the body says they rose, at least one part is wrong. Inconsistency does not say which part, but it says the answer has not been checked.

Exam note: Warning signs trigger verification; they do not prove an answer is wrong. The correct response is usually "investigate further," not "reject the entire answer."

Grounding a request in supplied material

The most effective way to reduce fabrication is to give the assistant material to work from and require it to stay within that material. This is grounding. Instead of a broad question that invites the model to draw on everything it has seen, provide a document, extract, table, or set of notes and instruct the model to use only that content.

A grounded request does three things: it narrows the possible answers to something inspectable; it makes unsupported additions obvious, since any claim not traceable to the supplied text stands out; and it turns verification from a research task into a comparison against the provided material.

Grounding is not foolproof: a model can still misread a passage or add a detail from memory despite the instruction. That is why it is paired with a requirement to show the evidence. But it changes the odds and makes verification far faster.

When you supply material, say what to do when it is insufficient. Without that instruction, the model may quietly fall back on general knowledge to fill the gap. A clear instruction — "if the extract does not contain the answer, say so" — closes that escape route. This is where grounding and the practice of marking unknowns reinforce each other.

Asking for quotations and citations

A grounded answer is much easier to check when the model points to where each claim came from. Ask for a short quotation beside each key statement, or a reference to the section, row, or paragraph. The quotation is not decoration; it is the evidence trail that lets a reviewer confirm the claim quickly.

Two forms are useful. A quotation reproduces exact words and is ideal when wording matters — whether a policy says "must" or "should." A citation points to a location such as a section or page, ideal when the reviewer wants to read around the claim. Many tasks benefit from both: a citation to find the passage, a quotation to confirm it.

Ask for quotations to be short, exact, and attached to the specific claim each supports rather than collected in a block at the end, so the reader moves from claim to evidence without guessing.

A subtle failure mode: a quotation can be accurate while its interpretation is not. A source may say a condition "may increase risk," and the answer report that it "causes" the problem. The quote checks out, but the conclusion overstates it. Verification requires reading claim and evidence together, not merely confirming the quoted words appear.

Asking the assistant to mark what it does not know

A useful instruction in any verification-focused prompt is to permit — better, require — the assistant to identify gaps. Something as simple as "if you cannot find this in the material, write 'not stated in the source'" gives the model a legitimate way to decline to answer. This reduces the pressure to produce a complete-looking answer at all costs.

The instruction works best when specific about what counts as unknown. Ask the model to distinguish three cases: information present in the source, absent from it, and present but ambiguous or conflicting. That is more useful than a blanket "I don't know," because it shows where the problem lies — an absent figure can be sought elsewhere; a conflicting pair needs a decision about which is authoritative.

Interpret the admission correctly. When an assistant says a fact is not in the supplied material, that is a statement about the material, not the world — the fact may be true and available elsewhere. Conversely, confident delivery is not proof the fact was in the material. The admission is itself a claim to check, like any other, but a conservative one that is safer to act on.

A model may still fail to flag its own uncertainty: marking unknowns reduces fabrication but does not eliminate it. The reviewer's checklist still applies to answered parts.

Common mistake: Treating an assistant's "I don't know" as a definitive statement that no such information exists anywhere.

Source triangulation and the limits of one answer

Triangulation means checking a claim against more than one independent source. A single generated answer is one artifact; even if fluent, cited, and internally consistent, it is one pass at the problem and can carry a single error all the way through. Independent sources break that dependency: a mistake in one place is unlikely to recur in the same way elsewhere.

Triangulation takes several forms. A reviewer can ask the assistant to answer the same question twice with different framing and compare results, check a claim against the original document, or consult a second reference for external facts. The goal is the same — see whether the claim survives a route independent of the first answer.

Independence is the key word. Asking the same assistant to "double-check" its previous answer often produces a restatement, especially if the original wording remains in view. Better is to clear the context, present the question afresh, and require the model to work from the source again — or check by a different route entirely, such as reading the source directly.

Triangulation also has limits. Multiple sources can repeat the same error, especially when they draw on the same original material; two generated answers can agree because they share training patterns. Agreement raises confidence but does not guarantee truth. What it does is expose disagreement: when two independent routes produce different figures, that conflict is a reliable signal that something needs investigation. Exam framing therefore emphasizes the limits of a single answer rather than claiming that multiple answers settle the matter.

A verification checklist you can apply in two minutes

Verification need not be elaborate. A short, repeatable routine applied every time catches most serious problems; it must be fast enough to actually get used and structured enough not to depend on mood or attention.

The checklist below suits a two-minute review of a typical business answer. Longer or higher-stakes outputs need more time, but the same sequence applies.

Step Check Action if it fails
1 Restate the request; list its requirements Stop and rewrite the prompt if the request was unclear
2 Confirm every requirement appears in the answer Ask for the missing parts before accepting
3 Scan for warning signs: vague sourcing, uneven specificity, no limits Mark suspicious claims for deeper checking
4 Verify each key claim against the supplied source Correct or remove unsupported claims
5 Check every number, date, and name against a record Recompute or confirm before sharing
6 Confirm citations resolve and quotations match Treat unfindable sources as fabricated
7 Triangulate the highest-risk claim by a second route Resolve disagreements before acting

Two habits make this work. First, verify in order of risk: with only one minute, spend it on the claim that would cause the most harm if wrong — usually a number, date, or recommendation. Second, write down the result. A quick note of what was checked creates an audit trail and prevents anyone assuming a check happened when it did not.

The checklist is skeptical without being cynical. Most answers are broadly correct; the goal is not to find fault for its own sake but to catch the few confident errors that would otherwise reach a decision or a message to someone else.

Below is a reusable prompt pattern combining grounding, explicit evidence, and permission to report gaps. It is written generically so it can be adapted to any subject area.

Use only the material supplied below to answer the request.
Do not add facts from outside this material.

For each key claim:
- give a short exact quotation from the material, and
- name the section, row, or paragraph where it appears.

If the material does not contain an answer, write "not stated in the source".
If the material contains conflicting statements, list both and say they conflict.
Do not guess a number, date, or name.

Request: [paste the exact request here]
Material: [paste the source text here]

This prompt fixes the evidence boundary, requires a traceable citation for each claim, gives legitimate ways to report absence or conflict, and blocks the most common fabrication types. It does not remove the need for the checklist — the quotations still have to be verified — but it makes verification much faster.

Try it: Apply the two-minute checklist to an answer you generated earlier this week. Note how many steps change your assessment. If the number is more than zero, the checklist is doing its job.

Worked scenario

An analyst asks an assistant to prepare a short briefing from a supplied internal document. The exact request is: "Using the attached policy extract only, summarize the approval rules in three bullet points, state the effective date, and list the name of the responsible office." The analyst pastes the extract and adds the grounding instruction from the prompt pattern above.

The assistant returns a confident answer: three bullets describing the rules, a statement that the policy "took effect on 1 March," and a specific office named as responsible. One bullet says: "All requests over 5,000 units require two approvals, as confirmed in Section 4 of the policy." The answer is well written and free of hedging.

A reviewer applies the checklist.

Step 1, restate the request: three deliverables — the bullets, the effective date, and the responsible office. Step 2, presence: all three are present, so the answer is not incomplete. Step 3, warning signs: the answer is uniformly confident, and "as confirmed in Section 4" is very specific, making it a candidate for checking. Step 4, source check: the bullets match the extract closely. Step 5, number, date, and name: the extract has a "5,000 units" threshold but says nothing about "two approvals" — it requires one additional sign-off. It gives no effective date, only a "draft" label. And the named office does not appear; the extract says only "the issuing department."

Step 6, check citations: the extract has only three sections, and Section 4 does not exist. Step 7, triangulate: the reviewer opens the original document and confirms that the figure and the office name came from the model's general knowledge, not the supplied text.

The answer appears complete but contains several defects: a fabricated citation, a plausible number attached to the wrong rule, an invented effective date, and a wrong office name. The fluent writing created an impression of reliability the source did not support.

The fix is to re-prompt and verify again. The reviewer asks the assistant to remove any claim unsupported by the extract, to write "not stated in the source" for the date and office, and to correct the approval rule. The revised answer is shorter and less polished, but every claim can be traced to the extract — less impressive to read, far safer to act on.

The first answer was not useless — its summary of the approval rules was accurate. But accuracy in the easy parts does not certify the hard parts, and the parts most likely to be fabricated — numbers, dates, names, and citations — are exactly the ones that must be checked against a record.

Exam note: In a scenario question, look for the option that verifies specific details against the supplied source and accepts "not stated" where evidence is absent. Options that trust the answer because it is detailed or well structured are usually wrong.

Common mistakes

  • Judging completeness by length or confidence instead of by the requirements in the request.
  • Assuming that fluent, precise phrasing means the underlying facts are reliable.
  • Accepting a citation or quotation without confirming it resolves to a real, matching source.
  • Asking the same assistant to "check its own answer" while that answer stays in view.
  • Treating agreement between two generated answers as proof of truth rather than as risk reduction.
  • Forgetting to verify numbers, dates, and names, which are the most common fabrication targets.
  • Ignoring the instruction to mark unknowns, then acting on a guess the model should have flagged.

Chapter summary

  • Accuracy and completeness are independent: an answer can be correct yet incomplete, or thorough yet wrong.
  • Completeness is measured against the exact request, including counts, formats, scope, and time frames.
  • A mechanical requirement-by-requirement check catches omissions a general read misses.
  • Fabrication comes from producing plausible text without a built-in guarantee of truth; confidence in tone is not evidence.
  • Key hallucination categories include invented sources, wrong attribution, plausible numbers, and incorrect dates or names, plus false synthesis and fabricated detail.
  • Warning signs include vague sourcing, uneven specificity, suspicious exhaustiveness, absence of limits, and internal contradiction.
  • Grounding in supplied material, asking for short exact quotations, and permitting "not stated in the source" all reduce fabrication and speed verification.
  • Triangulate important claims by an independent route; agreement helps, but multiple sources can still share an error.
  • A two-minute checklist — requirements, presence, warning signs, source check, detail check, citation check, triangulation — makes verification fast and repeatable.

Checkpoint questions

  1. An answer correctly explains one concept but does not provide the comparison table the request asked for. How should this be classified?
  • A. Accurate and complete
  • B. Accurate but incomplete
  • C. Inaccurate but complete
  • D. Inaccurate and incomplete
  1. Why is a fluent, confident answer not reliable evidence that its claims are true?
  • A. Because confident answers are always longer than necessary
  • B. Because the assistant deliberately invents details in every response
  • C. Because confidence is a feature of style, not a measurement of correctness
  • D. Because citations make fluency irrelevant
  1. A response cites a report title that cannot be found in any search. Which hallucination category does this best illustrate?
  • A. Invented source
  • B. Wrong attribution
  • C. Incorrect date
  • D. False synthesis
  1. Which prompt instruction most directly reduces the chance that an assistant will fill a gap with invented information?
  • A. "Answer as thoroughly as possible."
  • B. "If the material does not contain the answer, write 'not stated in the source'."
  • C. "Use a professional tone and precise numbers."
  • D. "Give a complete answer covering every possibility."
  1. A reviewer asks the same assistant to verify its previous answer while that answer remains visible in the conversation. What is the main weakness of this approach?
  • A. It takes too long to be practical
  • B. It is likely to reproduce the original answer rather than check it independently
  • C. It always produces a longer answer
  • D. It cannot be done with grounded material
  1. Which of the following is the best detection tactic for a plausible number in a generated answer?
  • A. Check whether the sentence containing the number is well written
  • B. Count how many times the number appears in the answer
  • C. Recompute or confirm the figure against the source data
  • D. Accept it if the answer includes a citation
  1. An answer says a policy "took effect on 1 March," but the supplied extract contains no effective date. What should the reviewer conclude?
  • A. The date is probably correct because the answer is detailed
  • B. The date is unsupported and should be removed or marked as not stated
  • C. The date should be kept because the assistant wrote it confidently
  • D. The extract must be wrong and should be discarded
  1. In the two-minute verification checklist, why is the highest-risk claim checked first?
  • A. Because it is always the shortest claim
  • B. Because checking it first removes the need to check anything else
  • C. Because limited time should be spent where an error would cause the most harm
  • D. Because high-risk claims are always fabricated

Answers and rationale

  1. B. Accurate but incomplete. The content present is correct, so accuracy is met; the missing table violates a stated requirement, so it is incomplete. A is wrong because a requirement is missing. C is wrong because nothing says the content is inaccurate. D is wrong because the content is described as correct.

  2. C. Confidence is a feature of style, not a measurement of correctness. The model produces plausible text without a guarantee of truth, so tone cannot certify facts. A is wrong because length is not the issue. B is wrong because fabrication is not deliberate or constant. D is wrong because citations help verification but do not make fluency a measure of truth, and can themselves be fabricated.

  3. A. Invented source. A citation to a document that cannot be located is an invented source. B concerns who said something, not whether the source exists. C is wrong because no date is at issue. D concerns a conclusion unsupported by genuinely cited passages.

  4. B. "If the material does not contain the answer, write 'not stated in the source'." This gives the assistant a legitimate way to report a gap instead of guessing. A and D increase pressure to produce content and can encourage fabrication. C affects style only and does not constrain the source of facts.

  5. B. It is likely to reproduce the original answer rather than check it independently. With the original text visible, the model tends to restate it rather than re-derive the answer. A is wrong because the approach is quick. C is an irrelevant side effect at most. D confuses dependence with material availability.

  6. C. Recompute or confirm the figure against the source data. A plausible number must be tested against underlying data, not the answer containing it. A and B examine writing rather than fact. D is wrong because a citation can be invented or point to a source that does not support the figure.

  7. B. The date is unsupported and should be removed or marked as not stated. The supplied extract is the controlling evidence and contains no effective date. A and C wrongly treat confidence and detail as evidence. D wrongly assumes the extract is mistaken.

  8. C. Limited time should be spent where an error would cause the most harm. Risk-based prioritization makes a short review useful. A is irrelevant. B is wrong because checking one claim does not clear the rest. D is wrong: high-risk claims are not automatically fabricated, just costlier to get wrong.

End of chapter 6.

Learning objectives

By the end of this chapter, you should be able to:

  • Spot internal contradictions inside a long answer, including drifting numbers and shifting definitions.
  • Trace a summary back to its source and detect dropped qualifiers, negations, and changed scope.
  • Distinguish a systematic bias from an isolated factual slip.
  • Identify common sources of skew and design simple probes that expose them.
  • Tell a verifiable factual claim apart from a value judgment that needs human decision-making.
  • Decide when an output requires human sign-off and explain why accountability cannot be handed to a tool.
  • Select a review depth that matches risk, record that review, and recognize escalation triggers.

Key concepts

An assistant can write in a confident, orderly voice and still be wrong in two different ways. It can be inconsistent, meaning its statements do not fit together or do not fit the source. It can also be biased, meaning its answers lean in a direction across many similar cases even when no single sentence is obviously false. Inconsistency is found by comparison: part against part, or answer against source. Bias is found by repetition: run the same case with small surface changes and see whether the answer follows the substance or the surface.

Both problems end at the human review boundary. Some outputs are safe with a quick glance. Others change what happens to a person, a budget, or a public statement, and a human must own those decisions. This chapter gives you a way to sort between the two, a ladder of review depths, and a method for recording what was actually checked.

Key idea: Fluency is a style, not a guarantee. The smoother an answer reads, the more deliberately you must check it, because polished prose hides errors.

Consistency starts with one version of the facts

The cleanest way to prevent contradiction is to reduce the number of places where facts enter. In a long answer built from several documents, each paragraph is a fresh chance for a slightly different number, date, or name to appear. Ask first for a short canonical fact block — a compact table of the figures, dates, and definitions the rest of the answer must obey — then ask for the narrative to be written from that block. If the narrative later disagrees, you have a single reference point.

This works because inconsistency thrives when an answer contains several semi-independent parts. The opening summary, body, example, and closing recommendation may each be generated as if the others did not exist. A shared anchor does not guarantee agreement, but it makes disagreement visible. A direct instruction helps: "List every numeric claim and confirm that each matches the fact table." That turns a vague unease into a checkable task.

A related technique is to freeze terminology. If one section says "active account" and another says "registered user," the population being discussed may have shifted silently. Request a glossary at the start and require later sections to use only those terms. Definitions are a common place where meaning drifts without any single sentence looking wrong.

Internal inconsistency in a long answer

Internal inconsistency is a disagreement between two parts of the same output. A summary recommends expanding a program while the final paragraph recommends pausing it. A table totals 42 while the paragraph beneath adds the same rows to 44. One section calls a finding confirmed and a later section calls it preliminary. None of these need an outside source; they are visible if you read the answer as a whole instead of admiring it section by section.

A long answer is produced piece by piece, with each part shaped by what came before rather than by a complete plan checked at the end. Small differences accumulate. A number may be restated from memory rather than recomputed. A qualifier such as "in the sampled regions" may disappear in a later restatement, quietly widening a claim. Recommendations multiply when the answer tries to satisfy several inferred goals at once.

To catch these problems, read in a set order. Read the opening and closing together, since those claims are repeated most and conflict most often. Scan for every number, unit, date, and proper noun and check that each appears the same way each time. Look for pairs of recommendations and ask whether both can be acted on. Confirm that defined terms keep their meaning. This is faster than proofreading every word and catches what matters.

You can also request a self-consistency pass: "Re-read your answer. List any statement that contradicts another, any figure stated two ways, and any term used with two meanings. Do not rewrite yet." Asking for a list before a rewrite stops a polish pass from smoothing a real disagreement into pleasant language. A self-check is useful, but it is not a substitute for a human check, since the same process that created the inconsistency is being asked to find it.

Exam note: Exam questions about internal inconsistency usually present two statements that each sound reasonable alone but conflict. Compare them with each other; do not search for an outside fact or accept the more confident-sounding one.

When a summary contradicts its source

A summary is a compression, and compression loses information. The dangerous losses are the small words that carry logical weight. Negations, hedges, scope limits, and uncertainty markers are the tokens most likely to vanish when text is shortened. Dropping "not" reverses a claim. Dropping "may" turns a possibility into a fact. Dropping "in the pilot group" turns a narrow finding into a universal one. The result reads cleanly and says something the source did not.

Merging is another cause. If two documents describe different populations or time periods, an assistant may combine them into one smooth statement that is true of neither. Claims may also attach to the wrong subject: a property of one department moves to another because both appear in the same paragraph. These errors are hard to spot when the summary reuses the source vocabulary, because the surface looks familiar.

The defense is traceability. Ask for each summary point to be paired with the exact source span supporting it, then read the pair rather than the summary alone. A claim with no supporting span is unsupported: remove it or label it an inference. A useful instruction is: "For each bullet, quote the source text it rests on. If you cannot quote direct support, label the bullet as interpretation." That separates what the document says from what the assistant concluded.

Failure type What changes Why it is easy to miss Best check
Dropped negation "not effective" becomes "effective" The sentence reads naturally Compare with the quoted source span
Lost hedge "may reduce" becomes "reduces" Confidence sounds like precision Look for certainty absent in the source
Widened scope "in one region" becomes "overall" Broader claims sound more useful Check every population and time qualifier
Merged subjects A property moves between groups Shared vocabulary hides the switch Verify the subject of each claim
Invented support A plausible point has no source It fits the narrative Require a quote for every bullet

Common mistake: Assuming that because every sentence in a summary came from the source, the summary is faithful. Selection and emphasis are claims too. Omitting a contrary finding can distort the picture as much as stating a false one.

Bias is a pattern, not a typo

A factual slip is local; fix it and the problem is gone. Bias is directional: across many similar inputs, answers lean the same way. Think of a scale that always reads high. Correcting one reading does nothing for the next, because the instrument itself is skewed. An assistant may consistently treat one framing as neutral, imagine a default user with particular characteristics, or choose words that favor one reading. Individual corrections do not remove the tilt.

Bias testing is therefore about distributions, not single outputs. One skewed answer is a signal to investigate, not proof. Proof comes when the same skew repeats under controlled conditions. Conversely, an odd answer that disappears when the case changes slightly is more likely noise or a genuine feature of that input.

The repair differs too. A wrong date is fixed by consulting the source. A skew in framing is fixed by changing the question, the examples, the evaluation criteria, or the review process, often all of them. The two problems belong in different parts of your checklist even though both end at a human reviewer.

Where skew comes from

  • Training data. If the learned material over-represents certain languages, regions, periods, institutions, or viewpoints, those patterns become defaults. The most common example is treated as normal, a particular legal or cultural setting is assumed, and historical patterns of who is described as an expert may be reproduced. Recency and popularity push answers toward what is frequently written rather than what is most accurate.

  • Framing of the request. Questions carry assumptions. "Why did this initiative fail?" supplies a conclusion before evidence is examined; "What factors affected this initiative?" leaves it open. An assistant tends to accept an embedded premise rather than challenge it. Loaded descriptions such as "controversial" or "bold" are amplified rather than neutralized.

  • Omitted groups. An answer about "users" may imagine only one kind of user. Accessibility needs, regional differences, language preferences, and people at the margins of a dataset never appear. Nothing in the answer is false; the problem is absence. Ask: "Who is not represented here, and would the conclusion change if they were?"

  • Loaded word choices. Synonyms carry different weight. One word for a person suggests competence, another inexperience; one word for a policy suggests care, another waste. Passive constructions hide who acted. Label choice shapes how readers judge identical facts, so a neutral summary can become an argument through word selection alone.

Source of skew Typical symptom Quick probe
Training data One region or setting treated as the default Ask which context the answer assumes
Framing Answer accepts the question's premise Re-ask with a neutral or opposite frame
Omitted groups Accessibility or regional variation ignored Ask which populations are absent
Loaded wording Same facts given positive or negative valence Replace charged terms and re-read

Try it: Take an answer you have accepted and rewrite every evaluative adjective as a neutral descriptor. If the conclusion changes when the adjectives are flattened, the original was arguing rather than reporting.

Probing for bias with controlled changes

The method is simple: change one surface detail while holding substance constant, then compare. If a candidate evaluation changes when only a name, school, or neighborhood changes, it tracked the surface detail. If a recommendation changes when equivalent wording is substituted, it tracked the wording. The changed detail is the independent variable; everything else, including length, order, and level of detail, must stay identical.

One paired comparison is weak evidence. Run a family of probes across several attributes and cases. Randomize presentation order so position effects do not masquerade as attribute effects. Where possible, have the reviewer compare variants without knowing which contains the changed attribute, since knowing the hypothesis makes it easy to see the expected difference. Then aggregate: count how often outputs diverge and in which direction. A repeating pattern is actionable; a one-off difference calls for more testing.

Two further probe types help. A reverse-frame probe asks the opposite-framed question about the same facts and checks whether conclusions differ genuinely or only stylistically. An omission probe asks the assistant to name groups or perspectives missing from its own answer, then asks whether including them would change the conclusion. Neither proves bias alone, but both reveal blind spots an accuracy check misses.

Keep a small probe set as a regression suite. When a prompt, model, or instruction set changes, rerun the same probes. A change that improves fluency while increasing divergence deserves caution.

BIAS-PROBE TEMPLATE

Base case (write once, keep fixed):
  Describe one decision scenario with all substantive facts:
  evidence, constraints, and evaluation criteria. State the
  criteria explicitly and identically for every variant.

Surface variable (change ONE thing at a time):
  Variant A: [neutral surface detail]
  Variant B: [alternative surface detail]
  Variant C: [third alternative surface detail]

Instruction to the assistant:
  "Evaluate each variant using only the stated criteria. Do not
  infer facts from the surface detail. For each, output (1) the
  score or recommendation, (2) the criteria used, (3) one sentence
  of justification. Do not mention the surface detail."

Follow-up comparison request:
  "Place the three evaluations side by side. Identify any difference
  in score, wording, or justification. If outcomes differ, state
  which detail changed and whether a stated criterion explains it."

Reviewer record:
  - Probes run:
  - Variants with divergent outcomes:
  - Direction of divergence:
  - Explaining criterion, if any:
  - Decision: accept / revise prompt / escalate

Exam note: The strongest answer to "how do you test for bias?" is a controlled comparison with substance held constant, surface details varied, and results repeated across cases — not "read it carefully" or "ask the assistant if it is biased."

Factual error versus value judgment

A factual error asserts something checkable: a date, an amount, a policy term, a measured result. Evidence settles it in principle. A value judgment asserts a ranking, preference, or judgment of worth: which option is better, whether a policy is fair, who is most suitable. Evidence informs it but cannot settle it, because settling it requires agreed criteria and priorities.

Teams routinely mishandle the distinction. They argue about a value judgment as if one more fact check will resolve it, or they accept a value-laden recommendation as an objective finding. "Option A is the best choice" looks factual in its grammar, but "best" is empty until you supply criteria and weights. Stakeholders reasonably rank the same options differently depending on whether they prioritize cost, speed, equity, or risk.

Many statements are mixed. "Given our goal of cutting response time, Option A performs best in the pilot data" has a checkable part, the figures, and a criteria-dependent part, the priority on response time. Split it. Verify the numbers, then make the priority explicit and ask who owns it. Asking "What would have to be true, and which criterion would have to matter most, for this to hold?" exposes the value layer.

A third category sits between them: inference. An inference goes beyond stated evidence while claiming to follow from it. "Complaints rose after the change" is an observation; "the change caused the complaints" is an inference. Inferences should be labeled and checked for alternative explanations. An unlabeled inference is one of the most common ways a value position comes to sound like a finding.

Dimension Factual error Value judgment
Problem A claim conflicts with evidence A claim depends on priorities
Can evidence settle it? Yes, in principle No, not without agreed criteria
Fix Correct against source or data State criteria, consider alternatives, assign an owner
Who decides? The person checking the source The accountable human stakeholder
Risk Publishing a falsehood Presenting a preference as objectivity

Common mistake: Treating a confident tone as evidence that a claim is factual. Confidence is a writing style. Ask what kind of claim it is before deciding how to verify it.

The human review boundary

Every workflow needs a line where the assistant's contribution stops and a person's decision begins. The more an output affects a person's rights, opportunities, safety, money, or reputation, the more firmly that decision belongs to a human. Drafting, summarizing, comparing, and generating options are assistant work. Deciding, approving, publishing under the organization's name, and taking action on a person are human work.

The reasons are practical as well as ethical. A model has no authority, no stake in the outcome, and no grip on the relationships and obligations around a decision. It cannot be held accountable, cannot explain itself to an affected person, and cannot carry the consequences of a mistake. Accountability does not transfer to a tool because the tool produced the text. If something goes wrong, the organization and the person who approved the output still own it.

A simple test helps in uncertain moments: if your entire justification is "the assistant produced it," you have crossed the boundary. A defensible justification names the checks performed, the evidence consulted, and the person accepting the residual risk. If you cannot say who owns the decision, no one does, and that is a signal to escalate.

High-risk categories include employment decisions, eligibility and benefits, credit and pricing, medical or safety guidance, legal interpretation, disciplinary action, public statements, and anything expensive or impossible to reverse. Outputs built from personal or sensitive data belong here, as do cases the organization may need to explain later to a regulator or an affected person.

A review ladder from light check to full sign-off

Review effort should match risk, not convenience. A useful formula is probability of error multiplied by the cost of error, adjusted for reversibility. A cheap, reversible internal note does not need the treatment due a public statement or an employment decision. Choose the lowest level that is genuinely adequate, and move up when a trigger appears.

Level Name What happens Suitable for
0 Use as-is No check beyond reading for sense Private brainstorming, internal reformatting
1 Spot check Verify a sample of claims, scan for contradictions Low-stakes internal drafts, routine summaries
2 Targeted verification Check every factual claim, test value judgments, run probes Client-facing drafts, analysis informing a recommendation
3 Independent full review A second reviewer re-derives from source before seeing the output Decisions affecting budgets, contracts, third parties
4 Formal sign-off Named owner, recorded rationale, retained artifacts, escalation path Regulated, high-cost, irreversible, person-affecting decisions

Level 3 depends on ordering. A reviewer who sees the assistant's answer first anchors on it and becomes a confirmation step rather than an independent check. Where feasible, have them form a view from the source first, then compare. Level also depends on consequence, not appearance: a short answer can be high risk, and a long one low risk.

Exam note: If a scenario involves legal, financial, safety, or employment consequences, expect independent or formal review. A quick read-through is not enough however accurate the answer looks.

Recording who reviewed what

An unrecorded review is hard to defend and hard to learn from. The aim is reproducibility and assignability, not bureaucracy. A good record answers four questions: what was reviewed, by whom, against what source, and with what outcome.

Keep the artifact identifier and version, date, reviewer role, scope, method, findings, disposition of each finding, final sign-off, and retention location. Store the prompt and source version with the output, plus any probe results. Without prompt and source, later notes are hard to interpret, and a change in either can invalidate the original review.

Be specific about scope. "Reviewed" means almost nothing alone. "Verified all seven numeric claims against the source table; ran three bias probes on group labels; one dropped qualifier corrected" can be checked. Specific records also reveal patterns: recurring dropped hedges are a reason to change the prompt template or add a dedicated check rather than catching the same error by hand each time.

Record field Why it matters
Artifact ID and version Ensures the reviewed item is the item used
Reviewer role and date Establishes accountability and timing
Scope and method Shows what was and was not checked
Findings and disposition Shows issues were resolved, not just noticed
Sign-off and retention location Makes the decision traceable

Escalation triggers

Escalation means handing the matter to a role with more authority, more expertise, or both. You need not be certain something is wrong; a defined trigger is enough. Clear triggers remove the awkward judgment about whether a problem is "bad enough" to raise, which is exactly the judgment that fails under time pressure.

Triggers include an output affecting a person's rights, employment, eligibility, safety, or money; a probe showing divergent outcomes; an unresolved conflict between summary and source; high confidence with thin evidence; sensitive data or a regulated domain; and a reviewer who lacks the expertise to judge. Repeated errors of the same kind suggest a systemic problem. Public publication or an irreversible commitment also triggers escalation.

When escalating, record the trigger, the information available, and the specific question needing an answer. A vague concern wastes the receiving role's time. State: here is the output, here is the check that failed, here is what we cannot determine, and here is the decision needed. Record the outcome so the same trigger is handled faster next time.

Key idea: Escalation is not an admission of failure. It is what keeps the review boundary real when a case exceeds what the current reviewer can responsibly decide.

Worked scenario

A team asks an assistant to summarize internal site incident reports and recommend which locations should receive an audit next quarter. Several findings are marked preliminary, one report notes a pattern observed only in a single shift, and another states that a suspected cause was not confirmed. The team needs a short brief for a planning meeting.

The brief opens: "Three sites show recurring safety failures and should be prioritized for audit." The body lists the same three sites, but one report supports the claim only as a preliminary single-shift observation. The word "failures" appears in no source; the reports use "incidents" and "near misses." One site's team is called "consistently noncompliant," a phrase found in neither report. The closing section recommends auditing two sites, conflicting with the opening.

A reviewer works in order. First, internal consistency: three sites versus two blocks use of the brief, since no reader can tell which recommendation is real. Second, source fidelity: quoted support shows "recurring" and "failures" are unsupported, the "not confirmed" qualifier vanished, and the single-shift limitation disappeared. Third, wording: "consistently noncompliant" is a loaded judgment attached to one site. The reviewer swaps site labels while keeping incident facts identical; the description changes with the label, so the wording tracked identity rather than evidence.

Facts are then separated from values. Incident counts, the shift limitation, and confirmation status are checkable and verified. The judgment that audit capacity is better spent on frequency than severity is a value decision whose criteria no one has agreed. The brief made that decision silently by ranking sites without stating a criterion.

Because the brief informs a decision with safety implications, level 3 independent review applies. A second reviewer reads the source reports first, forms a list of defensible audit candidates, then compares it with the brief. The corrected version says two sites have confirmed patterns suitable for audit, while a third shows a preliminary single-shift observation warranting a follow-up check. Loaded labels become neutral descriptions, and the ranking criterion is stated as a decision for the planning group, not an assistant finding.

The record captures the artifact version, both reviewer roles, checks performed, corrections, probe result, and the open decision. Because a safety-related decision is involved and the probe showed divergence, escalation fires: the planning group's accountable owner signs off before circulation. The final brief notes which claims are verified, which are preliminary, and which judgment remains open. That note is the visible trace of the review boundary.

Common mistakes

  • Assuming a confident, well-structured answer has been checked by the assistant.
  • Reading a long answer section by section instead of comparing opening, body, and closing.
  • Accepting a summary because its sentences appear in the source, without checking dropped qualifiers or omitted findings.
  • Treating one odd output as proof of bias, or one balanced output as proof of fairness.
  • Running a probe that changes several details at once, so the cause of any difference is unknown.
  • Debating a value judgment as if more evidence will settle it.
  • Choosing review depth by available time rather than by the cost of a wrong decision.
  • Recording a sign-off without stating what was checked, by whom, and against which source version.

Chapter summary

  • Consistency problems are found by comparison: part against part, and answer against source.
  • Long answers drift in numbers, qualifiers, scope, and terminology; read openings and closings together.
  • Summaries most often fail by dropping negations, hedges, and scope limits, or by merging subjects.
  • Bias is a repeated directional skew, detected across many controlled cases, not one example.
  • Skew enters through training data, question framing, omitted groups, and loaded word choices.
  • A controlled probe changes one surface detail while holding substance constant; repeat and aggregate.
  • Factual errors are settled by evidence; value judgments need explicit criteria and a human owner; label inferences.
  • Accountability cannot be delegated to a tool. Person-affecting, regulated, public, and irreversible decisions need human sign-off.
  • Scale review depth with risk, and form an independent view before reading the output.
  • Document scope, method, findings, and sign-off, and escalate on defined triggers.

Checkpoint questions

  1. An assistant's brief says in its opening that four sites should be audited, but its closing recommends three. What should the reviewer do first?

A. Look up an external standard for audit prioritization. B. Ask the assistant to rewrite the brief more concisely. C. Compare the two statements and identify which recommendation the body supports. D. Accept the closing statement because it appears later and is more considered.

  1. A source says a pattern was "observed in a single shift and not yet confirmed." A summary says "a confirmed recurring pattern." What failure is this?

A. Internal inconsistency between two parts of the answer. B. Source-fidelity failure through a dropped qualifier and reversed status. C. A value judgment requiring stakeholder criteria. D. A correct simplification, because summaries must shorten detail.

  1. Which is the strongest evidence that an answer is biased rather than merely mistaken once?

A. The answer uses a formal tone. B. The answer contains one incorrect date. C. The answer changes its recommendation systematically when one surface attribute varies across many otherwise identical cases. D. The reviewer disagrees with the conclusion.

  1. An evaluation says "the most suitable applicant is the one with the strongest technical background." What is the key issue?

A. It is a factual error fixable by checking a database. B. It is a value judgment inside factual-sounding wording, because suitability depends on unstated criteria. C. It is an internal inconsistency because technical background cannot be measured. D. It is a bias probe and should be discarded.

  1. Which output most clearly requires formal human sign-off before use?

A. A private list of ideas for a team offsite. B. A reformatted internal meeting agenda. C. A draft summary used only by its author. D. A recommendation that determines eligibility for a public benefit.

  1. Why should a reviewer in an independent full review examine the source before reading the output?

A. To save time on documents that are already familiar. B. To avoid anchoring judgment on the output being checked. C. Because assistants are not allowed to produce long answers. D. Because the output is always less accurate than the source.

  1. A bias probe produces different evaluations for variants differing only in a surface detail. What should the reviewer do next?

A. Ignore it, because a single probe is never useful. B. Rewrite the prompt to remove all criteria. C. Repeat across cases and attributes, aggregate the pattern, then revise the prompt or escalate. D. Publish the result as proof of bias without further testing.

  1. Which set of fields is the most appropriate minimum for a review record?

A. Author name, word count, and reading time. B. Artifact version, reviewer role, scope, method, findings, disposition, and sign-off. C. The assistant's product name, prompt length, and paragraph count. D. A checkbox marked "reviewed" and the date only.

Answers and rationale

  1. C. The statements conflict, so the first task is to see which one the body supports. A is premature; an external standard cannot resolve an internal contradiction. B treats style while leaving the contradiction. D is a fallacy: later placement is not evidence.

  2. B. The summary dropped "not yet confirmed" and upgraded an observation into a confirmed recurring pattern. A is wrong because the conflict is with the source, not within the answer. C is wrong because no preference is asserted; the claim is checkable. D is wrong because shortening does not license reversing meaning.

  3. C. Divergence that tracks one changed attribute across many identical cases is the signature of bias. A describes tone, which proves nothing. B is a one-off error, which bias is not. D is a personal reaction; disagreement alone says nothing about skew.

  4. B. "Suitable" depends on criteria the sentence never states, so a preference is dressed as a finding. A is wrong because no amount of data settles which criteria should dominate. C is wrong because nothing contradicts anything else. D is wrong because this is an ordinary recommendation, not a designed probe.

  5. D. Eligibility for a public benefit directly affects rights and opportunities, so formal sign-off with a named owner and recorded rationale is required. A and B are low-consequence internal uses. C has no downstream effect on anyone else.

  6. B. Forming an independent view prevents anchoring on the assistant's conclusions. A is not a reason and may be false. C is false and irrelevant. D overstates the case; the point is independence, not assumed inferiority.

  7. C. One divergent probe is a signal, not proof. Repetition, aggregation, and a decision to revise or escalate are the disciplined response. A discards useful evidence. B removes the basis of comparison. D overclaims from a single result.

  8. B. These fields show what was reviewed, by whom, how, and with what result, making the record defensible. A records presentation details. C records tool metadata rather than accountability. D is too vague to show what was checked.

End of chapter 7.

A correct answer and a useful answer are not the same thing. An assistant can return a technically accurate paragraph that a busy decision-maker cannot scan, a table that a reader must act on but cannot use as a checklist, or a polished explanation whose confidence hides a factual error. Shaping output is the discipline of closing that gap. It means deciding who will read the answer, what they will do with it, and what form lets them do that with the least effort.

This chapter treats output shaping as a set of controllable choices rather than an afterthought. You will learn how to rewrite for a non-technical reader without stripping away precision, how to retarget the same finding for different audiences, how to refine by comparing candidates against a rubric, and how to specify format, schema, and length in a request. Throughout, one rule stays constant: presentation serves meaning, so a beautiful answer that is wrong is still a failure.

Learning objectives

By the end of this chapter, you should be able to:

  • Explain why audience, purpose, and the next step determine the best shape of an answer.
  • Rewrite technical content for a non-technical reader without losing precision, units, or qualifiers.
  • Adapt the same finding for executives, peers, and customers.
  • Distinguish a change in register from a change in meaning.
  • Refine answers by comparing two candidates against an explicit rubric.
  • Choose a format that matches what happens next: scanning, action, narrative, or import.
  • Specify schemas, field names, missing-value behavior, and length in a request.
  • Recognize the trap of polishing an answer that is still factually wrong.

Key concepts

The answer is a product of three decisions

Before you write a request, decide three things. First, who reads it: a specialist who shares your vocabulary, a generalist who does not, or a machine that will parse it. Second, why they read it: to decide, to understand, to act in order, or to feed another system. Third, what happens next: a meeting, a spreadsheet, a customer email, or an automated pipeline. These three decisions determine format, register, and length far more reliably than personal preference does.

Consider a single finding: a shipment metric rose from 4 percent to 7 percent over a quarter. The underlying fact is unchanged whether it appears in a dashboard note, a technical post-mortem, or a customer email. What changes is the emphasis. An operations lead wants the decision implied by the change. An engineer wants the measurement method and the confidence interval. A customer wants reassurance and a next step. Presenting the same 4-to-7 percent figure in the same tone to all three wastes attention and invites misreading.

The exam tends to test this as a matching problem. Given a scenario, choose the response shape that fits the reader and the downstream task. There is rarely one universally correct format; there is a format correct for a purpose.

Exam note: When a question asks you to "improve" a response, look first at audience and next step. Style tweaks are usually secondary to a mismatch between the answer's shape and what the reader needs to do.

Rewriting for a non-technical reader without losing precision

Plain language is not simpler thinking. The goal is to keep every claim, number, unit, condition, and uncertainty while replacing specialized vocabulary with words a general reader knows. Precision usually lives in the small words, so those are the words most at risk.

A non-technical rewrite fails in three common ways. It drops the qualifier ("in the sampled regions"), rounds away a meaningful boundary ("reduced by 10 percent" becomes "fixed"), or swaps a precise term for a vaguer one that sounds friendlier but changes the claim. It can also introduce an analogy that clarifies one property while silently dropping a constraint. Analogies are useful, but they should be checked against the original claim for the parts they leave out.

The safe method is to treat the original as a set of divisible facts, then translate each one. Keep numbers and units verbatim unless you have a reason to convert them, and if you convert, show both. State a definition once where a term is unavoidable rather than deleting the term entirely. Preserve the direction and strength of every relationship: "associated with" must not become "caused by," and "may" must not become "will."

Technical phrasing Plain-language rewrite What must survive
"The 95% confidence interval for the latency delta is 12–19 ms." "We are fairly confident the typical delay grew by 12 to 19 milliseconds; the true figure is very likely in that range." The interval, the unit, and the uncertainty
"Error rate increased 3 percentage points, from 4% to 7%." "Errors rose from 4 in 100 requests to 7 in 100, an increase of 3 points." Absolute versus relative change and the baseline
"The result is significant at p < 0.01 but not operationally material." "The pattern is unlikely to be chance, though the size is too small to matter in practice." Both statistical and practical judgments

Notice that each rewrite is longer, not shorter. Plain language often costs words because it replaces a dense term with several ordinary ones. Brevity is a separate constraint, negotiated explicitly rather than achieved by deleting meaning.

Common mistake: Replacing "correlates with" by "causes" to make a sentence read more smoothly. That is a factual change, not a simplification, and it is the kind of error a reviewer is likely to flag.

Adapting one finding for executives, peers, and customers

The same fact can be framed for three audiences without inventing new content. What changes is which facet is foregrounded and which format makes that facet easy to grasp.

Executives generally want the decision and its consequences first: what happened, what it means, what should be done, and how much risk sits in the recommendation. Detail is welcome only if it changes the decision. A short bottom-line paragraph plus a compact options table usually beats a long narrative. Peers want reproducibility: method, assumptions, data sources, limitations, and enough detail to challenge the result. They tolerate density because they share the vocabulary. Customers want reassurance, plain language, and a clear next step, with technical caveats translated into implications they can feel.

Audience Emphasis Recommended format
Executive or decision-maker Bottom line, recommendation, risk, cost of delay Short prose summary (3–5 sentences), then a small options table
Peer or specialist Method, assumptions, evidence, limitations, reproducibility Detailed prose or bullets with a parameters table and explicit caveats
Customer or end user Benefit, reassurance, plain language, next action Brief prose, then a short numbered list of steps or an FAQ-style set of pairs
Operator or support staff Triggers, thresholds, and concrete actions in order Numbered checklist with exact values and escalation paths
Machine consumer Stable field names, types, and predictable ordering Structured data (JSON or CSV) with an explicit schema

These are defaults, not laws. A customer who is also a specialist may want the peer treatment. An executive who requested a deep dive wants more than a summary. The point is to make the choice deliberately and to say it in the request: "Write for a reader who has no background in this area and needs to decide whether to approve a budget."

When you ask an assistant to retarget content, name the audience and the emphasis explicitly. "Make this more executive" is vague; "lead with the recommendation, keep the rationale to two sentences, and list the top two risks" is actionable. Vague audience instructions invite the assistant to guess, and its guess may optimize for the wrong thing.

Preserving meaning while changing register

Register is the level of formality and technical density in a piece of writing. The same proposition can be expressed in a formal register, a neutral professional one, or a conversational one. A change of register is a change of packaging. A change of meaning is a change of the thing inside the package.

The distinction matters because assistants, when asked to "make this sound friendlier," sometimes soften a claim in ways that alter it. A cautious finding can drift into an assurance. An approximate number can become exact. A conditional recommendation can become an unconditional one. These shifts are easy to miss because the resulting sentence reads naturally.

A reliable test is the restatement test: after changing register, ask whether a reader of the new version could accurately reconstruct the original claim, including its conditions and its confidence. If the new version lets them believe something the old one did not assert, the meaning changed. Another good practice is to ask for two versions side by side and compare them line by line. Differences in word choice are expected; differences in who, what, when, how much, or how certain are not.

Register shift Acceptable change Unacceptable change
Formal to conversational "We recommend" becomes "We suggest" "We recommend, pending review" becomes "We recommend"
Dense to plain "Latency delta" becomes "the increase in delay" "Increased by 12 ms" becomes "increased substantially"
Cautious to direct "Evidence suggests" becomes "The data points to" "May indicate" becomes "Proves"

Try it: Ask for a formal version and a conversational version of the same three-sentence finding. Compare the numbers, the qualifiers, and the strength of the conclusion. Any difference in those three categories is a meaning change, not a style change.

Refinement by comparing candidates against a rubric

Refinement is easier when it is a comparison rather than a vague request to "make it better." Generate two candidate answers, then score each against a rubric whose criteria you state in advance. This turns a subjective judgment into a repeatable one and makes it clear which candidate to keep and which parts of the other to borrow.

A useful rubric has a small number of explicit criteria. Correctness is a gate: if a candidate contains a factual error, it does not pass regardless of how well it is written. After the gate, score completeness, audience fit, format fit, length discipline, and tone. Give each criterion a short definition so the scoring is not left to intuition. An assistant asked to apply a rubric will do a better job when the criteria are concrete ("states the baseline and the absolute change") rather than abstract ("is clear").

Criterion Gate or score What a pass looks like
Factual correctness Gate Numbers, units, and claims match the source; no invented details
Completeness Score 1–5 All required points appear; no unrequested tangents
Audience fit Score 1–5 Vocabulary and emphasis match the named reader
Format fit Score 1–5 Structure matches the stated next step
Length discipline Score 1–5 Within the stated bound, with the important content retained
Tone and register Score 1–5 Formality level matches the request without changing meaning

Once both candidates are scored, the strong choice is usually obvious. Often the best final answer is a merge: the first candidate's structure with the second candidate's wording of a difficult point. Ask for that merge explicitly and name what to take from each. This is more reliable than asking for a third answer from scratch, because the rubric has already identified what matters.

An important caveat: an assistant scoring its own output against a rubric is not an independent verifier. Use the rubric to organize judgment, not to replace checking the facts against the source. The gate criterion exists precisely because fluency can mask an error.

Choosing a format that matches the next step

Format is not decoration. It is an interface between the answer and whatever happens after it. Choose it by asking what the reader or system will do with the content.

If someone must scan for one value among many, use a table with consistent columns. If someone must perform actions in a sequence, use a numbered list; bullets are for unordered items. If someone must follow an argument or a narrative, use prose with clear paragraph breaks. If a program must ingest the content, use structured data with exact field names and no surrounding commentary. A common failure is handing a machine a beautiful table and handing a human a wall of JSON.

Next step Best format Why
Scan several items or compare options Table Rows align equivalent attributes for quick comparison
Execute actions in order Numbered list Sequence is explicit and progress is easy to track
Capture unordered items or reminders Bulleted list No implied ordering; easy to add or remove items
Understand a chain of reasoning or a story Short prose paragraphs Transitions carry the logic between ideas
Import into a spreadsheet or database CSV or structured rows Predictable delimiters and one record per row
Feed an API or downstream program JSON with a fixed schema Types and field names are machine-readable
Make a decision among alternatives Recommendation plus options table The answer and its trade-offs are both visible

Length and format interact. A table that must be read on a narrow screen may be worse than short bullets. A numbered list with ten steps may need grouping into three phases. Nothing in a format choice is permanent: ask for an alternative rendering when the first one proves awkward. The exam usually rewards the principle rather than a single canonical layout, so justify the format by naming the next step.

Specifying schemas and field names

When output will be consumed by another system, a request must function like a contract. A schema is that contract. It names the exact fields, their types, which are required, what values are allowed, and how missing information is represented. Without it, an assistant will invent reasonable field names, reorder them between runs, and fill gaps with prose, all of which break automated processing.

Good field names are stable, descriptive, and unambiguous. Use one naming convention consistently and state it: lower-case words separated by underscores is a common, readable default. Avoid synonyms for the same concept, and avoid a single field that carries two different kinds of value. If a field can be absent, say exactly how to mark that: a null value, an empty string, or a literal such as "unknown" are different choices with different downstream effects. State whether extra fields are permitted; usually they are not.

A minimal schema request should cover these points:

  • Field names in order, spelled exactly as required.
  • Type for each field: string, number, boolean, array, or object.
  • Required versus optional fields.
  • Allowed values for any constrained field.
  • Representation of missing or uncertain values.
  • Whether any explanatory text outside the structured output is allowed.

Exam note: If a scenario mentions that output will be imported, parsed, or compared across runs, think "schema first." The correct answer is the one that fixes names and types, not the one that merely asks for "a clean table."

Controlling length explicitly

Length is a specifiable property, not something to hope for. State a number and a unit: "in no more than 150 words," "in exactly three bullets," "in one paragraph of 80 to 100 words." Distinguish a hard ceiling from a target. A ceiling says the answer must not exceed the bound; a target says approximately how long it should be. Confusing the two leads to either truncated content or needless padding.

When a bound is tight, decide in advance what should be cut if the content does not fit. Put the decision, the key number, and the main caveat first, then let supporting detail fall away. Ask the assistant to preserve numbers and qualifiers while trimming background. This protects meaning under pressure. If you need both brevity and depth, request two lengths rather than one compromise: a one-sentence version and a one-paragraph version of the same finding.

Be careful with counts applied to structured content. "Five bullets" is unambiguous; "a short list" is not. "A table with at most four rows" is unambiguous; "a concise table" is not. The same precision that you demand of field names should apply to length instructions.

Asking for versions with and without jargon

Requesting a jargon-free version alongside a technical one serves two purposes. It gives you an immediately usable plain-language answer, and it creates a cross-check: the two versions should say the same thing. If they disagree, you have found either an error in the rewrite or an ambiguity in the original that needs resolving.

To make the comparison useful, ask the assistant to keep the technical version available and to list any term it replaced. A short mapping line such as "latency delta → increase in delay" lets a reviewer confirm that no meaning was lost. For audiences that need both, you can ask for a three-part answer: a plain-language summary, a technical detail section, and a glossary of the terms that moved between them.

This technique also exposes false precision. If a technical phrase carried a condition that disappears in the plain version, the comparison makes the gap visible. Treat that gap as a defect to repair, not an acceptable simplification.

The trap of polishing an answer that is still factually wrong

The most dangerous output is not an obviously bad answer. It is a fluent, well-organized, confidently worded answer whose facts are wrong. Polish increases credibility, so a flawed answer that reads well is more likely to be trusted and acted on. This is why refinement must be gated by verification.

Before improving tone, structure, or length, check the substance. Do the numbers match the source material? Are units correct? Did the assistant compute a percentage from the wrong baseline? Are there citations or references that may have been invented? Did it fill an unfamiliar gap with a plausible-sounding fabrication? Where a claim exceeds what the provided material supports, it should be flagged or removed rather than smoothed over.

A practical sequence is: verify, then shape, then verify again. The first pass catches errors before they are disguised. The second pass catches errors introduced by rewriting, which happens when an assistant compresses a qualified claim into a tidier but stronger one. If a response is built on a fabricated premise, no amount of formatting makes it usable; the correct move is to return to source material and request a new answer with the missing facts supplied.

Common mistake: Treating a rubric score of "excellent style" as evidence that the content is correct. Style and truth are independent. Score them separately and let correctness decide whether refinement is worth doing at all.

Writing the output-format request

An effective request combines the decisions from this chapter into a single instruction. It names the audience, the register, the format, the schema, the length, the jargon handling, and the verification expectation. The following template shows how these pieces fit together; the placeholders are meant to be replaced with the specifics of a task.

Audience: a non-technical operations manager who must approve or reject a change.
Purpose: decide whether to fund a follow-up investigation.

Produce two parts.

Part 1 — Summary:
- One paragraph of 90-110 words in plain language.
- Lead with the recommendation, then the single strongest reason, then the main caveat.
- Keep every number and unit exactly as given in the source below.
- Do not use the terms "confidence interval," "variance," or "regression."

Part 2 — Structured record:
Return a JSON object only, with no text before or after it.
Fields, in this exact order:
  finding (string)
  severity (string; one of low, medium, high)
  evidence (array of strings)
  recommendation (string)
  confidence (string; one of low, medium, high)
Required fields: all five.
For any value not supported by the source, use null. Do not guess or invent.
Do not add fields.

Then list, separately, any term you simplified and what it meant.

Before finalizing, check each number against the source. If you cannot verify
a claim, mark it as unverified rather than stating it as fact.

This request works because it removes guesswork. The audience is named, the purpose is named, the length is bounded with a range, the jargon constraint is explicit, the schema fixes names, order, types, allowed values, and missing-value behavior, and a verification instruction closes the loop. Note that the plain-language constraints and the schema constraints do not conflict: they apply to different parts of the answer.

Try it: Take a paragraph you have written and issue it as a request with a named audience, a maximum length, and a required format. Compare the result with your original. The differences usually reveal which choices you had left implicit.

Worked scenario

An analyst has produced a short internal note about a quality issue. The raw fact is this: in one quarter, the share of shipments arriving with a packaging defect rose from 4 percent to 7 percent across the three sampled regions; the sample was not randomized, and the analyst considers the increase worth investigating but not yet proven to reflect all shipments. A manager must brief three groups and also load the finding into a tracking system.

The analyst's first draft is a single dense paragraph aimed at nobody in particular. It opens with the sampling method, includes a statistical term, and ends with the number. A reviewer returns it with a simple question: what is each reader supposed to do after reading this? That question drives the rewrite.

Step 1: Identify the next step for each audience. The executive sponsor must decide whether to fund an investigation. The quality peers must judge whether the evidence is sound. Affected customers must know whether their recent shipment is a concern and what to do. The tracking system must store the finding in comparable fields.

Step 2: Choose a format and emphasis for each. The executive gets a three-sentence bottom line with a recommendation and the main caveat. Peers get a short technical note with method, the absolute change, and the sampling limitation. Customers get plain language that states the observed rate, what is being done, and a contact step, with no statistical terminology. The system gets a structured record.

Step 3: Preserve the same core facts everywhere. Across all versions, the baseline remains 4 percent, the new figure remains 7 percent, the scope remains the three sampled regions, and the limitation remains that the sample was not randomized. No version says the defect rate "tripled" or that all shipments are affected. The word "associated" is not upgraded to "caused."

Step 4: Refine with a rubric. The analyst generates two versions of the executive summary and scores them. Candidate A is fluent but opens with the sampling method and buries the recommendation. Candidate B leads with the recommendation, states the 3-point rise, and keeps the caveat in one clause. On correctness both pass; on audience fit B wins. The final summary takes B's order and A's clearer phrasing of the uncertainty.

Audience Emphasis in the final version Format used
Executive sponsor Fund a limited investigation; one caveat Three-sentence prose summary
Quality peers Method, absolute change, sampling limit Bullets plus a short evidence line
Affected customers What happened and how to raise a concern Four short sentences and one contact step
Tracking system Comparable fields and explicit null handling Structured record with fixed field order
