The resolution is not four different findings. It is one finding rendered four ways, each shaped for a reader or system, with the core facts held constant. The reviewer's final check is the restatement test applied across all four: does each version let its reader reconstruct the same underlying claim? When all four pass, the output set is internally consistent and fit for purpose.

Common mistakes

  • Choosing a format before identifying what the reader will do next.
  • Simplifying a claim by deleting its qualifiers, units, or baseline.
  • Treating a change in register as permission to strengthen a conclusion.
  • Asking for "a better answer" without stating the rubric or criteria.
  • Scoring style highly while skipping the factual correctness gate.
  • Giving a machine-readable format to a human reader, or prose to an importer.
  • Requesting a schema without specifying missing-value behavior or allowed values.
  • Stating a length as "brief" rather than as a number with a unit.
  • Polishing a response before checking whether its numbers came from the source.
  • Assuming the assistant's own rubric score counts as independent verification.

Chapter summary

  • Output shape follows audience, purpose, and the next step; there is no universally best format.
  • Plain-language rewrites should keep numbers, units, conditions, and uncertainty, even at the cost of extra words.
  • Executives want decisions and risk, peers want method and limitations, and customers want reassurance and an action.
  • Register changes packaging; meaning changes the claim. The restatement test tells them apart.
  • Refinement works best as a comparison of two candidates against a rubric with correctness as a gate.
  • Format follows the downstream task: tables for scanning, lists for action, prose for narrative, structured data for import.
  • A schema fixes field names, order, types, allowed values, and missing-value behavior.
  • Length should be specified as a number with a unit, with a decision about what to cut under pressure.
  • Requesting versions with and without jargon exposes lost meaning and false precision.
  • Verify before polishing, and verify again after rewriting; fluency is not evidence of truth.

Checkpoint questions

  1. An analyst must send one finding to a sponsor who needs a go or no-go decision in under a minute. Which opening is most appropriate? - A. A description of the sampling method, followed by the raw figures and a statistical test. - B. A direct recommendation, the one reason that drives it, and the main caveat. - C. A full history of how the data was collected, ending with the recommendation. - D. A structured JSON record with field names and types.

  2. Which rewrite preserves meaning while using plain language? - A. "Errors rose from 4 in 100 to 7 in 100 in the three sampled regions" from "Error rate increased from 4% to 7% across the three sampled regions." - B. "Errors tripled" from "Error rate increased from 4% to 7%." - C. "Errors rose substantially nationwide" from "Error rate increased across the three sampled regions." - D. "The change was caused by packaging" from "The change was associated with packaging."

  3. A technical sentence reads: "The result may indicate a problem, pending review." Which revision changes meaning rather than register? - A. "This could point to a problem, subject to review." - B. "The result suggests a possible issue, awaiting confirmation." - C. "The result proves there is a problem." - D. "There may be an issue, which we have not yet confirmed."

  4. When refining two candidate answers with a rubric, what should be treated as a gate before style is scored? - A. The length of each candidate. - B. Factual correctness against the source. - C. The friendliness of the tone. - D. The number of bullets used.

  5. A downstream program will parse the answer and compare it across many runs. What is the most important requirement? - A. A vivid narrative that explains the background. - B. A table with shaded headers for readability. - C. Fixed field names, types, order, and explicit missing-value handling. - D. A conversational register with short sentences.

  6. A request says the output must be "short." What is the main problem with that instruction? - A. It is too polite for a formal request. - B. It gives no number or unit, so the bound is ambiguous. - C. It prevents the use of a table. - D. It requires the assistant to invent field names.

  7. A response reads beautifully but cites a figure that does not appear in the source material. What is the correct next action? - A. Improve the phrasing further so the figure reads more convincingly. - B. Accept it, because fluency indicates the assistant verified the fact. - C. Replace it with a plausible estimate and keep the structure. - D. Reject the response, return to the source, and request a corrected answer with the figure supplied.

Answers and rationale

  1. Correct: B. A sponsor making a fast decision needs the recommendation first, one supporting reason, and the caveat so the risk is visible. A is wrong because method and statistics delay the decision point. C is wrong because a full history buries the answer under background. D is wrong because structured data suits a machine consumer, not a time-pressured human reader.

  2. Correct: A. It keeps the baseline, the new value, the unit, and the scope while replacing a percentage phrase with a count out of 100. B is wrong because 4 to 7 percent is not a tripling. C is wrong because it replaces "three sampled regions" with "nationwide," changing scope. D is wrong because "associated with" does not justify "caused by."

  3. Correct: C. "Proves" upgrades a cautious, conditional claim into a certainty, so meaning changes. A, B, and D preserve the possibility and the pending confirmation while changing only word choice and formality; they are register changes.

  4. Correct: B. Correctness is a gate because a factually wrong answer fails regardless of its style. A, C, and D are scoring dimensions that matter after the gate, but none of them can compensate for an error in the underlying claims.

  5. Correct: C. Automated comparison across runs depends on a stable contract: exact names, types, order, and defined handling of missing values. A and D are prose qualities that do not help a parser. B improves human readability but does not make the structure predictable.

  6. Correct: B. "Short" has no measurable bound, so different readers and systems will interpret it differently. A is irrelevant to the instruction's clarity. C is false because a short request can still require a table. D is false because field names depend on a schema request, not on a length request.

  7. Correct: D. An unsupported figure is a factual failure, and polish cannot repair it; the answer must be rebuilt from verified source material. A is wrong because better wording hides the error. B is wrong because fluency is not verification. C is wrong because inventing an estimate repeats the original failure.

End of chapter 8.

Choosing a model looks trivial until you must defend the choice. In a casual chat it hardly registers; in a work setting it becomes a design decision with consequences. A model that is too small may mishandle the ambiguous parts of a task; one that is too large may cost more, answer more slowly, and still produce the same result as a cheaper option. The foundations-level exam treats model selection as a reasoning exercise, not a recall exercise.

The aim is a way of thinking that works even when the names are unfamiliar. Capability tiers, workload signals, latency, cost, and risk are the durable concepts; brand names are the wrapping. By the end you should read a scenario, name the deciding constraint, and justify a tier choice without recognizing a product.

Learning objectives

  • Describe the generic capability tiers — compact, balanced, and capable — and the workload signals that favor each.
  • Recognize high-volume classification and extraction as compact-tier candidates, and multi-source analysis and ambiguous judgment as capable-tier candidates.
  • Explain why the cost of a wrong answer is a primary selection criterion, not an afterthought.
  • Treat latency and the waiting user as real constraints on a choice.
  • Explain what extended thinking or longer reasoning buys, and when the extra cost is justified.
  • Distinguish choosing one model globally from routing workflow steps to different tiers.
  • Compute and interpret cost per successful task instead of focusing on headline prices.
  • Reason from a scenario's constraints rather than brand recall when answering exam questions.

Key concepts

Model selection is a design decision, not a preference

It is tempting to reach for the most capable model for everything, the way one might always drive the largest vehicle in the lot. That instinct is expensive and often fails to improve the outcome. A model choice is better understood as a match between the shape of the work and the shape of the tool. The question stops being "which model is best" and becomes "which model is good enough for this task, under these constraints, at this price, with this level of acceptable risk."

That reframe matters because scenario questions contain a deciding constraint. One question may hinge on thousands of simple items processed cheaply; another on a single mistaken conclusion that triggers an expensive review. Both might involve the same assistant but point to opposite ends of the range. A candidate who memorized a product hierarchy has to guess; one who identified the constraint can reason.

Three ideas do most of the work: different tasks need different amounts of reasoning; each extra unit of capability costs time or money, so spend it where it changes the outcome; and the harm an error causes sets the floor for how much care a task deserves. Nearly every recommendation below applies one of these.

Capability tiers before brand names

Before any product name enters the conversation, speak in generic tiers. These are not official categories and do not map neatly to a vendor's catalogue; they are a reasoning vocabulary.

The compact tier covers smaller, faster models built for well-bounded tasks. They handle clear instructions, short outputs, and repeating patterns, and excel when a task leaves little room for interpretation. Their weakness shows up when a request is vague, several sources must be reconciled, or a lot of context must be held at once.

The balanced tier sits in the middle. It handles broad everyday knowledge work: drafting, summarizing, routine analysis, and questions needing some judgment but not heroic reasoning. For many teams it is the sensible default, a compromise between speed, cost, and competence.

The capable tier contains the largest, most reasoning-intensive models. These are the tools for genuinely hard tasks: conflicting evidence, subtle trade-offs, multi-step inference, or a decision where a shallow answer would be costly. They are slower and more expensive, which is acceptable because the task is high-value or high-risk. Using them on trivial work is like assigning a senior specialist to sort the mail.

Exam note: When a question asks you to select a model, translate the scenario into one of these tiers before looking at the options. The choice matching the tier implied by the constraints is usually correct, even if the model is unfamiliar.

Reading the workload: signals that point to a tier

Tiers are useful only if you can tell which one a task calls for. That means reading the workload for signals — any detail indicating how much reasoning, speed, or accuracy is needed.

Volume is one signal. If the same operation must be performed on thousands of items — sorting messages into categories, pulling a date and amount from forms — per-item cost and response time dominate, pushing toward compact. Ambiguity is another. If a small number of items must be weighed with conflicting signals and no clean combining rule, reasoning quality dominates and capable becomes attractive.

Structure matters too. A task with fixed output format and narrow input domain is predictable, and compact models excel at predictable work. One whose shape only becomes clear partway through — where the assistant must notice that two documents disagree and decide which to trust — rewards a stronger model. Consider consequence last: if a wrong answer is cheap to detect and fix, a lighter model and lighter review suffice; if it flows into a customer-facing document or regulated filing, they do not.

No single signal decides alone; ask which would change your mind. High-volume but high-stakes work should not be forced to the cheapest tier — the stakes override volume. A result reviewed line by line by a specialist needs less raw reasoning from the model. This weighting is what scenarios probe.

High-volume classification and extraction: the compact tier's home turf

Two task types recur in workflows and on the exam: classification and extraction.

Classification assigns an item to a category — routing messages to teams, labeling a ticket by urgency. The possible answers are limited, the input defined, and the correct label often checkable afterward. Because the output is small and the boundary usually clear, these are excellent compact-tier candidates. Running thousands through a capable model spends money without buying much.

Extraction pulls specific fields out of unstructured text. Given a contract, return the parties, effective date, and renewal term; given an invoice, return vendor, total, and tax. Extraction is harder than classification because values must be reproduced exactly and may be buried in noise, but it is still bounded: the fields are known in advance and no opinion is requested. A compact or balanced model handles routine extraction well when formats are consistent and fields named explicitly.

Both can be harder than they look: categories overlap and extraction may need interpretation of an ambiguous phrase. A compact-tier deployment should detect hard cases, having the compact model handle the bulk and flag low-confidence items for a second pass by a stronger model or reviewer. That is routing in miniature: average cost stays low without ignoring the difficult tail.

Common mistake: Assuming a "simple" task needs no verification. High-volume tasks fail in volume: a one-percent error rate across ten thousand items is a hundred errors. Budget for sampling even when each call is cheap.

Multi-source analysis and ambiguous judgment: when you need the capable tier

At the other end sit tasks where difficulty lies in the thinking. Multi-source analysis asks the assistant to read several documents, see how they relate, and produce a conclusion accounting for all of them. Sources may disagree, use different terminology, or cover different periods. The assistant must decide what is comparable, what is outdated, and what to do with contradictions. This is where a compact model most often produces a smooth but shallow answer that misses a conflict.

Ambiguous judgment is similar. These are tasks that cannot be reduced to a rule: deciding whether a borderline case meets a policy's intent, weighing several reasonable options, or interpreting an open-ended instruction. They require holding competing considerations in mind and reasoning about trade-offs. Capable models are more likely to surface tension explicitly, qualify a conclusion, and avoid the overconfident simplification a smaller model might offer.

The reason to pay for this tier is not eloquence but a better chance of getting the reasoning right. When output drives an expensive-to-reverse decision, the incremental price of stronger reasoning is small compared with a wrong turn. That is the cost-of-a-wrong-answer principle, the most reliable justification for capable.

There is a boundary. A capable model does not make a task verifiable. If facts are missing or sources unreliable, more reasoning cannot manufacture truth. The response is to gather evidence or ask the assistant to flag what it cannot determine — not to upgrade the model and hope. On the exam, an option offering a stronger model as the fix for unverifiable information is usually a distractor.

The cost of a wrong answer as a selection criterion

If one idea should carry into every question, it is this: the cost of a wrong answer is a first-class selection criterion. Prices are easy to compare because they are numbers on a page; error costs are harder because they are entangled with context, but they matter more.

Distinguish the harm of the error from the cost of detecting and correcting it. A typo in an internal note may cost nothing; a fabricated citation in a customer report may damage credibility and require a public correction. The same error has wildly different consequences. Price the downstream harm, then choose a tier and review process whose combined cost is less than the harm prevented.

This yields a symmetry: the more expensive the error, the more to spend on both capability and verification. High-stakes tasks justify a capable model because better reasoning lowers the chance of a serious mistake, but also human review because no model is infallible. Cheap, reversible tasks justify a compact model and light sampling, because spending more would not pay.

The exam surrounds a simple-looking task with a consequence. Generating short product descriptions looks like compact-tier drafting — until you notice they will be published without review across a large catalogue. Now error cost includes brand reputation, and the answer shifts toward a stronger model plus approval. The surface task did not change; the constraint did.

Latency and the person waiting on the other side

Cost is not the only resource a model consumes. In interactive settings latency — the delay from request to response — is often more visible. A capable model that reasons at length may take noticeably longer than a compact one, changing the experience of the person waiting.

Consider where the assistant sits. Embedded in a live customer interaction, every extra second is felt and may affect satisfaction or abandonment. There, a fast compact model answering adequately can beat a slower capable model answering slightly better, especially for routine questions. Used asynchronously — a report prepared overnight — latency matters far less, so capable-tier quality is affordable.

Latency also interacts with iteration. Quick responses let a worker try several phrasings and refine in a tight loop; slow ones make each iteration costly, so people iterate less and settle. That is a hidden cost of using a large model for exploratory work. A productive pattern is to explore fast, then run the final version through a stronger model.

Extended thinking and longer reasoning as a paid option

Many model families offer a mode that lets the assistant spend more computation reasoning before answering — extended thinking or longer reasoning. It is not a different model so much as a setting, and the extra work is typically billed as additional usage.

The payoff is specific, not general. Longer reasoning helps on problems with multiple steps, where an early wrong turn derails everything, and when the assistant must check assumptions, reconcile conflicting evidence, or plan before writing. It helps less on recall, formatting, or simple transformation, where there is no hidden depth. Paying for it there is like paying for a longer runway at an airfield that only handles short hops.

Treat it as a paid option with a specific payoff. Ask what the reasoning is expected to change. "It might be better" is weak; "it should catch the inconsistency between two sources a quick pass would miss" is concrete. On the exam, extended thinking is correct when the scenario stresses multi-step reasoning, conflicting information, or high error cost, and wrong when the task is high-volume, simple, or latency-sensitive.

Extended reasoning is not accuracy; it can produce a thorough explanation of a wrong premise. It helps when the task rewards careful thought and inputs are sound, but cannot substitute for good data or verification. Offering it as the fix for a fabricated source is wrong.

Model families and the small, medium, and large pattern

The discussion has stayed generic, which mirrors the exam. In practice a provider often releases a family: small, medium, and large variants built on similar design but tuned for different points on the speed, cost, and capability curve. They usually share an interface, making it easy to swap one for another in the same workflow.

The small variant favors speed and economy, the natural choice for high-volume bounded tasks. The medium variant aims for the middle ground and often becomes the workhorse. The large variant targets the hardest tasks, offering deepest reasoning at the highest price and greatest latency. Some families also include specialized variants, such as one tuned for long inputs, but the basic pattern suffices.

You are not expected to memorize which variant belongs to which family or has the longest context window. Map a scenario to a tier, then select the option whose stated characteristics match. A question might describe four unnamed models and ask which suits a task; the answer is the one whose trade-offs — fast and economical, or slow and deeply reasoning — align with the workload signals and error costs. Brand recognition is a shortcut at best and a trap at worst, because names change while the reasoning stays constant.

Exam note: If a question hinges on a detail you could only know by memorizing a specification, re-read it. Foundations-level questions are answerable from the scenario; the deciding fact is stated in the situation, not hidden in a catalogue.

Routing between tiers rather than one global choice

A common mistake is picking one model for an entire application. That "one global choice" is convenient but wasteful, because a workflow is a chain of different kinds of work with different needs. A single compromise is rarely optimal for any part.

Routing sends different steps to different tiers. A document pipeline might use a compact model to classify items, a balanced model to extract fields, and a capable model only for the small fraction where sources conflict. A drafting workflow might use a fast model for several rough options and a stronger one to refine the chosen option. The result is better average quality at lower average cost.

Routing works best when handoff criteria are explicit. "Send hard cases to the stronger model" is too vague. "Escalate when extracted fields disagree, when confidence is below a threshold, or when the item matches a flagged category" is testable: you can measure how often escalation triggers and whether it improves outcomes.

The cost is complexity: something must decide where each item goes and pass context forward. For a small, low-volume workflow that may not be worth it; one balanced model suffices. Routing earns its keep when volume is high, tasks heterogeneous, or price differences large enough that savings outweigh the machinery.

Measuring cost per successful task

Headline prices are seductive because they are simple. But a cheap model that fails often can cost more than an expensive one that succeeds reliably. A practical process measures cost per successful task rather than price per call.

Define success for the task — a correctly labeled item, an extracted field that passes a check, a summary accepted without revision. Over a representative sample, count how many attempts each model needs and add rework cost. Divide total spend by successes. This captures price and reliability together, and often reverses the ranking from sticker prices alone.

A compact model succeeding on ninety percent of routine items, with quick correction, keeps cost per success low. Another cheap model failing on thirty percent of items whose errors are hard to spot triggers full review each time; its cost per success may exceed a capable model working first time. The price per call did not change — the error cost did.

The metric also quantifies routing: if most items are handled cheaply and a few escalate, blended cost per success can be below either tier alone. That lets you defend choosing neither the cheapest nor the best model — you minimized the cost of a correct result.

cost per successful task =
    (cost of all model calls + cost of review and rework)
    / (number of tasks that met the success definition)

Where:
  cost of model calls   = calls x price per call
  cost of review/rework = reviewer time + any repeat generation
  successes             = tasks accepted without correction

The numerator includes human time. A "free" model generating output needing extensive checking is not free. Including review cost keeps the calculation honest and aligned with the cost-of-a-wrong-answer principle.

A decision matrix for model selection

The matrix maps workload signals to a likely tier and the main reason, expressed in task characteristics rather than product names.

Workload signal Likely tier Primary reason
Thousands of items with fixed labels Compact Per-item cost and speed dominate; little reasoning needed
Routine extraction from consistent documents Compact to balanced Bounded output; format reliability matters more than depth
Everyday drafting, summarizing, light analysis Balanced Broad competence at moderate cost and latency
Reconciling several sources that disagree Capable Must detect conflicts and weigh conflicting evidence
Ambiguous judgment with expensive consequences Capable Reasoning quality lowers the chance of a costly error
Live interaction where waiting is visible Compact to balanced Low latency protects the user experience
Background or asynchronous batch work Capable if needed Latency is cheap, so quality can take priority
Errors are hard to detect Capable plus review Better reasoning and verification both justified
Errors are cheap and obvious Compact Spending more would not pay for itself
Mixed workflow with easy and hard cases Routed tiers Match each step to the minimum capability it needs

Read this as a starting point, not a rulebook. When two rows conflict, the deciding factor is usually the cost of a wrong answer — it outranks convenience and often volume. A high-volume, high-consequence task deserves routing that keeps average cost low while protecting the difficult tail.

Illustrative cost comparison

The table uses invented figures for a batch of one thousand items, not real prices, to show how cost-per-success works.

Option Price per 1,000 calls First-pass success Review cost per failure Effective cost per success
Compact, no review 20 units 88 percent 5 units About 28 units
Compact with strong-model escalation 20 plus 12 97 percent 5 units About 33 units
Balanced 80 units 95 percent 8 units About 88 units
Capable throughout 300 units 99 percent 10 units About 303 units
Extended reasoning throughout 700 units 99.3 percent 10 units About 705 units

The compact model has the lowest raw price but a failure tail; cost per success rises once review is included. Escalation improves reliability at modest cost and may be best when errors are costly. The capable model is far more expensive per success, justified only when harm avoided exceeds the difference. Extended reasoning on every item is rarely sensible; its payoff belongs on the subset where deeper thought changes the answer.

Common mistake: Comparing only the price-per-call column rewards the cheapest model regardless of whether it works. The last column reflects reality because it folds in reliability and rework. An answer based purely on lowest price is usually incomplete.

What the exam is really asking: reasoning from the scenario

Model-selection questions are not tests of product knowledge but of whether you can find the deciding constraint. It is usually one of four things: the volume and repetitiveness of work, the ambiguity or reasoning depth demanded, the latency allowed, or the cost of getting it wrong.

A reliable approach has four moves: identify the task type and volume; the consequence of an error and how detectable it is; any stated latency or budget constraint; then choose the smallest adequate tier, escalating only where justified. That last move captures the exam's preference for proportionate choices — over-engineering is as wrong as under-engineering.

Watch for distractors solving the wrong problem. If the difficulty is missing source documents, a larger model is not the answer. If it is a customer waiting in real time, extended reasoning on every turn is not the answer. If it is uniform high-volume extraction, a capable model throughout is unlikely. Each fixes a different axis from the one causing trouble.

Selection is only one control among several: prompt quality, grounding, and human review all shape the outcome, and a strong model with a vague prompt can underperform a balanced model with a clear one. An option combining a tier choice with a better prompt or review step is often most complete.

Worked scenario

A regional services organization receives a steady stream of customer messages. A small team reads each, assigns it to one of six departments, and extracts the account reference and service date. The team lead wants to reduce manual load. Two constraints are stated: volume is around two thousand messages per week, and a misrouted message can sit unattended for days, delaying service. Most messages are short and clear, but a small share mention two services or phrase the date in a way needing interpretation.

First, classify the work. The core operations — classification and extraction — are bounded tasks with known outputs, strongly signaling compact tier, because per-item price and response time matter at two thousand a week. If the brief ended there, the answer would be a compact model with periodic sampling for drift.

Second, weigh the consequence. A misrouted message causes real delay, so errors are not trivial — but they are detectable: a department can spot a wrong route, and a suspect date can be checked against the original text. Because errors are visible rather than silent, the design can rely on a lightweight correction path rather than the most expensive model for everything.

Third, handle the ambiguous tail. Messages mentioning two services or unusual date phrasing are exactly where a compact model is most likely to guess. The design is routing: compact processes the clear majority, and any message matching an ambiguity rule (two service names detected, no date found, or confidence below a threshold) escalates to a capable model. A human reviews a random sample of the rest.

Fourth, define success and measure. Success is a message routed correctly with accurate reference and date, accepted without correction. Over a trial batch of a few hundred, the team records how many items each path handles, how often escalation triggers, and how many errors slip through, then computes cost per successful task for three options. Routed should win because it spends cheap processing on the easy majority and reserves stronger reasoning for the difficult minority.

The resolution is neither "pick the best model" nor "pick the cheapest." It is a routed design with explicit escalation rules, light human sampling, and a measurement plan. Extended reasoning on every message ignores volume and latency; a capable model for all ignores the bounded, repetitive nature of most work; a compact model with no checks ignores the cost of misrouting. Only the routed design accounts for every stated constraint.

Exam note: When a scenario contains both a high-volume and a high-consequence signal, look for the answer satisfying both rather than picking a side. Routing and selective escalation are the usual way.

Common mistakes

  • Choosing the most capable model for every task, ignoring cost and latency without improving bounded work.
  • Choosing the cheapest model on price per call alone, without considering failure rates and rework.
  • Treating accuracy as a property of the model alone, when prompts, grounding, and review also determine the result.
  • Recommending a larger model to fix a problem caused by missing or unreliable source material.
  • Ignoring the cost of a wrong answer, often the deciding constraint in a scenario.
  • Overlooking latency when a person is waiting in a live interaction.
  • Using extended reasoning on simple, high-volume, or latency-sensitive tasks where it adds cost without changing the outcome.
  • Applying one model globally to a heterogeneous workflow instead of routing steps to appropriate tiers.
  • Escalating vaguely ("send hard ones to the stronger model") without explicit, testable criteria.
  • Assuming product names matter on the exam, when questions are answerable from workload signals and constraints.

Chapter summary

  • Capability tiers — compact, balanced, capable — are a reasoning vocabulary that precedes any brand name.
  • Compact models suit high-volume classification and bounded extraction; capable models suit multi-source analysis and ambiguous judgment with costly consequences.
  • The cost of a wrong answer is a first-class criterion and usually the deciding one in scenario questions.
  • Latency is a real constraint: live interactions favor faster tiers, background work can afford deeper reasoning.
  • Extended thinking is a paid option with a specific payoff on multi-step or conflict-heavy problems, not a general accuracy upgrade.
  • Model families commonly offer small, medium, and large variants, but the exam asks you to reason from scenario characteristics, not recall specifications.
  • Routing different steps to different tiers beats a single global choice when volume is high and tasks heterogeneous.
  • Cost per successful task, including rework and review, is a better metric than price per call.
  • The smallest adequate tier plus proportionate verification is the sound default.

Checkpoint questions

  1. An analyst must assign forty thousand short messages to one of five fixed categories weekly. Labels are well defined and errors easy to spot later. Which tier is the most appropriate starting point, and why?

  2. A team must reconcile three internal reports disagreeing on a key figure before publishing a summary to leadership. A wrong conclusion would be costly to reverse. Which tier and additional control do you recommend, and why?

  3. A customer-facing chat answers routine questions in real time; customers abandon if responses are slow. Why might a compact or balanced model be preferable to a capable one here?

  4. A proposal enables extended thinking on every request in a high-volume extraction pipeline because "more reasoning means more accuracy." What is wrong with this, and when is extended thinking justified?

  5. A model family offers small, medium, and large variants. An exam question names four unfamiliar models and asks which suits a task with conflicting sources and expensive errors. How should a candidate reason to the answer?

  6. Model A costs less per call but fails on a fifth of tasks, each failure needing a full manual redo. Model B costs more but rarely fails. Which measure should decide between them, and how is it calculated?

  7. A workflow has three steps: classifying items, extracting standard fields, and resolving the small share where sources conflict. Why is a routed design likely to outperform a single model throughout?

Answers and rationale

  1. Compact tier. The workload is high-volume, repetitive, bounded, and easy to verify — classic classification. Compact keeps per-item cost and latency low. Balanced works but spends more without changing the outcome; capable is unjustified given little reasoning depth and detectable errors; "no verification needed" is wrong because volume multiplies errors.

  2. Capable tier plus human review. Conflicting sources require detecting and reasoning about disagreement, and the costly consequence raises required care. Review is still needed because a model cannot verify facts the sources do not settle. Compact may smooth over the conflict; a larger model alone confuses capability with verification; extended thinking alone misses the independent check.

  3. Latency dominates in a live interaction. A faster model answering adequately protects the experience and keeps iteration cheap; the slightly better answer does not compensate for visible waiting on routine questions. Capable throughout ignores latency; extended reasoning on every turn adds delay; "latency does not matter for quality" is false interactively.

  4. It adds cost without addressing the task. Routine extraction has little hidden depth, so extended thinking rarely changes the answer and raises price and latency. It is justified on the subset where multi-step reasoning or conflicting evidence affects the result. "More reasoning always means more accuracy" is false; "use it for escalated hard cases" is correct; "drop verification because reasoning is deeper" is wrong because reasoning is not verification.

  5. Map the scenario to a tier, then match stated trade-offs. Conflicting sources and expensive errors point to capable, so select the option described as slowest, most expensive, and deepest in reasoning. Picking a familiar name is unreliable; choosing the largest number ignores stated characteristics; assuming one variant does everything equally well contradicts the tier pattern.

  6. Cost per successful task: total model cost plus review and rework, divided by tasks meeting the success definition. This folds reliability into the comparison. Price per call ignores failures; "whichever is faster" ignores error cost; "always pick the reliable one" is incomplete because the calculation may still favor the cheaper model if its failures are cheap to fix.

  7. Routing matches each step to the minimum capability needed. Classification and extraction are bounded and high-volume, so compact or balanced handling is economical; only conflicting-source cases need stronger reasoning, and escalating just those keeps average cost low while protecting the risky tail. Capable for all overspends on easy work; compact for all risks ambiguous cases; "one model is always simpler and better" ignores the volume and cost differences that justify routing.

End of chapter 9.

A conversation with an AI assistant feels unlimited until it is not. Early on, the assistant seems to remember every detail and apply every instruction. After a long exchange, the same assistant may forget a decision made at the start, drop a formatting rule, or contradict an earlier definition. Nothing mysterious has happened. The session has simply outgrown the space available to hold it.

This chapter explains that space, called the context window, and the discipline of working within it. You will learn what fills the window, how to recognize that it is nearly full, and the three recovery strategies that keep long work coherent. You will also learn how memory features differ from an organized external record, what temporary modes do and do not promise, and how to carry preferences and decisions between chats. These are foundational exam topics because they sit at the boundary between what the model can do and what the user must manage.

Learning objectives

By the end of this chapter you should be able to:

  • Describe the context window as a single shared budget for instructions, conversation history, documents, tool output, and the answer itself.
  • Identify the practical signs that a conversation is approaching its context limit.
  • Explain why early decisions and instructions tend to get lost in long conversations.
  • Compare the restart, summarize, and persist recovery strategies and state when each is correct.
  • Compose a handoff summary that preserves the facts needed to continue work without the original thread.
  • Distinguish built-in memory features from a governed external record of key facts.
  • State what incognito or temporary modes do and do not guarantee.
  • Apply a continuity checklist to keep preferences, decisions, and obligations intact across sessions.

Key concepts

The context window as one shared budget

The context window is the finite amount of text that the model can consider while producing any single response. It is helpful to picture it as one desk surface with limited room. Everything relevant to the current reply competes for space on that desk: the system and developer instructions, the user's standing preferences, the entire visible conversation history, any documents pasted or attached, the output returned by tools, and the answer being written. These items are not stored in separate compartments. They share one budget.

This sharing has an important consequence. Space spent on one item is space unavailable for another. A long pasted report leaves less room for the conversation that follows it; a verbose tool output can crowd out the reasoning the task needs. Because the answer is generated within the same window, a request that consumes almost all the available space leaves too little room for a complete reply, and the response may be cut short or lose its ending.

It is tempting to think of the context window as a storage device that remembers the session. It is more accurate to think of it as working memory reassembled for each turn. On every new message, the relevant text is gathered again. Older material may be compressed or dropped when the total grows too large. The assistant has a window into a selected portion of the session, not a perfect permanent transcript.

Key idea: Context is a budget to be allocated, not a container that holds everything. Manage it the way you would manage a limited supply: spend it where it changes the answer and reclaim it where it does not.

What consumes the budget

Different kinds of content draw on the same budget in different ways. Understanding the categories helps you see where waste accumulates and where it is worth spending.

Category What it includes Typical behavior What the user can control
Standing instructions System rules, developer rules, saved preferences Present on every turn; usually highest priority Phrase preferences clearly; avoid duplicating them
Conversation history All prior user and assistant turns in the session Grows with every exchange; oldest parts may be compressed or dropped Remove tangents, close settled topics, start fresh when useful
Documents and pasted content Reports, tables, code, transcripts, reference text Enters the window in full when attached or pasted Supply only the relevant excerpt; summarize large sources first
Tool output Search results, file reads, query results, generated artifacts Can be large and verbose; accumulates across steps Request narrow results; discard raw dumps after extracting facts
The answer The response being generated Needs room reserved in advance Ask for concise output; reserve headroom for the final reply

Two patterns waste the most budget. The first is carrying raw material forward after its useful facts have been extracted; keeping a long source in the thread consumes space on every later turn. The second is repetition. Restating the same background each message, or requesting a full answer when only a correction is needed, adds cost without adding information.

Position matters too. Material at the very start of a long conversation is the most likely to be compressed away, and material in the middle of a long block can receive less attention than material near the beginning or end. This is not a precise rule, but it explains why a constraint buried in paragraph forty of a huge paste may be honored less reliably than one restated briefly at the point of use.

Exam note: A common exam trap is to treat the prompt you type as the only thing in the window. The instructions, the whole history, the documents, the tool results, and the answer all share the same space. If a scenario describes an answer that trails off or loses its conclusion, suspect that too much input left too little room for output.

Signs that a conversation is approaching its limit

There is no universally reliable counter a user can read. Instead, watch for behavioral signals that, taken together, are a strong hint.

  • Repeated clarification of settled facts. Assistant replies begin asking for information that was provided earlier, or they use an outdated version of a value that was later corrected.
  • Lost instructions. A formatting rule, tone requirement, or definition given at the start stops being followed even though it was never withdrawn.
  • Contradictions within a reply. The answer uses two incompatible definitions of the same term, or applies a rule one way in the first half and another way in the second half.
  • Drift toward generic output. Responses become shorter, vaguer, or more template-like, with fewer references to the specific details of the task.
  • Premature conclusions or truncated endings. The reply stops mid-thought, omits the requested summary, or fails to complete a list, especially when the input was very large.
  • Ignored recent corrections. A change you just requested is acknowledged but not reflected, suggesting earlier text is outweighing the newest instruction.
  • Degradation after a large attachment. Quality drops sharply immediately after a big document or a verbose tool result enters the thread.

When several of these appear, the right response is not to keep pushing harder with longer prompts. The right response is to change strategy: reduce what must be held in the window at once.

Common mistake: Assuming the assistant is malfunctioning and simply restating the request at greater length. Longer restatements add more text to an already crowded window and usually make the problem worse.

Why early decisions get lost in long conversations

Long projects accumulate decisions. A team agrees on a name, a scope boundary, or an approval rule in the first few messages. Dozens of exchanges later, the assistant may behave as though those decisions never existed, for several reasons.

First, as text volume grows, earlier turns must be compressed or removed to keep recent ones, and compression preserves the gist rather than the exact wording of a constraint. A nuance such as "exclude draft records from this count" can disappear while the general topic "records and counts" survives.

Second, later messages can overshadow earlier ones. A casual question in message thirty may read as revisiting a decision fixed in message three, especially without an explicit "keeping the earlier decision in place." Third, corrections stack: if a value changes three times, the thread holds four competing versions, and an older one can resurface.

The lesson is that important decisions should be restated where they matter and recorded somewhere that does not depend on the window. A short standing-decisions note, refreshed as the project evolves, is more reliable than the original conversation.

Recovery strategy one: restart with a distilled brief

Restarting means opening a new session and supplying a compact, self-contained description of the task and its current state. This is correct when the old conversation has accumulated noise, the topic has changed, or the thread is too degraded for reliable summarization.

A good brief states the goal, fixed decisions, work completed, open questions, constraints, and the exact next action. It should be short enough to read in a minute. Because the user writes it, its accuracy does not depend on the assistant having understood earlier turns.

The strength of restarting is cleanliness: the new session starts with high-value context and no clutter. The limitation is omission. If a crucial detail was never written down, abandoning the old thread loses it. Restart works best when key facts are already captured externally or the task is simple enough to restate completely.

Try it: Before closing a thread, write a five-line brief: goal, fixed decisions, work done, open issues, next step. If you cannot fill those lines from memory, the thread still holds information you need, and you should extract it before restarting.

Recovery strategy two: summarize the thread

Summarizing means asking the assistant for a condensed account of the conversation, then continuing from it. This is correct when the thread is long but coherent, the details are spread across many turns, and you want continuity without reconstructing the background yourself.

A useful summary captures decisions, constraints, definitions, deliverables, and outstanding items, and is explicit about what is settled versus open. It should preserve exact values such as dates, file names, numeric targets, and agreed wording, because approximate summaries blur precisely the details that cause errors later.

There is a subtle risk. If the assistant has already dropped a detail from its window, it cannot summarize it; a summary reflects what the model can currently see. For high-stakes work, review it against your own notes before relying on it. Also, a summary that merely restates the thread in slightly shorter form frees little space. It must be genuinely condensed.

Produced near the end of a session, a summary can double as the handoff artifact for the next one, making summarization a natural habit at milestones.

Recovery strategy three: persist key facts externally

Persisting means recording important facts outside the conversation: a project note, a shared document, a checklist, or a knowledge base. This is correct when work spans sessions, people, or tools, or when facts must be auditable or corrected in one place.

External persistence is the most durable strategy because it does not depend on the window. A written decision can be read fresh into any new session and can be reviewed, versioned, and approved. The tradeoff is effort: someone must maintain the record and keep it current. A stale record can be as misleading as a lost conversation.

The most effective approach combines all three: keep a compact external record, use summarization to close out a productive session, and restart from a brief that points to the record. They are complements, not rivals.

Choosing among the three strategies

The following comparison brings the decision into focus. Use the row that matches the situation rather than defaulting to one habit for every case.

Strategy Best used when How it works Main strength Main limitation
Restart with a distilled brief The thread is noisy, the topic has shifted, or quality has degraded badly Open a new session and write a compact, self-contained statement of goal, decisions, and next step Clean context with no accumulated clutter Detail can be lost if it was never recorded
Summarize the thread The thread is long but still coherent and the details are spread across turns Ask for a condensed account of decisions, constraints, deliverables, and open items, then continue Preserves distributed detail without manual reconstruction Cannot recover facts already dropped from the window; weak summaries save little space
Persist key facts externally Work spans sessions, people, or tools, or facts must be auditable Record stable facts in an external note, document, or knowledge base and read them into new sessions Durable, reviewable, independent of any single chat Requires ongoing maintenance and ownership

Exam note: If a scenario emphasizes that work must continue next week with a different colleague or system, favor persistence. If it emphasizes a single messy thread that is already producing contradictory answers, favor restarting. If it emphasizes a long but healthy thread that simply needs compaction, favor summarizing.

What belongs in a handoff summary

A handoff summary is a compact record that lets work continue without the original conversation. It is the bridge between sessions and the product of all three recovery strategies. It should answer the questions a capable newcomer would ask: What are we trying to do? What have we decided? What is finished? What is still open? What should happen next?

Include the goal, fixed decisions, constraints and definitions, deliverables produced, open questions or blockers, and a clear next action. Preserve exact values, mark uncertainty as uncertainty rather than guessing, and keep the document short enough to actually be pasted into a new session.

The template below can be copied and filled in. Placeholders are shown in brackets and should be replaced with the specific facts of the task.

HANDOFF SUMMARY

Goal:
[One or two sentences describing the outcome the work is meant to produce.]

Fixed decisions:
- [Decision 1, stated as a rule that still stands.]
- [Decision 2, including any version, date, or value that matters.]

Definitions and constraints:
- [Term] = [agreed meaning]
- [Constraint, tone rule, format rule, or exclusion that must be honored]

Work completed:
- [Deliverable or milestone finished, with its location if stored externally]
- [Deliverable or milestone finished]

Open items and blockers:
- [Question not yet answered, with who or what is needed to answer it]
- [Known risk or dependency]

Sources and references:
- [Document, dataset, or note that should be consulted, and where it lives]

Next action:
[The single next step, stated specifically enough to begin without further briefing.]

Write the summary while the work is still fresh, and separate settled facts from open questions visibly, because a reader who cannot tell the difference may reopen a closed decision or treat a guess as an agreement.

Memory features: convenience versus governed data store

Many assistants offer a memory feature that automatically retains facts about the user or the work and reuses them later. This is convenient: it can remember a preferred format or a standing constraint, reducing repeated setup.

Convenience is not governance. An automatic memory feature is designed to help the assistant sound consistent, not necessarily to be a system of record. Its facts may be inferred rather than confirmed, updated without a clear audit trail, and hard to inspect, correct, or export in full. The user may not know exactly what was retained.

A governed external record is explicit, reviewed, owned, and versioned, with deliberate corrections and policy-limited access. When a fact must be accurate, traceable, or shared, the governed record is the source of truth, with automatic memory treated only as a convenience layer.

Dimension Automatic memory feature Governed external record
How facts enter Often inferred from conversation Written and reviewed deliberately
Visibility May be only partly visible to the user Explicit and inspectable
Correction Quick, but may lack an audit trail Deliberate, versioned, and reviewable
Best use Preferences and low-risk continuity Decisions, definitions, and anything requiring accuracy
Risk Confident reuse of a wrong or outdated fact Staleness if nobody maintains it

Common mistake: Treating an assistant's confident reference to a "remembered" preference as proof that the fact is correct and current. Verify important facts against the record you control.

Incognito and temporary modes

Some interfaces provide a temporary or incognito mode in which a conversation is not retained in the normal history or is discarded afterward. These modes suit exploratory thinking or drafts that should not linger in the chat list.

What they do is specific: they typically prevent saving to the usual history and may prevent updating a persistent memory feature. What they do not do is guarantee anonymity, erase all copies of the text, or override an organization's logging and retention policies. The text still passes through the service to be processed, and applicable terms continue to apply.

This distinction is a frequent exam topic because it tests whether the candidate confuses conversation visibility with data-handling guarantees. Treat temporary mode as a housekeeping feature, not a security boundary. Sensitive material still needs policy, access controls, and an approved environment, regardless of the mode selected.

Managing writing preferences across chats

Continuity includes style. A user may want concise answers, a level of formality, a standard structure, or plain language. Repeating these preferences each session wastes context and invites inconsistency.

Keep a short preference note to paste into new sessions or store in an approved memory feature when policy allows. State each preference as a rule, not a history lesson: "Use short paragraphs and plain language" beats "Last time you were too formal." Avoid contradictions by deciding which rule wins when two conflict.

Preferences deserve the same governance thinking as facts. A style rule affecting public documents may need approval, and a preference encoding a legal or brand requirement belongs in the controlled source, not only in memory. Keep the note brief so it leaves room for the work.

Exam note: When a scenario describes inconsistent tone across chats, the best answer is usually to establish a single concise, reusable statement of the preference rather than repeatedly correcting the assistant after the fact.

A continuity checklist

Continuity is the discipline of keeping a piece of work coherent across time, sessions, and context limits. The following checklist can be applied at the start of a project, at natural stopping points, and whenever the warning signs appear.

  1. State the goal and the fixed decisions in one place that is not the chat itself.
  2. Capture exact values, definitions, and constraints; avoid relying on approximate recollection.
  3. Separate settled decisions from open questions in every record.
  4. Extract useful facts from large documents or tool output, then drop the raw text from the active thread.
  5. Restate critical constraints near the point of use, not only at the start of a long session.
  6. Watch for warning signs and act early rather than after answers become unreliable.
  7. Choose restart, summarize, or persist deliberately according to the situation.
  8. Write a handoff summary at every natural stopping point.
  9. Keep one concise, reusable statement of writing preferences.
  10. Match the tool to the policy: temporary mode for tidy history, governed storage for anything sensitive or auditable.

Used consistently, this checklist turns context management from a reaction into a routine.

Worked scenario

An analyst has worked for hours on a policy comparison. Early in the session the team fixed three decisions: use only published sources, present results in a two-column table, and exclude draft internal notes. A long report was pasted into the thread, followed by verbose tool output. By late afternoon, the latest tables include a draft note, the format has become three columns, and a previously rejected source has reappeared.

The analyst considers typing a longer correction, then realizes it would consume more of the scarce space. The signs point to a context problem, not an instruction problem.

The analyst checks the external project note, which holds the goal and the three decisions but not the full source list. Because those details are spread across a still-coherent thread, the analyst asks for a condensed summary of decisions, constraints, and sources accepted or rejected, then reviews it against the project note and corrects one blurred classification.

With the report's conclusions extracted, the raw text is no longer needed, so the analyst does not continue in that thread. Instead, a handoff summary is filled in using the template: goal, fixed decisions, definitions and constraints, the two-column rule, completed and remaining sections, the source list, and the single next action of finishing the final section. It is saved to the project note as the governed record.

A fresh session opens with that handoff summary. The assistant produces the final section in two columns, using only published sources and no draft notes. The next day, a follow-up again starts from the saved summary rather than a memory feature. The work stays consistent because controlling facts live outside any single window.

Notice how the three strategies combined: persistence supplied the stable record, summarization recovered distributed detail, and restarting restored a clean context. No single strategy would have handled the situation as well.

Exam note: The strongest answer in a continuity scenario is often the one that combines strategies and verifies a summary against an independent record, rather than trusting a single automated action.

Common mistakes

  • Treating the context window as unlimited storage instead of a budget shared by instructions, history, documents, tool output, and the answer.
  • Responding to degraded answers with longer prompts, which adds more text to an already crowded window.
  • Assuming a summary contains everything ever said, when it can only reflect what the assistant can currently see.
  • Using automatic memory as a system of record for facts that must be accurate, auditable, or shared.
  • Believing that temporary mode guarantees privacy or erases all copies of the text.
  • Burying a critical constraint in a large paste instead of restating it near the point of use.
  • Abandoning a thread before capturing details that exist nowhere else.
  • Keeping a preference note that contradicts itself or is too long to be reused.

Chapter summary

  • The context window is one shared budget; every extra token of input leaves less room for the answer.
  • Warning signs of a full window include lost instructions, repeated questions, contradictions, generic output, and truncated endings.
  • Early decisions get lost because older text is compressed, later messages overshadow them, and corrections stack.
  • Restart with a distilled brief when a thread is noisy or degraded; summarize when it is long but coherent; persist externally when facts must survive across sessions or people.
  • A handoff summary records the goal, fixed decisions, definitions, constraints, completed work, open items, sources, and the next action.
  • Automatic memory is a convenience; a governed external record is the source of truth for important facts.
  • Temporary mode affects conversation retention, not data handling guarantees or organizational policy.
  • A continuity checklist and a short reusable preference note keep long projects coherent.

Checkpoint questions

  1. A user pastes a very long document and then asks for a detailed multi-part answer. The reply begins well but ends abruptly without the final section. What is the most likely explanation?

A. The assistant ignored the document entirely.

B. Too much input consumed the shared window, leaving too little room to finish the answer.

C. The document contained a formatting error that stopped generation.

D. Temporary mode was enabled, which shortens responses.

  1. Which set of observations most strongly suggests that a conversation is approaching its context limit?

A. The assistant asks for facts given earlier, drops a standing format rule, and produces contradictory definitions.

B. The assistant answers quickly and uses short paragraphs.

C. The user attaches a small file and asks a follow-up question.

D. The assistant asks one clarifying question before starting.

  1. A long thread is still coherent, but a decision made at the start is buried among many turns and needs to be consolidated. Which strategy is most appropriate?

A. Restart with a distilled brief, because no thread should be trusted.

B. Persist externally only, because summarization never helps.

C. Summarize the thread, then verify the summary against an independent record.

D. Enable temporary mode to make the assistant forget older turns.

  1. Work must continue next month with a different colleague and an auditable record of decisions. Which strategy is most important?

A. Restarting from memory alone.

B. Persisting key facts in a governed external record.

C. Asking the assistant to remember the format.

D. Shortening each prompt without recording anything.

  1. What is the best reason to review a generated summary against your own notes before relying on it?

A. Summaries are always longer than the original thread.

B. The assistant cannot summarize a fact that has already dropped out of its window.

C. Summaries cannot include open questions.

D. Reviewing a summary disables the memory feature.

  1. Which statement about incognito or temporary mode is correct?

A. It guarantees that the text is never transmitted to the service.

B. It erases all copies of the text from every system.

C. It typically limits retention in the normal history but does not override processing or organizational policy.

D. It permanently increases the size of the context window.

  1. Which item belongs in a handoff summary?

A. Every message exchanged during the session, copied verbatim.

B. Only the assistant's opinions about the work.

C. Fixed decisions, constraints, completed work, open items, and the next action.

D. A list of unrelated background facts to maximize context.

Answers and rationale

  1. B. The answer is correct because input and output share one budget; a huge document and a request for a long reply can leave too little space to finish. A is incorrect because the reply began well, showing the document was used. C is incorrect because a formatting error in source text would not normally stop generation mid-answer. D is incorrect because temporary mode affects retention, not response length.

  2. A. These three signals together, asking for already-provided facts, dropping a standing rule, and contradicting definitions, are classic indicators of a crowded context. B describes a concise answer, which is not a warning sign. C describes an ordinary action with no bearing on the limit. D describes good practice, not degradation.

  3. C. When a thread is long but still coherent, summarization consolidates distributed detail, and verification catches anything the model can no longer see. A is incorrect because a coherent thread still holds useful detail and needless restarting can lose it. B is incorrect because summarization often does help and can feed the external record. D is incorrect because temporary mode does not selectively remove older turns.

  4. B. Cross-session, cross-person, auditable continuity requires a governed external record that does not depend on any chat window. A is incorrect because restarting from memory alone is unreliable and unauditable. C is incorrect because an automatic memory feature is not a controlled source of truth. D is incorrect because shortening prompts without recording facts guarantees loss.

  5. B. A summary can only reflect content currently visible to the model, so a detail already dropped cannot be recovered from it. A is incorrect because summaries are meant to be shorter. C is incorrect because a good summary explicitly lists open questions. D is incorrect because reviewing a text has no effect on memory settings.

  6. C. Temporary mode generally limits retention in the ordinary history while the text is still processed and organizational policies still apply. A is incorrect because the text must be transmitted to be processed. B is incorrect because no such universal erasure is guaranteed. D is incorrect because the mode does not change window capacity.

  7. C. A handoff summary is built from fixed decisions, constraints, completed work, open items, sources, and the next action. A is incorrect because a verbatim transcript defeats the purpose of compaction. B is incorrect because opinions are not reliable continuity facts. D is incorrect because unrelated background wastes the budget and obscures what matters.

End of chapter 10.

A business request rarely arrives in a form that can be built against. It arrives as a sentence in a meeting or a line in a ticket, often in the language of solutions: "We need an assistant that handles customer questions," or "Can the model just write our reports?" The work begins before any tool is opened. It begins with turning that sentence into something precise enough to test, deciding which situations genuinely suit an AI assistant, and recording boundaries so everyone agrees on what the project is and is not.

This chapter covers that front-end discipline: converting a vague ask into testable requirements, separating the requested solution from the real problem, choosing a first use case that can succeed, and compressing the understanding into a one-page brief.

Learning objectives

After this chapter you should be able to:

  • Convert a vague request into requirements that can be tested and accepted.
  • Ask clarifying questions before proposing a tool or a design.
  • Separate a requester's proposed solution from the underlying need.
  • Classify requirements as functional or non-functional and explain why both matter.
  • Define success metrics and acceptance criteria that are measurable and hard to game.
  • Select initial use cases that are low-risk, repetitive, and measurable.
  • Screen out use cases needing real-time systems, privileged actions, or unverifiable facts.
  • Build a requirements matrix and a fit-assessment matrix.
  • State explicit non-goals and assemble a one-page brief.

Key concepts

From a vague request to a testable requirement

A vague request is not wrong; it is unfinished. "Make support faster" cannot be built or accepted because it has no scope, threshold, or way to tell when it is achieved. A testable requirement is precise enough that a reviewer can write a check and get a clear pass or fail.

Compare these:

  • Vague: "The assistant should give good answers."
  • Testable: "For a sample of fifty common product questions, the answer must include the correct policy value in at least forty-five cases and must never state a value that contradicts the current policy document."

The second names a population, a behavior, a threshold, and a failure condition. Anyone can run the check and agree on the result.

Four questions convert the first kind into the second:

  1. What is the trigger or context? When does this apply?
  2. What exactly happens? Describe observable behavior, not intention.
  3. How will we know? Name the evidence or measurement.
  4. What threshold counts as success? Give a number, bound, or binary condition.

If you cannot answer all four, the statement belongs on an open-questions list, not in a specification. Untestable requirements become disputes later, when a stakeholder says the result is not what they meant and there is no agreed standard to settle it.

Exam note: A foundations question may present a soft request and ask what to do first. The strongest answer is usually to clarify and make it testable — not to choose a model, draft a prompt, or start building.

Clarifying questions come before solutions

The common early mistake is solution jumping: hearing a request and immediately naming a tool or feature. It feels productive because it produces something concrete, but it locks in assumptions before the problem is understood. A tool chosen against an unclear need is judged against a moving target.

Clarifying questions are cheap — they cost a conversation; the wrong design costs weeks. Useful families include:

  • Outcome: What would be different if this worked? What is being improved?
  • Current state: How is the task done today, by whom, with what handoffs?
  • Users: Who interacts with the result, and what do they already know?
  • Data and sources: What information is needed, where does it live, how current is it?
  • Constraints and risk: What budgets, policies, or deadlines bound the work? What happens if an answer is wrong?
  • Measurement: How would success be observed, and is there a baseline?

Ask open questions first. Closed questions that suggest an answer ("Would a chatbot be useful?") produce agreement rather than information. "What happens today when a customer asks that?" is more revealing.

Also distinguish clarifying questions, which resolve stated ambiguity, from discovery questions, which surface what no one mentioned — exception cases, undocumented approval steps, the spreadsheet that is the real system of record. A set built only from clarifying questions can be internally consistent and still miss the workflow.

Sometimes the need turns out not to be an AI problem. That is a successful outcome. Being willing to recommend a template, a better search index, or a process change is responsible practice: an assistant should be chosen because it fits the need, not because the conversation began with the word "AI."

Try it: Take a request such as "summarize our meetings." Write six questions — at least two about outcome and two about risk — before writing any prompt.

Separating the ask from the need

What a person requests and what they need are different objects. The request is a proposed solution, often borrowed from something they have seen. The need is the problem that solution was meant to solve. Confusing them produces elegant answers to the wrong question.

A manager might ask for an assistant that "automatically replies to all customer emails." Taken literally, that is unattended external action with no review: wrong answers sent out and reputational exposure. The underlying need might be narrower — "the team spends the first hour each day typing the same acknowledgment and routing instructions." The right answer could be reviewed draft generation, saved replies, or an email template. None is the system requested, and all may serve better.

Treat the ask as a hypothesis about the need and probe downward:

  • What does this let you do that you cannot do today?
  • What happens immediately before and after this step now?
  • Why is the step painful — slow, repetitive, error-prone, or unclear?

The last question matters because the cause determines the remedy. Delay caused by waiting for approval is an authority problem, not a text-generation problem. Delay caused by retyping the same content is repetition, and generation may help. Delay caused by missing information is a data problem, and no assistant can safely invent the missing fact.

Common mistake: Listing "build a chatbot" as a requirement freezes the design before the problem is understood. Record the ask in the brief, but record the need as the requirement.

Functional and non-functional requirements

Functional requirements describe what the system does: behaviors, transformations, outputs. Non-functional requirements describe how well: the qualities and constraints governing performance, safety, and acceptability. Both are necessary, and for an assistant the non-functional class often carries more risk.

A functional requirement for a support assistant might be: "Given a product question and the current knowledge base, produce a draft answer that cites the source section used." A corresponding non-functional requirement might be: "Never state a policy value absent from the supplied source," or "Return the draft within ten seconds," or "Every draft is reviewable before sending."

The distinction matters because non-functional qualities are easy to omit from a demo. A prototype can produce plausible text while failing on accuracy, privacy, latency, or auditability. If those were never written down, nobody tests them until a real user is affected.

Common non-functional categories:

  • Accuracy and groundedness: answers supported by supplied sources; uncertainty expressed, not hidden.
  • Privacy and data handling: sensitive inputs are not exposed or retained beyond need.
  • Latency and throughput: responses fit the workflow's time budget.
  • Consistency and tone: output matches organizational voice and terminology.
  • Auditability: outputs can be traced, reviewed, and reproduced.
  • Availability and fallback: the workflow degrades gracefully when the assistant is unsure or unavailable.

Quantify wherever possible. "Fast enough" is not testable; "under ten seconds for ninety percent of pilot requests" is. Where a quality resists a number, define an observable test such as a reviewer checklist for tone.

The requirements matrix

A requirements matrix turns scattered statements into something trackable. Each row is one requirement; columns capture what is needed to test, prioritize, and govern it.

ID Requirement Type Source / reason Acceptance test Priority Risk / notes
R1 Draft an answer to a product question using only the current knowledge base Functional Support need: repetitive first-hour replies On 50 questions, 45+ cite the correct source section High Verify source freshness
R2 Express uncertainty when the knowledge base lacks the answer Functional Risk of confident wrong answers On 10 unanswerable cases, 9+ state no source found High No invented values
R3 No draft states a policy value absent from supplied sources Non-functional / accuracy Compliance constraint Scan plus reviewer check finds zero unsupported values High Blocking requirement
R4 Return a draft within 10 seconds for 90% of requests Non-functional / latency Fits the support queue Measure 50 requests; 45+ under 10 seconds Medium Depends on retrieval size
R5 Every draft is labeled and human-reviewed before sending Non-functional / auditability External-communication policy Review log shows 100% of pilot drafts approved High Enforced by workflow
R6 Mask customer identifiers before processing Non-functional / privacy Data handling policy Sample inputs contain no direct identifiers High Test all fields

The identifier gives each requirement a stable reference. The source column preserves the reason so competing priorities can be weighed. The acceptance test converts intent into a check. Priority forces a decision; risk records where attention is needed. Rows can carry statuses such as "proposed," "met," or "deferred." The value lies in the habit of keeping each row testable and each reason visible.

Exam note: If a matrix contains "be helpful and accurate," the flaw is that it is untestable: no population, threshold, or evidence.

Success metrics and acceptance criteria

Two ideas are often confused. Acceptance criteria are the conditions a deliverable must satisfy to be accepted, usually tied to one requirement. Success metrics are the broader business measures showing whether the project was worth doing. Acceptance asks "did this pass?" Success asks "did this help?"

An acceptance criterion might be "at least forty-five of fifty drafts cite the correct source." A success metric might be "average handling time for the target category fell twenty percent against baseline." The first is a component gate; the second judges the initiative.

Good metrics are:

  • Baseline-able: the current state can be measured before the change.
  • Attributable: improvement can reasonably be connected to the intervention.
  • Bounded: a defined population, window, and calculation rule, so two people get the same number.
  • Resistant to gaming: a speed measure is paired with a quality measure.
  • Balanced: at least one quality or risk measure accompanies any efficiency measure.

A useful pattern pairs a leading indicator, which moves quickly and gives early feedback (percentage of drafts accepted without edits), with a lagging indicator, the eventual business outcome (customer satisfaction or resolution time). Leading indicators allow correction during a pilot; lagging indicators confirm value afterward.
