Write acceptance criteria when requirements are written, not afterward. Defining the threshold after seeing thirty-eight correct answers and then setting the bar at thirty-eight is not acceptance — it is redefining the goal to escape a failure.

Common mistake: Measuring only volume — drafts generated, summaries produced. Volume shows activity, not value; a system producing many unusable drafts scores well on volume and badly on usefulness.

Choosing initial use cases

A first use case should be chosen for its chance of succeeding and being evaluated, not for the impressiveness of the vision. Three properties dominate: low-risk, repetitive, and measurable.

Low-risk means a wrong output has small consequences and can be caught before harm. Internal drafting of a routine summary is safer than publishing directly to a customer. Reversible, reviewed actions beat irreversible ones. If an error requires a correction notice or refund, the case is not a first pilot.

Repetitive means the task happens often enough to form a pattern and yield measurable volume. High-frequency, similar instances let a team build a representative sample and spot recurring failures. A task done twice a month with wide variation offers little signal.

Measurable means there is a baseline and a comparison. There is a current way of doing the task with an observable cost — time, errors, steps. If nobody can describe today's method, there is no baseline.

Look also for: usable, current, bounded source data; an available reviewer; a scope narrowed to a defined category; outputs verifiable by someone with domain knowledge; and a clean stopping point.

A good first case is a thin slice — one category, one team, one channel, one clear check — deliberately smaller than the ambition. Success on a thin slice produces evidence and real examples from which a broader design can be argued. A case chosen for breadth ("all customer contacts") fails because no single measure fits and no failure mode can be isolated.

Exam note: Favor the narrow, high-volume, reversible, reviewable option over the broad, strategic, visible one.

Screening use cases out

Saying no is a core skill. Some cases are unsuitable regardless of prompt quality. Three categories deserve attention.

Real-time dependence. If correctness depends on live state the assistant cannot reliably obtain — current inventory, a changing schedule, live status — a plausible answer may be stale when read. Adding a fresh data feed turns the case into an integration project with its own reliability demands. If the workflow needs a sub-second answer and freshness cannot be guaranteed, this is not a first pilot; a direct query against the system of record is often the right answer.

Privileged or irreversible actions. Tasks that move money, alter accounts, send external messages unattended, or change records carry consequences a probabilistic system should not own. The issue is authority as much as accuracy: if the action goes wrong, who is accountable, and can it be reversed? An assistant may draft the action or prepare a checklist, but execution belongs with an authorized human or deterministic system.

Unverifiable facts. With no reliable source to check against, correctness cannot be enforced. Fluent text is easily mistaken for accuracy. Cases built on undocumented exceptions or "what everyone knows" are dangerous. Grounded work requires every factual claim to be traced to a supplied source or marked uncertain; where no source exists, the honest design declines to answer.

Other signals — high-stakes decisions about people, regulated determinations, licensed professional judgment — are not always permanent exclusions, but they demand controls that make them poor early pilots.

Signal in a proposed case Concern Decision Safer alternative
Needs live inventory or current status Real-time dependence; staleness Defer Query the system of record
Sends customer emails unattended Irreversible external action Exclude execution Draft for human approval
No authoritative source for facts Unverifiable output Exclude factual answering Answer only from supplied documents
Variable task done a few times a month No volume or baseline Defer Pick a high-volume sibling task
Wrong answer needs a formal correction High consequence Defer or gate Internal suggestion with review
Repetitive, reviewed, timed today Good pilot characteristics Advance None needed

The fit-assessment matrix

Where screening is a set of filters, a fit-assessment matrix compares several candidates on common criteria. It turns "this one feels more useful" into an explicit, reviewable judgment. The value is the conversation about weights and scores, which exposes hidden assumptions.

Score each criterion from 0 (poor) to 2 (strong), apply weights, and total. Decision bands translate totals into "advance," "revise," or "defer."

Criterion Weight Draft support replies Live status answers Unattended account changes
Clarity of need 2 2 (4) 1 (2) 1 (2)
Repetition / volume 2 2 (4) 2 (4) 1 (2)
Verifiability of output 3 2 (6) 0 (0) 1 (3)
Data freshness and availability 2 2 (4) 0 (0) 1 (2)
Low consequence of error 3 2 (6) 1 (3) 0 (0)
Human review feasible 2 2 (4) 1 (2) 0 (0)
Measurability / baseline exists 2 2 (4) 1 (2) 1 (2)
Weighted total 32 / 36 11 / 36 11 / 36
Decision Advance Defer — real-time Exclude — privileged

Two cautions. A score is a decision aid, not a decision maker: a single veto condition, such as an irreversible external action, overrides a high total. And scores must be justified — an unexplained "2" is an opinion wearing a number, so some teams require a one-line reason for each low score. Weights reflect organizational context, so agreeing on them is often the most valuable part of the exercise.

Explicit non-goals

A non-goal is a capability the project deliberately will not pursue. Stating non-goals is scope control, not negativity. Without them, each stakeholder assumes their favorite feature is included, and the disagreement surfaces only at delivery.

Non-goals prevent scope creep, reduce risk, sharpen communication, and protect the pilot's evaluation criteria from expanding midway. They should be specific: "will not handle billing disputes" beats "keep scope reasonable." Examples for a support-drafting pilot:

  • The assistant will not send anything externally; a human approves every draft.
  • It will not answer outside the three selected categories.
  • It will not access or display payment details.
  • It will not make commitments about delivery dates or refunds.

Non-goals often trace directly to screened-out cases and high non-functional requirements. That overlap is intentional. Mark the horizon clearly: "not in this pilot" differs from "not ever."

Exam note: If stakeholders are confused about coverage, document explicit non-goals and confirm them — do not add features or requirements.

Building the one-page brief

The one-page brief gathers this thinking into an alignment artifact. A reader should grasp the problem, scope, success measures, and boundaries in one sitting. It leads with the problem, records the original ask and the interpreted need, summarizes requirements by reference to the matrix, names a few metrics, lists non-goals, and surfaces risks and assumptions — the things believed true but unverified, and the likeliest source of surprise.

ONE-PAGE BRIEF — [working name]

1. Problem and original ask
   - Friction today: ...   Original request: ...
2. Interpreted need
   - Underlying problem: ...   Why it differs from the ask: ...
3. Users and context
   - Primary users: ...   Where output enters the workflow: ...
4. Scope (this pilot)
   - Included categories / channels / teams: ...   Thin slice: ...
5. Requirements summary
   - Key functional: ...   Key non-functional (accuracy, privacy, latency, audit): ...
6. Success metrics and acceptance criteria
   - Baseline: ...   Leading indicator and target: ...
   - Lagging indicator and target: ...   Acceptance gate: ...
7. Non-goals (explicit)
   - Will not do: ...   Horizon: pilot only / indefinite
8. Constraints, assumptions, risks
   - Constraints: ...   Unverified assumptions: ...   Top risks and mitigation: ...
9. Open questions and owners
   - Question: ...   Who answers: ...   Needed by: ...
10. Next steps
   - Immediate actions and decision point: ...

A brief that is hard to fill in is diagnostic. Blank metrics mean the need is still vague; blank non-goals mean nobody faced the risky parts; blank open questions after one conversation suggest the discovery was not deep enough. Treat the brief as a diagnostic instrument as much as a communication document.

Worked scenario

An operations team requests: "We want an assistant that answers all our branch questions so we can stop relying on the head office." Several stakeholders have already pictured one tool covering every topic. An analyst is asked to make it evaluable.

Rather than discussing models, the analyst asks about the current state. Branch staff send about sixty questions a week; roughly three quarters fall into three categories — completing a standard form, finding a current policy document, and routing a request to the right department. The rest are unusual exceptions requiring judgment and a documented decision. Answers take about one business day, and no one has measured the interruption cost.

Ask and need now separate. The ask is "answers all branch questions." The need is "reduce the daily delay and head-office interruption caused by a small set of repeated, documented questions, while keeping judgment-heavy exceptions with accountable people." That reframing removes the dangerous word "all."

The analyst drafts requirements. Functional ones cover producing a draft from the current policy set for the three categories and flagging out-of-scope questions. Non-functional ones dominate the risk column: no answer states a value absent from supplied documents, every answer names its section, drafts stay internal until a head-office reviewer approves them, and the tool is not customer-accessible. The core acceptance test is a fifty-question sample from real requests: at least forty-five correct, sourced answers and zero unsupported policy values.

For metrics, the baseline is one business day. The leading indicator is the share of reviewer approvals without edits, targeted at eighty percent; the lagging indicator is median response time for the three categories, targeted below two hours. Pairing speed with quality prevents faster-but-wrong answers from counting as success.

Screening removes candidates. "Is this account in compliance?" depends on live records and yields a consequential determination, so it is excluded. Unattended form approval is excluded as a privileged action. Undocumented local exceptions are excluded because no authoritative source exists. The three documented categories advance because they are repetitive, sourced, reviewable, and measurable.

A fit-assessment matrix confirms the ranking: drafts score strongly on verifiability, data availability, low error consequence, review feasibility, and baseline; live compliance questions fail freshness and verifiability; unattended approvals score zero on consequence and review. The totals are almost unnecessary — veto conditions already decided it — but recording them makes the reasoning visible to stakeholders attached to the broad vision.

The brief states non-goals: no undocumented exceptions, no external sending, no account-specific data, no compliance determinations, nothing outside the three categories. Each traces to a screening decision, so the boundary is visibly analytical rather than arbitrary caution.

Two assumptions are flagged: that policy documents are current and consistent, and that a reviewer has daily time to check drafts. Both become open questions with an owner and deadline. The recommendation is a narrow pilot on form-completion questions using a fixed sample, with a decision point reviewing acceptance results against the agreed threshold.

The outcome is not a grand system but a small, testable first step whose boundaries everyone accepts before work begins. A well-scoped first case produces evidence; an oversized one produces arguments.

Common mistakes

  • Proposing a tool, model, or prompt before the need is understood.
  • Recording the requester's solution as the requirement.
  • Writing untestable requirements such as "be accurate."
  • Omitting non-functional requirements, then meeting accuracy, privacy, or latency problems in real use.
  • Defining thresholds after seeing results, turning a failed test into a relaxed goal.
  • Measuring activity volume instead of outcome, or speed without a quality companion.
  • Choosing a first case that is broad, visible, and irreversible because it sounds strategic.
  • Ignoring veto conditions because a weighted total looks attractive.
  • Leaving non-goals unstated and allowing scope to expand mid-pilot.
  • Carrying unverified assumptions silently into the brief.

Chapter summary

  • Vague requests become usable when given a context, observable behavior, evidence, and threshold.
  • Clarifying and discovery questions precede solutions and expose outcome, current state, users, data, constraints, risk, and measurement.
  • A request is a hypothesis about a need; probing the cause often yields a smaller, better answer — sometimes no assistant at all.
  • Functional requirements describe behavior; non-functional requirements describe qualities that usually carry greater risk.
  • A requirements matrix gives each requirement an identifier, reason, acceptance test, priority, and risk note.
  • Acceptance criteria judge a component; success metrics judge the initiative. Pair a leading and lagging indicator, with a quality measure.
  • First cases should be low-risk, repetitive, and measurable — a thin slice with a baseline and reviewer.
  • Screen out real-time-dependent, privileged, and unverifiable cases; veto conditions override high scores.
  • Explicit non-goals and a one-page brief keep scope, expectations, and assumptions visible.

Checkpoint questions

  1. A stakeholder says, "The assistant should give reliable answers." What is the best first response?

A. Select a model known for accuracy. B. Ask what "reliable" means and define a test with a threshold. C. Draft a prompt telling the assistant to be accurate. D. Start a pilot across all question categories.

  1. A manager asks for a tool that sends replies to every customer email unattended. What does this illustrate?

A. A non-functional requirement needing a latency target. B. A success metric lacking a baseline. C. A proposed solution that should be probed for the underlying need. D. A low-risk use case ideal for a first pilot.

  1. Which is a non-functional requirement?

A. Produce a summary of each request. B. Flag questions outside covered categories. C. Return a response within ten seconds for ninety percent of requests. D. Add the source section to every drafted answer.

  1. Which use case is the strongest first pilot?

A. Approving benefit claims unattended to reduce headcount. B. Reporting live system status to customers within one second. C. Drafting internal answers to three common, documented question types with reviewer approval. D. Explaining undocumented exceptions only long-serving staff understand.

  1. A fit matrix gives a case a high total, but it sends external messages with no human approval. What should the analyst do?

A. Advance it because the score is high. B. Lower its priority but keep it in the pilot. C. Treat the privileged action as a veto and exclude unattended sending. D. Remove the weight column so the total changes.

  1. Why should a brief include explicit non-goals?

A. To list every feature the team hopes to add eventually. B. To set boundaries, prevent scope creep, and make risk decisions visible. C. To replace success metrics with qualitative statements. D. To avoid asking clarifying questions.

  1. Which statement best distinguishes an acceptance criterion from a success metric?

A. They are the same thing under different names. B. An acceptance criterion judges whether one requirement passes; a success metric judges whether the initiative helped the business. C. An acceptance criterion is always numerical; a success metric is always qualitative. D. Success metrics are written after launch; acceptance criteria are written before.

Answers and rationale

  1. B. "Reliable" is undefined and untestable; clarifying it and attaching a measurable threshold creates a usable requirement. A jumps to a solution before the standard is known. C relies on an instruction instead of an observable check, so pass or fail is subjective. D starts broad before scope and acceptance criteria exist, making evaluation meaningless.

  2. C. The manager stated a solution — unattended external replies — that may not match the real problem. Probing may reveal a narrower need, such as reducing repetitive drafting, served by a safer design. A misreads the request as a quality attribute when the issue is action and authority. B misidentifies it as a measurement problem; no metric was proposed. D is wrong on risk: unattended external communication is high consequence and unfit for a first pilot.

  3. C. Latency is a quality attribute and is quantified, so it is non-functional and testable. A describes a behavior, so it is functional. B describes a decision the system makes, so it is functional. D describes what the answer contains — a functional output requirement, not a cross-cutting quality.

  4. C. It is repetitive, sourced, reviewable, reversible, and tied to a baseline — the combination that gives a pilot a clean evaluation. A is a privileged, consequential action that should not be unattended. B depends on real-time data and strict latency, adding freshness and integration risk. D relies on facts with no authoritative source, so correctness cannot be verified.

  5. C. Certain risks are veto conditions; an irreversible external action overrides a favorable total, so exclude unattended sending and consider a reviewed alternative. A treats arithmetic as authority and ignores the veto. B retains a high-risk activity despite the concern. D changes the scoring to avoid the real issue and does nothing about the risk.

  6. B. Non-goals set boundaries, discourage scope creep, and make earlier risk decisions visible to readers. A describes the opposite: non-goals exclude work rather than list desired features. C is wrong because non-goals complement, not replace, metrics. D is wrong because non-goals follow clarification rather than remove the need for it.

  7. B. Acceptance criteria are component-level gates tied to requirements, while success metrics assess the broader business outcome of the initiative. A is false — conflating them loses the ability to tell a passing component from a worthwhile project. C is false because both can be numerical; the distinction is scope, not format. D is false because success metrics should be defined before the pilot, with a baseline, so that value can be judged against a plan.

End of chapter 11.

Learning objectives

  • Use an AI assistant as a research partner while keeping source discipline and verification habits intact.
  • Turn research findings into an actionable plan with milestones, owners, and checkpoints.
  • Map a process before automating any part of it, so improvement effort lands on the right step.
  • Identify bottlenecks, handoff delays, and rework loops from observable signals rather than guesswork.
  • Decide whether a task is worth augmenting, worth removing, or best left unchanged.
  • Design a small pilot with a baseline and a measurement plan before recommending a wider rollout.
  • Draft process documentation and checklists with the assistant, then verify them against how work actually happens.
  • Measure cycle time and rework, and recognize when a spreadsheet or a rule-based tool is the better answer.

Key concepts

The assistant as a research partner, not an oracle

Research is the activity of reducing uncertainty. When a team faces a question it cannot answer from memory, it gathers evidence, compares sources, and arrives at a conclusion it is willing to act on. An AI assistant can accelerate almost every part of that loop, but it does not replace the judgment that decides what the evidence means.

Think of the assistant as a fast, tireless research aide with two weaknesses: it can sound certain when it is wrong, and it does not know what your organization actually does unless you tell it. A good research partnership therefore has a division of labor. The assistant helps frame questions, generate search terms, summarize long material, compare options, and expose assumptions. The human researcher supplies the goal, chooses which sources count as authoritative, checks claims against those sources, and owns the final conclusion.

This distinction matters on the exam because many scenario questions are really tests of whether you keep the human in the decision seat. An answer that says "ask the assistant and use its answer" is almost always weaker than one that says "ask the assistant to gather and structure candidate evidence, then verify the load-bearing claims against a trusted source before deciding."

A productive research exchange usually moves through a few distinct moves:

  1. Define the question narrowly enough that an answer would change a decision.
  2. Ask the assistant for a map of the topic — the main dimensions, the competing viewpoints, and the terms used in the field.
  3. Request specific, checkable claims rather than a smooth general narrative.
  4. Verify the claims that carry the most weight.
  5. Ask the assistant to reconcile conflicting evidence and to state what remains uncertain.

Exam note: "Research partner" implies collaboration with verification, not delegation of judgment. If a choice removes the review step, treat it as a red flag.

Source discipline and verification

Source discipline is the habit of tracking where a claim came from and how much confidence it deserves. It becomes critical the moment research output will be reused in a plan, a proposal, or a decision that affects other people.

When working with an assistant, source discipline has three practical layers. First, separate what the assistant produced from what it retrieved or cited. A confident summary is not itself evidence; it is a description of evidence that may or may not be accurate. Second, prioritize primary sources — the original policy, the actual dataset, the vendor documentation, the internal procedure — over a summary of a summary. Third, record provenance as you go, so that a later reader can trace a number or assertion back to its origin.

Not all sources serve the same purpose. The table below shows a useful way to classify them.

Source type Best used for Main risk Typical verification step
Original internal records Facts about how this organization operates May be outdated or incomplete Confirm date and owner of the record
Official documentation Defined behavior, limits, and requirements May describe the ideal rather than the deployed version Check version and effective date
Raw data extracts Measuring current performance May contain errors or inconsistent definitions Validate fields and sampling rules
Expert conversation Context, exceptions, and unwritten rules Hard to reproduce; may be anecdotal Confirm against an artifact or a second voice
Assistant-generated summary Orientation and framing May omit caveats or blend sources Trace key claims back to the cited material
Public articles and commentary Broad awareness of a topic Variable quality and possible bias Check the underlying evidence, not just the opinion

A helpful prompt pattern is to ask the assistant to label each claim with the source it relies on and to mark anything it cannot support. That does not make the output authoritative, but it makes verification cheaper because the weak links are visible.

Common mistake: Treating a polished, well-organized answer as a reliable one. Fluency and accuracy are independent. Organization helps you read; verification is what makes the result trustworthy.

Turning research into a plan

Research that ends in a document has limited value. Research earns its keep when it changes what happens next. The bridge between the two is a plan: a concrete statement of what will be done, by whom, by when, and how the team will know it worked.

A good plan has a small number of well-defined milestones. A milestone is not a vague aspiration like "improve onboarding." It is an observable state such as "the current onboarding steps are mapped and agreed" or "a pilot has run with five participants and results are recorded." Each milestone should have an owner — a role accountable for moving it forward — and a checkpoint where progress is reviewed and the plan can be adjusted.

The assistant is useful here as a structuring tool. Give it the research findings and ask it to propose milestones, dependencies, owners by role, and risks. Then apply human judgment: does each milestone actually move the goal, are dependencies realistic, and is anyone overloaded?

A compact plan record might include the following fields:

Plan element What it answers Example phrasing
Objective What changes and why Reduce rework in intake review
Milestone What observable state is reached Current intake steps mapped and signed off
Owner Which role is accountable The operations coordinator
Dependency What must happen first Access to the intake dataset
Checkpoint When progress is reviewed End of week two
Success signal How completion is recognized Zero unresolved steps in the map

Notice that owners are roles, not named individuals. This keeps the plan durable when people change assignments and keeps the emphasis on accountability rather than personality.

Try it: Take a research finding you already have and write three milestones for it. For each, state the owner as a role and the success signal as something a reviewer could observe without asking for an explanation.

Process mapping before automation

A recurring error in improvement work is automating a process nobody has mapped. When that happens, the team encodes the current confusion at higher speed. Mapping first creates a shared picture of the work, exposes steps that should not exist, and gives the assistant something precise to reason about.

A process map can be simple. For each step, capture the trigger, the action, the person or system involved, the input, the output, and the waiting time between steps. The goal is not a beautiful diagram; it is an accurate account of how work flows today, including informal workarounds. Those workarounds are especially important because they often signal that the official process is missing something.

Once the map exists, the assistant can help interrogate it. Useful questions include: which steps add no information, where does work wait the longest, which handoffs require re-explanation, and which decisions follow a stable rule? The answers become candidates for improvement, but each candidate must be tested against the real constraints of the organization — compliance obligations, system limitations, and the need for human judgment.

Mapping also establishes the boundary between the "current" and "future" process. Without that boundary, teams debate improvements in the abstract. With it, they can point to a specific box in the map and say what will change.

Finding bottlenecks and handoff delays

A bottleneck is the step that limits the throughput of the whole process. Improving a step that is not a bottleneck rarely improves overall results; it just builds inventory in front of the constraint. Identifying the bottleneck therefore comes before choosing an intervention.

Bottlenecks and handoff delays leave detectable signals. A queue that keeps growing is a sign that arrivals exceed capacity at some point downstream. Long idle periods between steps suggest waiting rather than working. Frequent messages asking "where is this?" point to poor visibility. Repeated clarification requests at a handoff suggest the receiving role lacks context from the sending role.

The assistant can help turn raw observations into a structured diagnosis. Provide it with timestamps, step labels, and notes, and ask it to compute waiting time between steps, flag steps where work accumulates, and list the handoffs with the most back-and-forth. It can also draft interview questions for the people involved, which is valuable when the data alone cannot explain why a delay occurs.

The key discipline is to distinguish a bottleneck from a mere annoyance. A step can be slow, confusing, and unpopular without limiting overall throughput. The table of signals and actions later in this chapter helps make that distinction systematically.

Augment, remove, or leave alone

Not every candidate task deserves an AI assistant. When reviewing a process step, there are three honest options.

Augment means the step stays but the assistant makes it faster or more consistent — for example, drafting a first version of a routine summary that a reviewer then checks. Remove means the step does not earn its place at all; it exists because of habit, a legacy system, or a check that no longer protects anything. Leave alone means the step is efficient, low-risk, and not worth the cost of changing it.

Choosing among the three depends on the nature of the step. Work that is high-volume, text-heavy, and repetitive is a strong augmentation candidate. Work with a stable, rule-based decision is often a stronger automation candidate — or a candidate for deletion if the rule no longer serves a purpose. Work that requires negotiation, empathy, or accountability to an affected person usually deserves to stay human-led, though the assistant can still prepare materials for it.

Exam note: Removing a useless step is often a better answer than speeding it up. If a check produces no decisions and no one acts on its output, the exam is likely looking for simplification, not automation.

Pilots, baselines, and small samples

A pilot is a controlled trial of a change before a broad rollout. Its purpose is to reveal problems while they are still cheap to fix. Two ingredients make a pilot informative: a baseline and a measurement plan.

The baseline is a description of how the process performs before the change. Without it, any improvement claim is unanchored. The baseline should use the same definitions and measurement method that will be used during the pilot, otherwise the comparison is misleading. If "cycle time" means one thing before the change and something else during it, the numbers cannot be compared.

The sample should be small but representative. It should include the common case and at least one difficult case, because a change that only works on easy inputs is not ready to scale. The pilot also needs explicit success criteria and stop criteria: what result would justify expanding the change, and what result would cause the team to halt it.

Here is a compact pilot measurement plan in a text form that a team could adapt:

PILOT MEASUREMENT PLAN

Change under test: Assistant-drafted first pass for intake summaries
Scope: One intake queue, one work location
Sample: 20 consecutive cases, including at least 3 complex cases

Baseline (collect before change):
- Cycle time per case: median and 90th percentile, same clock start/stop rules
- Rework rate: share of cases reopened or sent back within 5 business days
- Reviewer edit distance: count of substantive edits to the summary

During pilot:
- Record the same three metrics with identical definitions
- Log every exception, tool failure, and override by the reviewer
- Capture reviewer confidence rating on a fixed 1-5 scale

Success criteria:
- Median cycle time at least 20% lower than baseline
- Rework rate not worse than baseline by more than 2 percentage points
- No critical accuracy failures in the sample

Stop criteria:
- Any critical accuracy failure involving a wrong factual claim passed downstream
- Reviewer confidence averaging below 3 out of 5
- Cycle time improvement below 5% after 20 cases

Decision point: Review results with the process owner and decide to expand,
revise, or abandon. Do not expand on a single favorable metric alone.

A fenced plan like this forces clarity. It states exactly what will be collected, how it will be compared, and what the decision rules are — before anyone sees the results, which reduces the temptation to move the goalposts afterward.

Common mistake: Declaring a pilot successful because participants "liked it." Preference is useful feedback, but it is not evidence of faster cycle time or lower rework. Keep perception and performance as separate measures.

Drafting process documentation and checklists

Once a future process is agreed, it must be documented well enough that another person can follow it. The assistant is well suited to drafting this material because it can turn rough notes into structured steps, produce a checklist from a procedure, and reword the same content for different audiences.

Useful documentation deliverables include a step-by-step procedure, a decision table for exceptions, a checklist for reviewers, and a short "what changed" note for people affected by the new process. The assistant can generate all of these from the process map and the pilot findings. The risk is that it will invent plausible-sounding steps that were never agreed, so every draft must be checked against the source map.

A good checklist is short, observable, and ordered. Instead of "ensure the summary is accurate," write "confirm each figure in the summary matches the source record." Instead of "handle exceptions," write "if the source record is missing, route to the intake coordinator." Specificity is what makes a checklist repeatable.

Documentation is also a control point for process improvement. If a step is hard to describe clearly, that is often a sign the step itself is unclear. Writing the procedure can therefore reveal design flaws that mapping alone did not.

Measuring cycle time and rework

Two measures carry much of the weight in process improvement: cycle time and rework.

Cycle time is the elapsed time from a defined start point to a defined end point for one unit of work. It includes waiting, not just active effort. Because it includes waiting, it tends to expose handoff delays that a measure of "time spent working" would hide. The definition of the start and end points must be fixed and documented, or the metric drifts.

Rework is work done again because the first attempt did not meet the requirement. It can appear as a reopened case, a corrected document, a repeated approval, or a second round of clarification. Rework is a direct measure of quality failure and a hidden source of capacity loss: the time spent redoing work is time not available for new work.

These two measures complement each other. A change that cuts cycle time but raises rework is usually a bad trade, because errors move downstream and cost more to fix later. A change that reduces rework but leaves cycle time unchanged may still be worthwhile if it lowers risk and frees capacity. The strongest case is improvement on both measures without a rise in critical errors.

Metric What it captures Common trap Good companion measure
Average cycle time Overall elapsed time across cases Distorted by a few extreme cases 90th percentile cycle time
Median cycle time Typical case duration Hides the long tail Distribution or percentile view
Rework rate Share of cases needing redo Depends on how "redo" is defined Reason codes for rework
Queue length Work waiting at a step Reflects arrivals as well as capacity Waiting time per step
Edit distance Amount of reviewer correction Sensitive to formatting changes Substantive-error count
Completion rate Share of cases finished Ignores quality of completion Rework rate

Practical tip: When a number looks suspicious, ask how it was defined before asking whether it is good. Most measurement disputes are definition disputes in disguise.

When a spreadsheet or rule-based tool is the better answer

Part of using an assistant well is knowing when not to use it. Many tasks have a deterministic structure: a fixed calculation, a lookup against a table, a threshold check, or a simple conditional rule. For these, a spreadsheet formula or a rule-based automation is faster, cheaper, and more predictable than a generative tool.

A spreadsheet is usually the better choice when the logic is explicit, the data is structured, the volume is moderate, and the user needs to inspect and adjust the rules directly. A rule-based tool is better when the same decision must be applied consistently at high volume with an audit trail. An assistant is better when the input is messy or unstructured, the output requires language, or the task needs judgment that cannot be reduced to a rule.

The table below summarizes the decision signals and the recommended action — a consolidated view you can apply when triaging process steps.

Observed signal Likely interpretation Recommended action
Work waits longest at one approval step Approval is the bottleneck Add capacity or remove a redundant approval before automating anything
Same handoff repeatedly needs clarification Receiving role lacks context Standardize the handoff packet; consider a checklist
A check produces no decisions anyone acts on Step has no protective value Remove the step rather than automate it
High volume of repetitive text drafting Augmentation candidate Pilot assistant-generated first draft with human review
Decision follows a fixed, written rule Deterministic task Use a spreadsheet formula or rule-based tool
Frequent corrections trace to missing fields Upstream data quality problem Fix the input source; do not paper over it downstream
One role is overloaded across many steps Capacity imbalance Rebalance ownership before adding tools
Errors cluster in a single complex case type Edge case not handled Add explicit exception rules and test them in the pilot
Output is rarely read by anyone Documentation or reporting waste Question whether the artifact is needed at all
Change is hard to describe in words Underlying process is unclear Re-map the process before drafting documentation

The exam rewards this kind of discrimination. A scenario describing a fixed calculation is steering toward a spreadsheet, not an assistant. A scenario describing messy narrative input and a need for a written summary is steering toward augmentation with review.

Exam note: "Can the assistant do this?" and "should the assistant do this?" are different questions. The second one weighs determinism, cost, risk, and auditability, not just feasibility.

Worked scenario

A shared services team processes intake requests. Each request arrives as a message with attached notes, is summarized by a coordinator, reviewed by a team lead, and then passed to a delivery role that acts on it. The team is considering using an assistant to write the summaries. Rather than starting with the tool, the coordinator follows a disciplined sequence.

First, the team frames the question narrowly: "Can we shorten the time from request arrival to delivery without increasing errors?" That phrasing sets both a goal and a guardrail. A second goal, "reduce the coordinator's typing," would be too narrow because it might be achieved while the overall process stays slow.

Next, the coordinator maps the current process using recorded timestamps and short conversations with each role. The map shows four steps and three handoffs. Waiting is not evenly distributed: requests sit, on average, more than a day between the review step and the delivery step, while active work at each step is under an hour. A second signal appears in the message history — the delivery role repeatedly asks for context that already exists in the notes but was not carried into the summary.

The coordinator asks the assistant to help interpret the map and to propose candidates. The assistant identifies two distinct issues: a handoff that loses context, and a long wait before delivery. It also points out that the coordinator's manual summarization, while tedious, is not the largest source of delay. That distinction changes the plan.

The team evaluates three options. Automating the summary alone would speed one small step and leave the long wait untouched. Adding a structured handoff packet would address the repeated clarification requests. Changing the review-to-delivery trigger from a batch at the end of the day to a continuous handoff would attack the largest wait. The team chooses the handoff packet and the continuous trigger as the first improvement, with summary drafting as a second, separately measured change.

Before rolling anything out, the team records a baseline: median cycle time, ninetieth percentile cycle time, rework rate, and the number of clarification messages per case. They write a pilot plan modeled on the one earlier in this chapter, with twenty consecutive cases, explicit success and stop criteria, and the same metric definitions before and during the trial. The assistant drafts the new handoff checklist and the "what changed" note; the team lead verifies each checklist item against the map and removes two items the assistant invented.

During the pilot, median cycle time falls substantially, the clarification messages nearly disappear, and rework stays flat. One complex case exposes a gap: the new checklist has no field for a known exception, so the delivery role still has to ask. The team adds an exception field, revises the procedure, and runs a second short pilot before expanding to other queues.

The outcome is instructive. The assistant was valuable as a research aide, a mapping analyst, and a documentation drafter — but the biggest gain came from restructuring a handoff, which is a process change, not a tool change. The team avoided the trap of automating the most visible annoyance while ignoring the true constraint.

Common mistakes

  • Automating a step before mapping the process, which preserves confusion at higher speed.
  • Treating an assistant's fluent summary as verified evidence instead of a lead to check.
  • Improving a step that is not the bottleneck and expecting overall throughput to change.
  • Measuring only time spent working and missing the waiting time between handoffs.
  • Declaring a pilot successful on participant enthusiasm without a baseline or performance data.
  • Changing metric definitions between the baseline and the pilot, making comparison meaningless.
  • Asking the assistant to draft a procedure without checking it against the agreed process map.
  • Choosing a generative tool for a fixed calculation that a spreadsheet would handle more predictably.
  • Ignoring the long tail of cases by reporting only the average cycle time.
  • Skipping stop criteria, so a struggling pilot continues long after it should have been revised.

Chapter summary

  • An assistant is a research partner that helps frame, gather, and structure evidence; the human owns verification and the final decision.
  • Source discipline means tracking provenance, favoring primary sources, and checking the claims that carry the most weight.
  • Research becomes useful when converted into a plan with observable milestones, role-based owners, dependencies, and checkpoints.
  • Map the process before automating it, including waiting time and informal workarounds at handoffs.
  • Bottlenecks are found from signals such as growing queues, long idle periods, and repeated clarification — not from intuition.
  • Each step should be judged as an augmentation candidate, a removal candidate, or best left unchanged; removal often beats acceleration.
  • A credible pilot needs a baseline, a representative small sample, fixed metric definitions, and predefined success and stop criteria.
  • Cycle time and rework are complementary measures; improving one while worsening the other is usually a poor trade.
  • Assistant-drafted documentation is efficient but must be verified against the process map, and checklists should be specific and observable.
  • Deterministic, structured tasks usually belong in a spreadsheet or rule-based tool, not a generative assistant.

Checkpoint questions

  1. A team wants to shorten a slow process. What should they do first, and why?

  2. Which of the following is the strongest sign that a step is a bottleneck rather than merely unpopular? - A. The step is disliked by the people who perform it. - B. Work accumulates before the step while the step itself is actively occupied only briefly each day. - C. The step involves a large amount of text. - D. The step was recently added to the process.

  3. A review step produces a report that nobody reads and triggers no decisions. What is the best response? - A. Use an assistant to generate the report faster. - B. Add more reviewers to the step. - C. Remove the step after confirming it serves no current requirement. - D. Increase the detail in the report.

  4. Which pilot design is most likely to produce a trustworthy result? - A. Compare results to a baseline collected with different metric definitions. - B. Include only the easiest cases to show a clean improvement. - C. Record success criteria after seeing the results. - D. Use a representative small sample, fixed definitions, and predefined success and stop criteria.

  5. A task applies a fixed written rule to a structured table of numbers at high volume. What is the most appropriate tool choice? - A. A generative assistant, because it can handle any task involving text and numbers. - B. A spreadsheet formula or a rule-based tool, because the logic is deterministic. - C. A longer conversation with an assistant to reason through each row. - D. A manual review with no tool, because automation is never appropriate.

  6. During a pilot, cycle time drops by 25% but rework rises sharply. What is the best interpretation? - A. The change is a clear success because the primary metric improved. - B. The change should be expanded immediately to capture the speed gain. - C. The trade-off is suspicious; errors likely moved downstream and the change needs revision. - D. Rework is irrelevant once cycle time improves.

  7. An assistant drafts a new procedure document from a process map. What must happen before the document is adopted? - A. It should be adopted as written because the assistant structured it clearly. - B. It should be checked item by item against the agreed process map and corrected. - C. It should be discarded because assistants cannot draft documentation. - D. It should be shortened until no verification is needed.

Answers and rationale

  1. They should first map the current process and establish a baseline before choosing any change. Mapping reveals where work actually waits, which handoffs lose context, and which steps add no value; the baseline gives them something to compare against. Starting with a tool risks accelerating the wrong step. Defining the goal and guardrail at the same time keeps later decisions anchored to overall throughput and quality rather than a narrow sub-goal.

  2. B. Accumulating work before a step, combined with only brief active occupancy at the step, is the classic signature of a capacity constraint limiting the whole flow. Option A describes dissatisfaction, which does not establish a throughput limit. Option C describes a property of the content, not a constraint. Option D says nothing about current behavior; a recently added step may be efficient or irrelevant. The exam favors observable flow signals over opinion or history.

  3. C. A step whose output is never read and triggers no decisions has no current protective value, so removal is the cleanest improvement; confirmation is needed in case an unrecorded requirement exists. Option A and option D spend effort making a useless artifact better or faster. Option B adds capacity to work that produces nothing. Speeding up waste still produces waste.

  4. D. A representative sample, fixed definitions, and predefined decision rules make a comparison meaningful and reduce hindsight bias. Option A breaks comparability because different definitions measure different things. Option B selects an easy case set that will not reveal edge-case failures. Option C invites moving the goalposts after results are known. Good pilots are designed before outcomes are visible.

  5. B. Deterministic logic on structured data is handled more reliably, cheaply, and auditably by a spreadsheet formula or rule-based tool. Option A overgeneralizes; generative tools are not the default for fixed calculations. Option C wastes effort by asking a tool to reason repeatedly over a rule that can be applied once. Option D rejects automation unnecessarily when a simple deterministic tool is clearly suitable. Feasibility is not the same as suitability.

  6. C. A large speed gain paired with a sharp rise in rework suggests the change is pushing errors downstream, where they cost more to correct; the net effect on capacity and quality may be negative. Option A and option B focus on a single metric and ignore the guardrail. Option D wrongly dismisses rework, which is a direct measure of quality failure and hidden capacity loss. The correct response is to revise the change and retest.

  7. B. Assistant-drafted documentation can invent plausible steps that were never agreed, so each item must be checked against the source map before adoption. Option A trusts fluency as accuracy. Option C rejects a genuinely useful drafting capability. Option D confuses brevity with correctness; a short document can still contain wrong steps. Verification against the agreed process is the controlling requirement.

End of chapter 12.

An assistant does not arrive in an organization as an abstract capability. It arrives inside somebody's working day, at a particular step, next to a particular decision. Whether that step becomes faster, steadier, or unrecognizable depends less on the assistant than on the design choices made around it. This chapter covers those choices: how to add help without unsettling a process that works, how to recognize when the process itself needs redrawing, how to learn cheaply before committing, and how to describe the result in language a decision-maker can act on.

The exam treats workflow design as judgment: choose a proportionate change, protect the places where mistakes matter, and report evidence without exaggeration.

Learning objectives

  • Distinguish augmentation of a workflow from redesign of its structure, and justify the choice with evidence.
  • Identify signals that the current process no longer fits and that redesign should be considered.
  • Choose a prototyping approach matched to the question being asked, and build a useful test set.
  • Design and interpret a sample-question test for a parent or customer FAQ flow.
  • Decide where generated content enters a process and where a human must approve it.
  • Communicate value in terms of time, quality, and risk using measured evidence.
  • Explain limits and handle questions about jobs, accuracy, and policy without overpromising.
  • Assemble a pilot readout that supports a specific decision.

Key concepts

A workflow is a chain of decisions, not a list of tasks

A workflow is a repeatable path from a trigger and an input to an output somebody accepts. It has steps, handoffs between roles, decision points, a system of record, an owner, and a measure of quality. The steps are the visible part. The gates — where a person checks, approves, rejects, or escalates — carry the trust.

An assistant changes the cost and reliability of steps. It can draft, summarize, classify, extract fields, compare an answer against a source, or route a case. Those changes ripple. If drafting becomes nearly free but approval remains a weekly batch, the queue does not disappear; it moves to approval. If classification becomes reliable, a triage step that existed to catch misrouting may no longer earn its place. The design question is not "where can the assistant write things?" but "which step just became cheaper, and what does that do to the gates around it?"

Exam note: When a question asks what to do first, look for the option that locates a specific step and its existing control. Answers that change the whole process before understanding the step are usually premature.

Augmentation: adding help without moving the walls

Augmentation means inserting assistance into one part of a process while leaving its roles, gates, systems, and accountabilities intact. A support analyst still receives the question, consults the same knowledge base, sends the answer for review, and the reviewer still decides what goes out. The only difference is that a suggested answer and its source pointers now appear at the drafting step.

This approach is easy to explain, cheap to reverse, and preserves existing controls. It also produces clean evidence: with the rest of the chain unchanged, differences in outcome trace to the inserted step. For the exam, augmentation is often correct when output is checked downstream or when evidence is needed before a larger change.

Augmentation tends to fit when:

  • Current output quality is acceptable; the problem is effort, delay, or inconsistency under load.
  • The bottleneck is a reading, drafting, summarizing, or looking-up activity with a visible output.
  • A competent reviewer already examines that output, so new content cannot travel unchecked.
  • The change is reversible and the downside of a bad draft is contained.

Its main failure mode is displaced effort. If a reviewer spends as long checking a fluent draft as writing one from scratch, nothing was saved; the work changed shape. A second failure is "automation by attachment," where the assistant is bolted on without changing anything else, so the organization captures little benefit while carrying new oversight duties. Make the draft visibly a draft, show sources beside it, and measure review time as carefully as drafting time.

Exam note: "Minimal disruption" does not mean "no change." It means the change is localized: same owners, same gates, same system of record.

Redesign: recognizing when the old shape stops making sense

Redesign changes structure: which steps exist, who owns them, their order, where handoffs occur, and where approval sits. It is justified when the old shape no longer fits the reality the assistant creates.

The signals are distinctive. Rework stays high no matter how good the generated text is, pointing to a problem in how information reaches the step. A step that used to consume most of the time becomes almost instantaneous, so a queue forms elsewhere and the old gate protects nothing useful. Handoffs exist only because people needed a second system to check something now checkable in one place. Errors trace to contradictory sources or to a sequence that asks for a decision before the facts are available. Volume, policy, or audience has changed enough that the original sequence was designed for a different world.

Evidence must precede surgery: redesign changes accountability, documentation, and training, so it should follow a demonstrated mismatch, not enthusiasm.

Dimension Augment an existing workflow Redesign the workflow
Trigger Output acceptable but slow or effortful Sequence is structurally wrong; bottleneck has moved
Scope One step changed Steps, order, handoffs, or gates changed
Ownership Largely unchanged Explicitly reassigned or redefined
Approval gate Kept where it is Moved, merged, added, or removed on evidence
Evidence needed Local timing and quality data End-to-end data, failure analysis, stakeholder input
Reversibility High; the step can be switched off Lower; retraining and new documentation required
Main risk Effort shifts to reviewers; benefit stays small Unintended gaps in control or accountability

Prototyping: buying information before buying commitment

A prototype is not a small product. It is an instrument for answering a few named questions before the organization spends real money or changes real responsibilities. It needs a hypothesis, a bounded set of cases, a fixed trial boundary, success criteria, and an exit rule.

Write a hypothesis that can be wrong: "For routine policy questions whose current guidance is in the knowledge base, the assistant can produce a draft a reviewer accepts with minor edits, so drafting can be assisted without adding review time." That names the case type, behavior, quality bar, and process consequence. "The assistant will help" cannot be tested.

Stage What happens Question it answers What it cannot answer
Desk walkthrough Fixed examples worked through on paper Does the sequence make sense at all? Real variability, user reactions
Offline replay Historical anonymized cases run through the assistant How often is output correct, and where does it fail? Live behavior, workload effects
Shadow mode Live cases get output that is never sent Does performance hold on current traffic? Whether people will use and trust it
Limited live pilot Small group uses output behind a human gate Does the whole chain work, including escalation? Full-scale load, edge populations
Staged expansion More cases admitted with monitoring Do controls hold as volume and variety grow? Long-term drift without measurement

The test set deserves care. A set of only easy, well-documented questions measures nothing. Reflect the real mix: common cases, rare legitimate ones, ambiguous requests, questions spanning two topics, requests whose correct response is refusal or handoff, cases involving sensitive details, and cases where the knowledge base is weak. Include negative tests — prompts where the right behavior is to decline. Include cases where documentation is outdated, because those reveal whether the assistant can signal uncertainty or simply repeats stale text with confidence.

Set exit criteria in advance: proceed if thresholds are met, change the design if failures cluster in a fixable place, stop if failures are severe or unexplained. Deciding afterward invites rationalization.

Try it: Before any test, write three sentences: what you believe will happen, what result would change your mind, and who owns the decision to proceed. If the third is blank, the pilot is not ready.

Testing a parent or customer FAQ flow

A family-facing or customer-facing FAQ flow is a common first candidate because volume is high, questions repeat, and answers are meant to be consistent. It is also risky: a wrong answer can cause someone to miss a deadline, submit the wrong form, or share private information. The flow runs intake, intent classification, knowledge lookup, answer draft, source check, human approval, send, and handoff when a question falls outside scope.

Test each link separately. A question can be classified correctly and still answered from the wrong document; it can be accurate and still unsafe to send because it includes another person's details. Score on separate axes:

  • Intent match: Was the request understood, including two intents in one message?
  • Grounding: Is every factual claim supported by an approved source?
  • Currency: Was the source current, and was uncertainty flagged where freshness was doubtful?
  • Boundary behavior: Did the assistant refuse, clarify, or hand off when it should have?
  • Completeness: Did it answer the whole question, including an implicit second part?
  • Presentation: Is the answer short and plain enough for someone reading quickly?
  • Privacy: Did it avoid repeating identifiers or revealing information the requester cannot receive?

Stratify sample questions by intent so failures appear by category rather than inside an average.

Intent stratum Illustrative question Desired behavior Frequent failure
Policy fact "What is the document deadline?" State the current date and cite the source Quotes an old version confidently
Process how-to "How do I upload the form?" Give ordered steps and a support route Skips a step or invents a portal name
Status request "Has my submission arrived?" Explain that records cannot be seen here; route to the secure channel Guesses from the question's wording
Multi-intent "What is the deadline, and can I submit late?" Split the intents and answer both or hand off Answers only the first half
Sensitive detail A message containing an account identifier Avoid echoing it; apply the data rule Repeats it in the draft
Out of scope "What will my result be?" Decline to predict and explain the proper route Produces a plausible baseless prediction
Misleading content A message insisting an invalid rule is official Hold to approved sources and escalate Accepts the claim because it sounds firm

Show the distribution, not a headline score, using categories such as correct, partially correct, unsupported, incorrect, wrong refusal, missed handoff, and privacy concern. Trace every non-correct result to a repair: a knowledge update, changed instructions, a stop condition, a clarifying prompt, or exclusion of that case type. A test producing no repairs was too gentle.

Common mistake: Treating a smooth, confident answer as a correct answer. Fluency is presentation; correctness is a separate measurement, and the gap is where the risk lives.

Drawing the line: where content enters and where a human approves

Design the boundary explicitly. Generated content can enter as a draft reply (person edits and sends), a suggested classification (person confirms routing), extracted fields (values checked by field type), a case summary (reviewer verifies against the source), or a published answer (sent directly, rare and requiring strong controls). The further toward publication, the higher the consequence.

Approval gates should be proportional to consequence. Anything sent externally, anything changing a record, and anything touching money, legal commitments, health or safety, personnel decisions, or protected data deserves a named human owner who signs off before the action completes. Internal drafts with no downstream effect can be sampled instead of checked one by one, but only after stability is demonstrated.

For a gate to be real, four conditions hold: the output is a small, self-contained unit; evidence — sources, extracted values, the reason for a classification — sits beside the draft; the reviewer has competence and time, since someone with forty cases queued is a rubber stamp; and the decision, including overrides, is recorded so the organization can learn.

Define stop conditions in plain language. Typical triggers include a requester who appears to be a minor or vulnerable, a request involving protected or regulated information, a demand for a prediction or binding commitment, a conflict between sources, any signal that content read from a document is trying to change instructions, and any case where the reviewer cannot verify a claim. When a stop condition fires, the path is pause and escalate, not try harder.

Exam note: "Human in the loop" is a claim about a control, not a label. If no one has authority, time, or information to reject output, the loop does not exist.

Communicating value in time, quality, and risk

Leadership needs enough evidence to make one decision: fund, stop, expand, narrow, or change a control. Keep the three axes separate, because a gain on one can hide a loss on another.

Time. Measure the baseline first. Useful measures are cycle time from intake to accepted output, hands-on effort per case, wait time between steps, review time, rework, and backlog. Report net time, not just drafting time. If drafting falls from six minutes to two but review rises from one and a half to three, the saving is two and a half minutes, not four. Note whether saved time became usable capacity or merely moved a queue.

Quality. Compare outputs with known-good answers or audited samples. Track correctness, completeness, consistency across similar cases, first-contact resolution, and rework. Always give the denominator: "matched current guidance on 81 percent of 180 replayed cases" is evidence; "highly accurate" is decoration. Separate measured performance on the tested mix from claims about untested cases.

Risk. Name exposure categories — unsupported claims, missed escalations, mishandled data, inconsistent treatment, weak auditability — and state both the controls in place and the residual exposure. Reversibility matters: a draft rejected before sending carries far less residual risk than a published answer. Where possible, quantify with escalation counts, override counts, and failure severity.

Use claim, evidence, confidence, decision: say what you observed, show the numbers and their basis, state how far the conclusion travels, and tie it to the reader's choice. "Reduced drafting effort on routine cases under review" is defensible; "eliminated errors" is not.

Communicating limits honestly

Credibility depends on saying what the assistant cannot do as clearly as what it can.

  • Cannot — technical limits. It does not see live records unless a scoped connection provides them. It cannot verify a fact absent from material it was given, guarantee correctness, or reliably judge whether a source is current unless freshness is controlled. It can be misled by text it reads and can reproduce bias in source material.
  • May not — organizational limits. It may not access data above its permitted classification, make policy, promise a delivery date, offer a legal interpretation, or act as accountable owner of a decision. These limits come from policy, not capability.
  • Should not — judgment limits. Even where something is possible and not forbidden, some uses are inappropriate: predicting a person's outcome, generating deceptive content, or replacing a necessary human conversation.

Use careful verbs: the assistant drafts; the institution decides; it suggests a category; a person confirms it. State what was not tested beside what was. Honest limits are a control, because users who understand the boundary over-rely less.

Exam note: If an answer choice claims the assistant can verify anything, guarantee accuracy, or take responsibility, treat it as wrong. Verification and accountability remain human or organizational functions.

Handling predictable questions

"Will this cost jobs?" Answer factually and without speculation. Describe the change in task mix: routine drafting decreases, review and exception handling increase. Say that a pilot cannot support staffing conclusions and that workforce decisions belong to the designated planning process. Do not promise nothing will change; do not dismiss the concern as resistance.

"How accurate is it?" Give the measured result with conditions and denominator, then explain the gate. "On a replay of 180 past cases, four in five drafts matched current guidance; failures clustered in two outdated documents since corrected; all external replies still require human approval" is honest and useful. Add the failure path: a bad draft is caught, corrected, logged, and used to improve the test set. Accuracy is a number plus a boundary plus a failure path.

"Is this allowed under policy?" Do not improvise policy in a chat. Name the authoritative document and the role that owns its interpretation, state the control currently applied, and route the question to that owner. Record the answer so later inquiries receive the same response. Where disclosure is required, say it will happen through the approved channel.

A short response bank reviewed by the relevant owners keeps answers consistent.

Building the pilot readout

The readout is the bridge between a pilot and a decision. Its job is not to impress but to let a reader who attended none of the meetings reach a defensible conclusion. Put the decision requested near the top and keep the main body short, with detail in an appendix.

PILOT READOUT OUTLINE

1. Decision requested: approve / extend / narrow / stop, with exact scope
2. Why the pilot ran: hypothesis, workflow step, problem addressed
3. Scope and baseline: case types, volume, exclusions, comparison source
4. Method: stage, sample size, case selection, who reviewed
5. Results by axis: time (net), quality (with denominator), risk (residual)
6. Failure catalogue: category, count, cause, repair and owner
7. Stakeholder concerns: concern, evidence, response or remaining action
8. Limits: what was not tested — cases, populations, conditions
9. Cost and effort: setup, review burden, training, upkeep, ownership
10. Recommendation and options: preferred path, alternatives, revisit triggers
11. Open questions: each with an owner and a next step
12. Appendix: rubric, sample questions, raw counts, control map

Separate observation from interpretation: "four drafts repeated an identifier" is an observation; "the risk is acceptable" is a judgment. Put failures, uncertainty, and costs in the main body. A readout of successes only invites suspicion about omissions.

Stakeholder Typical concern Evidence to present
Senior leadership Net benefit, decision risk Net time, quality trend, cost model, options
Frontline staff Workload, changing job content Review-time data, overrides, feedback, task mix
Compliance or legal Policy adherence, auditability Control map, escalation log, recorded approvals
Security or IT Access and data flows Connector scope, permission list, logging notes
Families or customers Correct answer, access to a person FAQ test results, handoff statistics, human route
Finance Cost per case and upkeep Setup and review effort, maintenance owner, volume sensitivity

Worked scenario

A family support desk handles roughly nine hundred questions a week, mostly about deadlines, document submission, and process steps; about half are repeats. A team lead proposes using an assistant to draft replies. An analyst records a baseline: nine minutes median handling time, six of them locating guidance and writing, and a three percent sampled error rate in audited replies.

The first decision is scope. Nothing is structurally broken: answers are generally correct and a reviewer already checks every external reply. The team chooses augmentation. The assistant sits after intake and suggests a draft with source pointers. Intake, the knowledge base, the reviewer gate, and the system of record stay where they are.

The hypothesis is narrow: for routine questions whose current guidance is in the knowledge base, a reviewer should accept the draft with minor edits and should not need more review time than today. The prototype is an offline replay of 180 anonymized closed cases, stratified to include common questions, rare legitimate ones, multi-intent messages, sensitive details, and cases relying on older documents.

The results are mixed in a useful way. Eighty-one percent of drafts matched current guidance, twelve percent were partially correct, and seven percent were wrong. Every wrong answer traces to one of two causes: three stale knowledge documents, or a question containing two intents where the draft answered only the first. Drafting time falls from about six minutes to two; review time rises from one and a half to two and a half because reviewers check sources. The net saving is three minutes per case, not four. One draft repeats an account identifier into a reply. The reviewer catches it, but the team converts it into a stop condition rather than treating it as a near miss to forget.

The boundary is now explicit. Output remains a draft; no generated text goes directly to a family. Replies with account-specific details trigger a mandatory check of the secure-channel route. Multi-intent questions are split before drafting. The knowledge coordinator updates the three stale documents before any live use. Sample-based review is rejected for now because the failure rate is not yet stable.

The readout requests one decision: approve a limited live pilot with mandatory review for routine cases only. It presents the net three-minute saving, the 81-percent figure with its denominator, the cause analysis, and residual risk of a missed escalation. It states what was not tested: peak-volume weeks, languages other than the primary one, and cases needing specialist escalation. It lists setup cost and ongoing review burden, not only savings.

Anticipated questions are handled directly. On jobs, the team lead describes a shift in task mix and says a replay supports no staffing conclusion; workforce planning owns that conversation. On accuracy, the analyst gives the measured figure with its boundary and explains the review gate. On policy, the compliance owner confirms the data-handling rule, and the response bank points later inquiries to the current policy document.

The decision is to proceed with the narrow pilot. The team also records a contingency: if wrong answers remain frequent after the knowledge fixes, it will not simply add more review. It will reconsider the design — for example, restricting the assistant to routing while keeping policy answers entirely human-authored. That is the difference between redesign based on evidence and redesign based on mood.

Common mistakes

  • Starting with the tool and looking for a step to attach it to.
  • Calling a pilot successful because the output reads well, without measuring against a source.
  • Measuring drafting time while ignoring the time reviewers spend checking drafts.
  • Building a test set from easy questions that flatter the system.
  • Treating a confident answer as a grounded answer.
  • Leaving an approval gate that nobody has the authority or time to operate.
  • Publishing pilot percentages as guarantees about all case types.
  • Answering jobs, accuracy, or policy questions from memory instead of from owners and documents.
  • Redesigning before evidence shows that the structure, not the execution, is the problem.

Chapter summary

  • A workflow is steps plus trust gates; an assistant changes step costs and can move the bottleneck.
  • Augmentation changes one step while keeping roles, gates, and systems intact.
  • Redesign changes structure and follows evidence that the old sequence no longer fits.
  • Prototypes begin with a falsifiable hypothesis and match fidelity to the question.
  • Test sets include common cases, edge cases, multi-intent requests, sensitive details, refusals, and stale sources.
  • FAQ testing scores intent, grounding, currency, boundaries, completeness, presentation, and privacy separately.
  • Generated content enters as a draft or suggestion by default; mandatory approval precedes external or high-consequence actions.
  • Value is reported as measured time, quality, and risk, with denominators and net effects.
  • Limits fall into cannot, may not, and should not; verification and accountability stay human.
  • A pilot readout leads with the decision requested and includes failures, costs, untested areas, and owners.

Checkpoint questions

  1. A support process produces correct replies, but analysts spend most of their time finding guidance and writing drafts. A reviewer already checks every reply before it is sent. What is the most proportionate first change?

A. Redesign intake, approval, and routing at the same time. B. Insert a suggested draft with source pointers at the drafting step and keep the reviewer gate. C. Allow the assistant to send replies once its drafts look fluent. D. Focus only on prompt wording and ignore workflow design.

  1. Which observation most strongly suggests redesign rather than augmentation?

A. Drafts need minor wording edits before acceptance. B. Review time increases slightly because reviewers check sources. C. Drafting becomes almost instantaneous and cases pile up at a weekly approval batch. D. A pilot uses a smaller sample than the full population.

  1. A team wants the cheapest early check of whether a proposed sequence makes sense before using real cases. Which approach fits?

A. A staged expansion to all case types. B. A desk walkthrough using a fixed set of worked examples. C. A live pilot with no human gate. D. Publishing generated answers and collecting complaints.

  1. Which sample set would best test a family-facing FAQ flow?

A. Only the twenty most frequently asked questions. B. Only questions answered correctly in an earlier trial. C. A mix of common and rare questions, multi-intent requests, sensitive details, refusals, and stale-source cases. D. Only questions written in perfect, unambiguous language.

  1. What makes a human approval gate meaningful?

A. The word "reviewed" appears in the process diagram. B. A reviewer has competence, time, evidence, and authority to reject output, and the decision is recorded. C. The assistant is instructed to say that a person is in the loop. D. Review happens monthly for a random sample, regardless of case type.

  1. A pilot cut drafting from six minutes to two, but review rose from one and a half minutes to three. How should this be reported?

A. Report a four-minute saving and leave review out of the figure. B. Report the net three-minute saving with conditions and denominator, and note that capacity gains are unproven. C. Report only the percentage improvement in drafting speed. D. Report that the assistant eliminated the review problem.

  1. A colleague asks, "Will this replace staff?" Which response is most appropriate?

A. Promise that no role will ever change as a result of the pilot. B. Say the concern is unfounded and move to efficiency metrics. C. Describe the shift in task mix, state that a pilot cannot support staffing conclusions, and point to the workforce planning process. D. Guarantee that productivity gains will outweigh any staffing impact.

Answers and rationale

  1. Correct: B. The process is accurate and already has a trusted gate, so the localized change is to assist drafting while preserving review. A is wrong because simultaneous redesign of several stages is disproportionate without evidence of structural failure and makes the effect impossible to isolate. C is wrong because removing the gate exposes an external audience to unverified output before any evidence supports it. D is wrong because prompt wording cannot settle where assistance belongs or how it is controlled.

  2. Correct: C. When a formerly expensive step becomes nearly free and work accumulates at a later gate, the bottleneck has moved and the old structure protects the wrong thing. A is a normal effect of edits and does not indicate structural failure. B is an expected cost to measure, not proof the design is obsolete. D concerns the pilot's precision, not the workflow's shape.

  3. Correct: B. A desk walkthrough with fixed examples is the cheapest way to test whether a sequence is coherent before touching live cases. A is a later stage that assumes the design already survived testing. C creates avoidable risk and answers a different question. D exposes real people to errors and yields unreliable evidence.

  4. Correct: C. A representative mix exposes the failures that matter, including rare cases, multiple intents, sensitive information, correct refusals, and unreliable knowledge. A misses edge-case behavior. B is biased toward flattering results. D excludes the vague, shortened requests that arrive in practice.

  5. Correct: B. A gate is a control only when a capable person has the information and authority to reject output and the decision is recorded. A changes nothing in practice. C is an assurance in wording, not a control. D ignores case risk and cannot catch problems before external actions.
