Answers and rationale

Practice Paper A

A1. B — Judgment, safe use, and clear communication are the emphasis; dates and shortcuts are trivia.

A2. B — A project space preserves knowledge and instructions across days; isolated chats and dictation do not.

A3. A — Navigation panel, conversation area, and feature controls are interface parts; the rest are unrelated systems.

A4. B — Client-bound chat output is a work product and must be checked; blind trust and total bans are extremes.

A5. C — Artifacts are separate, iterable objects for documents and code; short replies belong inline.

A6. B — Controls set response-wide preferences; the prompt states the particular task.

A7. A — Recurring summaries need a scheduled task; one-off messages and dictation do not repeat.

A8. B — Memory can retain details with privacy implications, so use it deliberately rather than casually.

A9. C — Audience, length, tone, and structure make the request actionable; the other options are vague.

A10. B — Delimit untrusted text and label it data; blending it invites the assistant to follow it as commands.

A11. A — A positive example shows the target detail; a negative instruction alone leaves it undefined.

A12. B — A few representative examples teach the pattern without the noise of irrelevant or excessive ones.

A13. B — Blended steps hide where errors entered; separation makes failures isolatable and repairable.

A14. B — Each chained step consumes the previous output; that is what makes ordering useful.

A15. C — A named paragraph and a named addition are actionable; vague or destructive feedback is not.

A16. A — Parallel work needs independent outputs; a dependency forces order.

A17. B — Both sides plus the largest uncertainty support a decision; one-sided framing hides the trade-off.

A18. B — A research mode gathers and cites sources, and verification closes the remaining gap.

A19. B — Early ideation benefits from breadth before evaluation, not a demand for immediate perfection.

A20. B — Draft, critique, revise separates creation from evaluation and strengthens the final version.

A21. B — An unsupported figure needs a source check; blind trust and blanket bans are not proportionate.

A22. B — Confident fabrication is hallucination; refusal, brevity, and formatting faults are separate issues.

A23. A — Answering only from supplied material and flagging gaps grounds claims; guessing and padding do not.

A24. A — Accuracy asks if statements are true; completeness asks if all required elements are present.

A25. B — Contradiction calls for checking inputs and reconciling through review, not confidence or chance.

A26. A — The review boundary marks decisions needing human judgment before action, not a technical setting.

A27. B — Explicit criteria can be checked for relevance and fairness; an unexplained ranking hides bias.

A28. B — Steady prompts, constraints, and sources isolate variation; randomising makes consistency unjudgeable.

A29. B — Plain language, a short glossary, and examples adapt technical content without losing substance.

A30. B — Format instructions shape structure such as tables or word limits, not the model or truth.

A31. B — A word cap and fixed point count are measurable; "short-ish" gives no usable target.

A32. B — Specific, criterion-based changes applied singly show their effect; vague or emotional feedback does not.

A33. B — A low-stakes task suits a faster adequate model; the largest option wastes resources.

A34. B — A context window is finite, so long inputs may need chunking or selective inclusion.

A35. A — Restating key context in a fresh session or project restores continuity better than unrelated turns.

A36. B — Balance task fit, latency, cost, and data sensitivity, then test; one-factor optimisation is shortsighted.

A37. B — Sensitive records need a sensitivity and permissions check before a connector is enabled.

A38. A — The phrase joins feasibility with appropriateness and authorisation, not speed or length.

A39. B — Employment use keeps a human accountable and records the assistant's role; automation removes oversight.

A40. B — Escalate when authority, policy, or confidence is exceeded, not for routine or quick tasks.

Practice Paper B

B1. B — A first pass with flagging protects time for confident items and returns to uncertain ones later.

B2. A — Team plans typically add administrative controls and shared data-handling settings.

B3. A — Voice dictation suits hands-free capture, but transcription errors require a quick accuracy check.

B4. B — Work products carry verification and citation duties that casual chats do not.

B5. B — An artifact is a separate object that can be opened, edited, and revisited.

B6. B — Sandboxed execution supports calculation and transformation, not secrets, bypasses, or replacing review.

B7. B — A skill is packaged instructions for a task type, not hardware, a certificate, or a format.

B8. B — Stale project knowledge should be updated or removed; renaming or adding files does not fix it.

B9. A — The five parts are role or frame, task, constraints, format, and examples.

B10. B — Prominent placement and a lean prompt keep the key instruction visible.

B11. B — Excess detail constrains the assistant unnecessarily and does not speed work or ensure accuracy.

B12. B — Separate critique exposes weaknesses before revision without changing the model or promising perfection.

B13. B — Inspecting the intermediate output finds the earliest failing step faster than a full rewrite.

B14. B — A version log shows what changed and whether quality improved; it does not replace testing.

B15. A — A checkpoint verifies an intermediate result before downstream reuse, stopping early errors from spreading.

B16. B — Step count follows real subtasks and dependencies, not word count or file names.

B17. B — A table with task, owner role, due date, and source line is actionable and traceable.

B18. B — Stated assumptions expose hidden premises a reviewer can test; they do not remove uncertainty.

B19. A — Explicit constraints plus several options with trade-offs support grounded ideation.

B20. B — Map the current process and its friction points before proposing any redesign.

B21. B — An unlocatable citation may be fabricated, so verify or remove it rather than accept or pad.

B22. A — Authoritative sources plus an answer-only-from-them instruction is the strongest grounding practice.

B23. B — Fluency is a surface quality and no guarantee of truth, so verification is still required.

B24. B — Correcting and recording the change supports prevention; concealment, blame, and abandonment do not.

B25. B — Skew calls for examining framing and assumptions plus an independent check, not acceptance or amplification.

B26. B — A style instruction and normalisation pass fix uneven tone while preserving accurate content.

B27. B — The human who reviews and approves retains accountability; tools and automation do not.

B28. B — Effective review needs domain context, authority, time, and access to sources.

B29. B — Leading with the conclusion and using plain language serves executives while keeping detail available.

B30. A — Verbosity controls detail and length, not training data, permissions, or connection speed.

B31. B — Tailored versions keep verified facts constant while adjusting framing for each audience.

B32. B — A table matches reader needs and eases comparison; it does not guarantee correctness.

B33. B — Chunking, selective context, and a capable model meet both length and reasoning demands.

B34. B — Greater capability can cost time or resources, so match models to difficulty.

B35. B — Summarising decisions into a fresh session or persistent project restores continuity.

B36. B — Test against the real task while weighing quality, speed, cost, and data handling.

B37. B — Confidential data needs a policy and permission check plus authorisation before sharing.

B38. A — Governance is the policies, roles, and checks that steer responsible use.

B39. B — Regulated use keeps a human accountable, documents the process, and applies policy.

B40. B — "Should" concerns appropriateness, authorisation, and harm; "can" concerns mere feasibility.

End of chapter 18.

Learning objectives

  • Convert an underspecified request into a precise, testable instruction without changing the requester's intent.
  • Apply a repeatable verification pass to every answer before it is forwarded or acted upon.
  • Choose the smallest fitting assistant feature and model tier for a given task, and explain the trade-off.
  • Distinguish what a tool can do from what it should do in a given context.
  • Diagnose common failure patterns in generated answers and apply a targeted repair.
  • Recognize the scenario patterns that recur in the exam and respond with a defensible sequence of steps.

This chapter is a set of clinics rather than a survey of new material. A clinic takes an ordinary, slightly messy situation, names what is wrong with it, and shows the corrected move. The four skills below are the ones that decide most scenario questions: making a request clear, checking the answer, selecting the right capability, and judging appropriateness while repairing defects. Each appears on the exam in disguise, so the goal is to recognize the shape of the problem rather than memorize a script.

A single rhythm ties the clinics together. Before you accept any task, clarify what is being asked; choose a capability that fits; produce the answer; then verify and repair it. Keep that loop visible in your mind even when a question seems simple, because the exam frequently hides a verification or suitability problem inside an otherwise ordinary request.

REUSABLE FOUR-STEP CHECKLIST

1. CLARIFY  — Restate the goal, audience, format, constraints, and a test for success.
2. CHOOSE   — Pick the smallest feature and model tier that can do the job well.
3. PRODUCE  — Ask for the answer, requesting reasoning or sources where useful.
4. VERIFY   — Check correctness, scope, and safety; repair before sending.

Exam note: When two answers differ only in whether verification happens, the one that includes a check before use is almost always the stronger choice.

Key concepts

Each clinic below follows the same pattern: a starting situation, a diagnosis, a corrected approach, and a short drill with answers. Treat the four as one continuous workflow rather than independent tricks.

From fuzzy request to precise instruction

Starting situation. A team lead says, "Get me something on our quarterly numbers." An analyst pastes that line into an assistant and receives a long, generic essay about the industry. Nothing in the answer is wrong, yet nothing is usable. The request named a topic but not a decision, an audience, or a format.

Diagnosis. A vague request leaves the assistant to guess. Guessing is not a defect in the tool; it is the expected result when essential parameters are missing. The four parameters that most often go missing are context (what situation this sits in), role (whose voice and depth), deliverable (a summary, a comparison, a draft message, a table), and success test (what a good answer must contain or avoid). When any of these is absent, the assistant fills the gap with plausible defaults, and plausible defaults rarely match a specific business need.

A second issue is hidden intent. "Something on our quarterly numbers" may actually mean "identify which product line declined and draft three sentences I can paste into a status update." The literal request and the real goal are different. Correcting the wording without uncovering the goal produces a tidy answer to the wrong question.

Corrected approach. Rewrite the request so that a colleague could answer it without asking follow-up questions. State the decision the answer must support, the audience, the required shape, and any boundaries such as length, tone, or data the assistant must not invent. If the underlying goal is uncertain, ask one focused clarifying question rather than guessing. Silence is not agreement; when the brief could reasonably mean two different things, a single question is faster than a wrong deliverable.

The table below shows how a rough request is tightened. Notice that the correction adds constraints; it does not add a new topic.

Vague element What is missing Tightened version
"something on numbers" Deliverable and decision "a three-bullet summary of revenue by product line"
"our quarterly" Context and source "using the figures in the attached table for the last quarter"
no audience Voice and depth "written for a non-financial manager"
no boundary Scope and honesty "do not estimate any figure that is absent; say it is missing"

Here is the tightened instruction as a reusable pattern. It is deliberately plain; clarity does not require elaborate phrasing, only complete information.

Context:     We are preparing a status update for non-financial managers.
Task:        Summarize revenue by product line in three bullets.
Source:      Use only the figures in the supplied table.
Constraints: No invented numbers; flag any missing figure; neutral tone.
Success:     A manager could paste the bullets into the update unchanged.

Try it: Before sending any important request, read it once and ask, "Could two different people interpret this differently?" If yes, tighten it first.

Drill. An analyst receives: "Check this policy and tell me if it is fine." Name the two fastest clarifications that would turn this into a usable task, and explain why each matters.

Drill answers: First, clarify the standard — "fine" for compliance, clarity, tone, or length are different tests, and naming the test determines which issues count as problems. Second, clarify the deliverable — a pass or fail verdict with reasons, a list of problems, or a rewritten version produce very different work. Both matter because they remove the two largest guesses: what "good" means and what form the answer takes. Without them, any answer the assistant gives is a guess about the analyst's intent.

Inspecting the answer before you send it

Starting situation. A support agent asks an assistant to draft a reply to a customer about a delayed shipment. The draft is warm, fluent, and confident. It also promises a delivery date the company has not confirmed and cites a policy that changed last month. The agent copies it into the message and sends it. Two follow-ups later, the error is obvious and the agent must walk it back.

Diagnosis. Fluent writing is not evidence of correctness. An answer can be well-structured, grammatical, and completely wrong on the facts that matter. This is the verification gap: the moment between receiving a draft and using it is skipped. Three specific risks hide in that gap. First, plausible fabrication — details that fit the pattern but were never supplied. Second, staleness — facts that were once true but have changed. Third, scope drift — the answer quietly addresses a nearby question rather than the one asked.

The exam tests this by offering an option that says, in effect, "send the draft as written because it reads well." That option fails on principle. Polish is a presentation quality, not a correctness check.

Corrected approach. Run a short, fixed inspection. Compare every factual claim to an authoritative source available to you. Check that each requirement from the clarified request appears in the answer. Look for details that were not in your inputs — dates, names, numbers, references — and treat them as unverified until confirmed. Finally, judge tone and audience fit. If the answer fails any check, repair it before use rather than merely appending a disclaimer.

The inspection is fast because it is structured. You do not reread the whole answer aimlessly; you check specific categories in order. A fixed order also prevents a common failure: spending all your attention on wording and never reaching the factual claims. Start with facts, since those carry the most risk, and end with tone, which is the cheapest to fix.

Common mistake: Adding "please verify this information" to a flawed answer and forwarding it. The reader is not the verification step; you are. A caveat does not convert an unchecked claim into a checked one, and it can even make the claim look more credible by surrounding it with caution.

A practical way to make verification stick is to ask the assistant itself for a support trail: which statements came from the supplied material, which are inferences, and which are unknown. That does not remove your responsibility, but it makes gaps visible so you can examine them quickly. When the stakes are high, cross-check against a primary record rather than trusting the assistant's own account of its sources. An assistant can describe its reasoning persuasively and still be wrong about the underlying fact.

Verification check What you look for Passing condition
Source agreement Each fact traced to supplied or authoritative material No unsupported dates, figures, or references
Requirement coverage Every constraint from the clarified task All named constraints appear
Scope Answer addresses the actual question No substitution of a nearby question
Safety and policy No commitments or claims beyond your authority Wording stays within approved bounds
Audience fit Tone and depth suit the reader Reader could act on it without translation

In a scenario question, the correct option often names one of these checks explicitly. Recognizing the category tells you whether the option is a genuine verification step or a decorative one, such as asking for a second draft without checking either version.

Drill. A draft answer states that a service "always meets its target" and quotes a response time, but the source you supplied contains only a weekly average. Identify the verification failure and the repair.

Drill answers: The failure is an unsupported absolute plus a figure that does not match the source. "Always" overstates what a weekly average can show, and the quoted response time is not present in the material. The repair is to replace the claim with the supported statement — that the source reports a weekly average — and remove or re-source the specific figure before the draft is used. This is a source-agreement failure first and a wording failure second.

Matching the feature and model to the task

Starting situation. A candidate is given a scenario: a team needs to extract dates and amounts from a batch of scanned invoices and place them in a spreadsheet. The proposed solution is to use a general conversational assistant with the largest available model, pasting each document into the chat window one at a time. The scenario asks for a better approach.

Diagnosis. The proposal treats every task as a chat task and every improvement as a bigger model. Both assumptions are costly. Chat is a poor fit for a repetitive, structured extraction over many files; a document-processing or structured-output capability is designed for that shape of work. A larger model does not compensate for using the wrong feature, and it adds latency and cost. This is a capability mismatch, not an intelligence shortfall.

The reverse error is equally common. Choosing the smallest, cheapest option for a task that needs careful reasoning or strict adherence to a long instruction saves little and produces rework. A team that routes a complex analysis to a weak tier often ends up repeating the work, correcting errors, or escalating it anyway. Selection is about fit across three axes: the nature of the task, the need for external or private information, and the tolerance for error.

There is a fourth axis that scenario questions like to test: whether the task needs a tool at all. If the answer can be looked up exactly or calculated deterministically, a language model is an unnecessary middle step. Recognizing that boundary is part of choosing well, and it is the reason the exam frames some questions around "which approach" rather than "which model."

Corrected approach. Describe the task in terms of its shape, then map that shape to a capability. Repetitive extraction of fields into a schema calls for structured output on a document pipeline. Questions about private documents call for a retrieval feature that grounds answers in those documents. Open-ended drafting or brainstorming suits a general chat interface. Multi-step analysis with strict rules suits a reasoning-oriented mode. Only after the feature is chosen does model tier matter: use a smaller, faster tier for well-bounded work and a stronger tier for multi-step reasoning, long instructions, or high-consequence answers.

The table gives a compact mapping you can reuse in scenario questions.

Task shape Fitting capability Model tier tendency
Extract fields from many documents Document processing with structured output Smaller tier is usually enough
Answer from private or changing material Retrieval grounded in a defined source set Mid tier, with source checks
Draft, brainstorm, or rewrite prose Conversational assistant Mid tier for style control
Multi-step analysis or strict rule-following Reasoning-oriented mode Stronger tier, with verification
Deterministic calculation Calculator or code, not language generation Not a model choice at all

Exam note: If a task is deterministic — arithmetic, exact lookups, rule-based validation — the best answer may be a non-generative tool. The exam rewards recognizing when a language model is the wrong instrument.

Two additional filters refine the choice. Ask whether the assistant needs information it cannot see; if so, supplying the right source through a retrieval feature is more reliable than relying on memory. Ask what happens if the answer is wrong; higher consequence justifies a stronger tier and a verification step, not a larger prompt alone.

Cost and speed enter the decision only after fit is settled. Among capabilities that can do the job, prefer the smaller tier that meets the quality bar, because it makes iteration cheaper and verification faster. That is a practical preference, not a rule that overrides accuracy; a high-consequence answer earns the stronger tier precisely because the cost of being wrong is larger than the cost of running it.

Drill. A team wants a short summary of a single paragraph that a colleague already pasted into the request. Which feature and tier are appropriate, and why?

Drill answers: A general conversational assistant at a small or mid tier is appropriate. The source is already in the request, so retrieval is unnecessary; the task is a simple transformation, so a stronger reasoning tier adds no value. Choosing the smaller fitting option is correct because the work is bounded and the cost of a wrong summary is low, leaving room for a quick human check. If the summary will be forwarded to a large audience, the same feature is still right, but the verification step becomes more important.

Can versus should, and repairing weak answers

Starting situation. An assistant is technically able to draft a message that implies a guarantee the company has not approved. A teammate argues that because the tool "can generate it," using it is efficient. Another scenario involves an assistant asked to draft an internal note that rates colleagues' performance from incomplete chat logs. Both requests are possible. Neither is obviously appropriate.

Diagnosis. Capability and appropriateness are different questions. "Can it produce the text?" is a technical fact. "Should it be produced, and by this means?" is a judgment about authority, fairness, privacy, and consequence. Conflating them is the most dangerous error in the clinics because it tends to appear in polished, confident answers. A tool that can draft a persuasive message can also overstate a promise; a tool that can summarize private conversations can also turn incomplete fragments into a pseudo-evaluation that looks authoritative.

Appropriateness depends on context: who is authorized to make the claim, whose data is involved, whether the affected people can review it, and whether a human with accountability will sign off. When any of those is unclear, the correct move is to narrow the task or escalate, not to generate and hope. The exam encodes this as the difference between an answer that merely completes the request and an answer that notices the request itself should be reshaped.

Corrected approach. Pause and ask two questions before generating: is this claim or action within my authority, and would I be comfortable if the affected person saw exactly how it was produced? If either answer is uncomfortable, constrain the request — remove the guarantee, anonymize and aggregate the data, or hand the decision to a responsible reviewer. Then produce the narrower output and verify it as before.

When an answer is already weak, repair it by category rather than by rewriting from scratch. The diagnostic table below is the shared tool for this clinic. It turns a vague sense of "this looks off" into a named problem with a first move. Naming the problem matters because different defects need different repairs: adding length helps almost nothing, while removing an invented reference fixes the answer completely.

Symptom in the answer Likely cause First corrective move
Confident citations or figures that do not exist Fabrication from plausible patterns Remove them; re-answer from supplied sources only
Correct facts in the wrong format Deliverable not specified Restate the required shape and regenerate
Vague hedging everywhere Over-caution or missing success test State the decision and the test explicitly
Refusal on a safe, ordinary task Misread intent or missing context Reframe with purpose and constraints
Compliance with an unsafe or unauthorized request "Can" mistaken for "should" Narrow scope or escalate to a reviewer
Long answer that misses the point Nearby question substituted for the real one Restate the actual goal and trim to it
Internally inconsistent numbers Reasoning drift across a long answer Recompute with a deterministic tool and align

Common mistake: Treating a refusal as a failure to be pushed through. Sometimes the boundary is correct. The right response is to examine why the task was refused and reformulate it legibly, not to insist on the same request in different words until the system gives in.

The last row of the table deserves special attention. Inconsistent numbers within one answer are a sign that the model drifted as it generated a long response. The fix is not to ask for a more careful tone but to recompute the figures with a deterministic tool and then make the prose match. This is another instance of the capability lesson: some corrections belong outside the language model entirely.

Drill. An assistant produces a flawless-looking summary of a customer complaint that recommends a refund without any policy basis. State the diagnosis and the repair.

Drill answers: The diagnosis is an unauthorized commitment produced from an incomplete basis — a "can versus should" problem, not a writing problem. The repair is to strip the recommendation, ground the summary strictly in the complaint text, and route the refund decision to the team that owns that authority. The prose may be excellent; that is precisely why the content error is easy to miss. A second acceptable observation is that the missing policy basis should be supplied or the conclusion should be flagged as unsupported.

Worked scenario

A regional manager asks for help preparing a ten-minute briefing to the leadership group on why customer satisfaction fell in the last quarter. The available material is a dashboard export with three months of scores, a folder of support notes, and a draft the assistant produced earlier. The manager wants the briefing by afternoon and says, "Just make it convincing."

The candidate begins with the clarify step. "Convincing" is not a success test; the briefing must be accurate and useful to a leadership audience. The candidate restates the request: identify the two largest drivers of the decline supported by the supplied material, present each with one figure and one anonymized example, flag any conclusion the data cannot support, and keep it to three slides. This converts a rhetorical goal into a checkable deliverable and gives the manager a clear chance to correct the interpretation before work begins.

Next comes the choose step. The scores are structured; the support notes are unstructured text. The candidate uses a document-reading capability grounded in the two supplied sources rather than asking a general assistant to recall industry trends. A mid-tier model is sufficient because the work is summarization with light pattern-finding, not deep reasoning. Deterministic percentages are computed directly from the export, not generated by the model, because exact figures should never be left to a generative step.

The produce step yields a draft with three claims. One claim attributes the decline to a policy change; the dashboard shows a timing correlation but the support notes do not confirm causation. A second claim quotes a precise satisfaction drop for a region whose sample size is small. A third claim includes a promise that a fix will ship next month, drawn from the earlier draft rather than any current source. Each claim is plausible enough that a rushed reader would accept it.

The verify pass catches all three. The causal claim is rewritten as a correlation with an explicit note that the data cannot establish cause. The regional figure is replaced with a range and a caveat about sample size. The shipping promise is removed because it is an unauthorized commitment. Each repair maps to a row in the diagnostic table: unsupported inference, unsupported precision, and authority overreach. The candidate also checks that every constraint from the clarified request appears in the final slides.

Finally, the can-versus-should check considers the support notes. They contain identifiable customer details. The briefing will use anonymized, aggregated examples only, and the candidate declines to include names even though the assistant could reproduce them from the notes. The finished briefing is accurate within its sources, appropriately cautious, and ready for the manager to deliver with an honest note about what the data does not show.

Exam note: The strongest answer in a scenario is rarely the one that generates the most text. It is the one that produces a bounded, checked, appropriately scoped result and says what was deliberately left out.

Common mistakes

  • Treating a fluent answer as a verified answer. Readability is not accuracy.
  • Choosing a larger model to compensate for the wrong feature. Fit the capability first.
  • Using a conversational tool for deterministic work such as exact calculations.
  • Asking for "more detail" when the real problem is an unclear deliverable.
  • Assuming a refusal is always a tool failure; sometimes the boundary is correct.
  • Forwarding an answer with a "please verify" note attached, which shifts your check onto the reader.
  • Allowing generated text to make commitments, guarantees, or judgments beyond your authority.
  • Repairing a weak answer by adding length instead of naming the specific defect.
  • Letting a colleague's urgency replace the clarify step; speed does not make a vague request clear.

Chapter summary

  • A vague task becomes usable when you supply context, role, deliverable, and a success test.
  • Verification is a fixed sequence: source agreement, requirement coverage, scope, safety, and audience fit.
  • Select the capability by task shape and information source, then pick the smallest fitting model tier.
  • Deterministic work belongs to deterministic tools, not to language generation.
  • "Can" is a technical fact; "should" is a judgment about authority, fairness, and consequence.
  • Diagnose bad outputs by category so the repair is targeted rather than cosmetic.
  • In every scenario, clarify, choose, produce, and verify before the answer leaves your hands.

Checkpoint questions

  1. An analyst receives the request "summarize the report." What is the best first move?

A. Ask the assistant to produce a longer summary so nothing is missed. B. Clarify the audience, length, and decision the summary must support. C. Choose the largest model to ensure the summary is accurate. D. Produce a summary and add a note asking the reader to verify it.

  1. A draft answer includes a precise date that does not appear in any supplied source. Which check catches this first?

A. Audience fit. B. Requirement coverage. C. Source agreement. D. Tone consistency.

  1. Which task best fits a document-processing capability with structured output?

A. Brainstorming names for a new product. B. Extracting dates and amounts from a batch of invoices into a table. C. Writing an encouraging note to a colleague. D. Deciding whether to approve a refund.

  1. A request is technically possible but would let a draft imply an unapproved guarantee. What should happen?

A. Generate it, since the tool can do it. B. Generate it with a disclaimer attached. C. Narrow the request to remove the guarantee or escalate to the owner of that decision. D. Ask a stronger model to phrase the guarantee more carefully.

  1. Which statement best expresses "can versus should"?

A. If an assistant can produce an answer, using it is appropriate. B. Capability answers what is possible; appropriateness answers what authority and context permit. C. Stronger models remove the need to ask whether a task is appropriate. D. Refusals always indicate a mistake in the assistant.

  1. An answer lists several impressive references that turn out not to exist. What is the likely cause and first repair?

A. Cause is over-caution; repair by adding more hedging. B. Cause is wrong model tier; repair by using a larger model. C. Cause is fabrication of plausible details; repair by removing them and re-answering from supplied sources. D. Cause is a scope drift; repair by shortening the answer.

  1. For a scenario requiring exact percentages from a spreadsheet export, which approach is strongest?

A. Ask a language model to estimate the percentages. B. Compute the percentages directly from the export and use the model only to explain them. C. Use the largest model and request step-by-step reasoning. D. Skip the numbers and describe the trend qualitatively.

Answers and rationale

  1. B. Clarifying audience, length, and purpose supplies the missing success test, which is the essence of the first clinic. A is wrong because a longer summary does not fix an undefined goal and may add noise. C is wrong because model size does not resolve ambiguity in the request. D is wrong because it skips verification and pushes that duty onto the reader.

  2. C. Source agreement traces each fact to supplied or authoritative material, so an unsupported date is caught immediately. A concerns tone and depth, not factual support. B checks that constraints appear, not that facts are grounded. D is a subset of audience fit and would not flag an invented date.

  3. B. Repetitive field extraction into a defined structure is exactly the shape document processing with structured output is built for. A is open-ended drafting, which suits a conversational tool. C is a short prose task with no structured requirement. D is a decision about authority and policy, not an extraction task.

  4. C. Narrowing the request or escalating respects that the guarantee is not yours to make; appropriateness, not capability, governs. A confuses technical ability with permission. B does not cure an unauthorized claim — a disclaimer still leaves the claim in the reader's hands. D merely polishes the same inappropriate commitment.

  5. B. This states the distinction cleanly: capability is about what a system can produce, while appropriateness is about what context, authority, and fairness allow. A is the conflation the clinic warns against. C wrongly assumes a stronger model removes judgment. D is wrong because some refusals are correct boundaries.

  6. C. Invented but plausible references are a classic fabrication pattern, and the first move is to remove them and rebuild the answer from supplied sources. A misdiagnoses the problem as excessive confidence rather than invented content. B treats a source problem as a capacity problem. D addresses focus, not falsity, so the fake references would remain.

  7. B. Deterministic figures should be computed with a deterministic tool, and the assistant used only to interpret them; this is the capability-matching principle. A and C ask a generative model to produce exact arithmetic, which invites error. D avoids the question rather than answering it.

End of chapter 19.

This chapter does not introduce a new body of knowledge. It turns everything you have studied into habits you can carry into the exam room. A foundations exam rarely rewards the candidate who can recite the most facts. It rewards the candidate who can look at a realistic situation, notice what matters, eliminate what does not, and choose the answer a careful, responsible practitioner would choose.

Treat this chapter as a workspace rather than a narrative. Return to the checklist when a practice question goes wrong, use the glossary to close vocabulary gaps, rehearse the triage rules until they feel automatic, and copy the one-page revision sheet onto a single sheet of paper.

Learning objectives

By the end of this chapter you should be able to:

  • Apply a consolidated decision checklist to any scenario question before selecting an option.
  • Recall the meaning of core terms that recur across the exam and distinguish terms that are easily confused.
  • Recognize the common ways wrong answer options are constructed, including absolute language and polished-but-wrong output.
  • Use a repeatable triage sequence to read, frame, and answer scenario questions under time pressure.
  • Resolve situations in which two answer options both appear plausible.
  • Manage time, flag uncertain items, and avoid the traps that cost marks in the final minutes.

Key concepts

A consolidated decision checklist

A checklist externalizes judgment. Under pressure, working memory narrows and it is easy to fixate on the first plausible option. A short sequence pulls attention back to the criteria that decide the answer, with earlier questions eliminating whole families of wrong options before you compare the survivors.

  1. What is being asked? Restate the stem in your own words, focusing on the decision, not the scenery. Many scenario paragraphs describe an industry, a team, or a tool in vivid detail that has no bearing on the correct choice.
  2. What is the responsible default? When the situation involves uncertainty, data, or access, the defensible answer is usually the one that verifies, minimizes, or asks before it acts. Note the constraints that matter: privacy, accuracy, permission, and auditability.
  3. Where should the fix live? Match the problem to the correct layer. A bad answer is one that solves a data problem with a wording change, or a permissions problem with a nicer prompt. This is the "wrong surface" family of distractors described later.
  4. Which options contain absolute language? Words such as always, never, all, none, only, guaranteed, and immediately are warnings. Responsible practice is full of qualified, conditional statements, so an unqualified claim is frequently a trap.
  5. Does any option grant more access than the task requires? If a task needs to read one record, an option that grants broad or administrative access is almost never the right answer, even when it would technically work.
  6. Would the chosen action detect its own mistakes? Prefer answers that include verification, review, or a check against a trusted source over answers that trust fluent output at face value.
  7. Among the survivors, pick the smallest responsible action. If two options both seem correct, the better one usually does less: fewer permissions, narrower scope, less data exposed, more verification, and a clearer record of what was done.

To make the checklist usable at speed, it helps to compress it into a situation-to-first-move map. The table below is the compact version to memorize.

Situation in the stem First move to look for Reject options that
Output sounds confident but is unverified Check against an authoritative source before relying on it Treat fluency as evidence of truth
A task needs one specific record or field Request the minimum data and the minimum permission Grant broad or standing access "to be safe"
A prompt produces unreliable results Tighten the instruction, add context, and give an example Blame the entire tool or abandon verification
Sensitive or personal data is involved Apply the approved handling rule and ask when unclear Assume internal use removes all obligations
Two answers both seem correct Compare scope, reversibility, and verification Choose on phrasing alone
A deadline is emphasized in the scenario Preserve the responsible step despite pressure Skip review because time is short
The question asks what to do first Establish the missing fact or permission Jump to a downstream action

Exam note: The checklist is not a formula that replaces understanding. It is a way to make sure that stress does not push you into answering on instinct. Apply it consciously on every practice item until the sequence becomes automatic.

Glossary of core terms

Precision with vocabulary pays directly in scenario questions, where a single word in an option can change its meaning. The table below gathers the terms that recur most often and pairs each with the distinction that matters on an exam.

Term Working definition Distinction that matters
Prompt The instruction and context given to an AI assistant to steer its response A prompt guides output; it is not a permission or a guarantee of accuracy
Context Background information supplied so the assistant can interpret a request More context helps only when it is relevant and trustworthy
Instruction A specific directive about what to produce and how Vague instructions invite vague or invented output
Constraint A boundary the response must respect, such as format, length, or scope Constraints narrow output; they do not verify its truth
Grounding Tying a response to supplied, checkable source material Grounding reduces invention but still requires review
Hallucination A fluent, plausible statement that is unsupported or false Confidence in tone is not evidence of correctness
Verification Checking a claim against a reliable source before relying on it Verification is an action, not a feeling of certainty
Confidence score An indication of how likely a response or classification is to be correct A score expresses probability, not proof
Source The underlying material from which a claim can be confirmed A citation is useful only if the source actually supports the claim
Least privilege Giving an identity only the access needed for a specific task Least privilege is narrower than convenience and is the safer default
Permission Authorization to perform a specific action on a specific resource Having access to a system is not the same as being allowed to use all of it
Authentication Confirming who or what is making a request Authentication establishes identity, not entitlements
Authorization Deciding what an authenticated identity may do Authentication answers who; authorization answers what
Credential A secret used to prove identity, such as a key or token Credentials must be protected, rotated, and never placed in casual prompts
Secret Any sensitive value that should not be exposed in logs, prompts, or code A secret pasted into a prompt should be treated as exposed
Audit trail A record of what was requested, decided, and changed Auditability supports review and accountability after the fact
Data classification Labeling information by sensitivity, such as public, internal, or restricted The classification determines how data may be used and shared
Personal data Information that relates to an identifiable person Handling rules apply regardless of where the data is stored
Retention How long information is kept before it is disposed of Keeping data longer than necessary increases exposure
Human review Deliberate examination of output by a qualified person before use Review is a control, not a formality or a rubber stamp
Bias Systematic skew in data or output that favors some outcomes Neutral-sounding output can still carry biased assumptions
Transparency Being clear about how a result was produced and what it relies on Transparency helps a reviewer judge whether to trust a result
Automation Having a system carry out steps without manual intervention Automation amplifies both good and bad decisions
Escalation Routing a decision to someone with the appropriate authority or expertise Escalating is a valid answer when the risk or ambiguity is high
Surface The layer or interface at which a change is made Fixing the wrong surface leaves the real cause in place

Several entries form pairs: authentication and authorization, prompt and permission, grounding and verification. Exam options frequently swap one member for the other, and noticing the swap is a large part of the skill being tested.

Common mistake: Assuming that "the assistant said so" settles a question of fact. A response is a suggestion to be checked, not an authority to be obeyed.

How wrong answer options are built

Wrong options are rarely random. They are designed to attract a candidate who is reading quickly or reasoning superficially. Learning the families of distractors lets you discard an option for a structural reason rather than a vague feeling.

Absolute language. An option claiming something is always, never, guaranteed, or impossible asserts a certainty that responsible practice seldom supports. Foundations exams favor qualified answers because real work is conditional: it depends on the data, policy, risk, and context. Treat an unqualified claim as a prompt to look harder, not automatically as wrong, since occasionally the absolute statement is the correct rule.

Wrong surface. This distractor addresses a genuine problem but at the wrong layer. If the scenario describes sensitive data being exposed through an over-broad query, an option that rewrites the prompt wording does not fix the underlying access problem. If the issue is a missing fact in the source material, no amount of polite phrasing repairs it. Ask yourself where the failure actually originates and require the answer to act there.

Skipping verification. Some options move straight from a generated answer to a decision: publish it, send it, apply it. They omit the check that would catch an error. Since fluent output can be wrong, the answer that inserts a review step is usually safer. This pattern is especially tempting when the scenario emphasizes speed.

Over-privileged access. Here the option solves the task by granting far more capability than needed: full administrative rights, unrestricted data, or permanent rather than time-limited permission. It may work, but it violates least privilege. The correct option accomplishes the goal with the narrowest sufficient access.

Polished-but-wrong output. This is the most seductive family because the option itself is well written. It sounds professional and comprehensive, yet it contains a factual error, an unsupported claim, or a wrong assumption. Fluency is not a criterion of correctness. Check whether the option matches the facts in the stem and whether each claim can be traced to something reliable.

The table below turns these families into a quick recognition guide, with the telltale sign and the question that neutralizes each one.

Distractor family Telltale sign Question that neutralizes it
Absolute language always, never, guaranteed, impossible Is this really unconditional in every case described?
Wrong surface Fix targets a different layer from the cause Where does the problem actually originate?
Skipping verification Generated output is used directly What check would catch a plausible error?
Over-privileged access Broad, permanent, or administrative rights What is the narrowest access that would work?
Polished-but-wrong output Confident tone, unsupported specifics Which claims are supported by the stem or a trusted source?

Triage rules for scenario questions

Scenario questions reward a consistent reading sequence. The following triage rules are designed to be applied in order, so that each one narrows the field before the next begins.

Read the question line before the full paragraph. Knowing what is being asked lets you filter the story. If the question asks for the first action, details about later steps are noise; if it asks for the most likely risk, details about benefits are secondary.

Mark the constraints. As you read, note words that change the answer: only, except, first, best, most likely, should not, and after. These words define the decision. A candidate who misses "first" may choose an option that is correct eventually but wrong as an immediate step.

Identify the responsible default. For any scenario involving data, access, or uncertain output, the safe default is to verify, to minimize, and to ask when authorization is unclear. Holding this default in mind makes the correct option feel familiar and the reckless options feel obviously foreign.

Eliminate by family before comparing. Cross out options that use absolute language, fix the wrong surface, skip verification, or over-grant access. This often leaves only one or two options, which makes the final comparison much easier than trying to weigh four or five at once.

Compare on scope and reversibility. If two options remain, prefer the one with the smaller blast radius. An action that is narrow, reversible, and well recorded is safer than one that is broad and difficult to undo, all else being equal.

Commit and move. Once you have applied the sequence, choose and continue. Reopening settled items repeatedly consumes time and replaces a reasoned choice with a second-guessed one.

When two answers both look right

Almost every candidate encounters items where two options seem defensible. This is usually intentional. The exam is testing whether you can apply a finer criterion than "both sound reasonable." Use the following ladder, moving from the most objective test to the most subtle.

  1. Scope: Which option does the smaller, more precise thing? The answer that matches the exact task without extra capability is usually right.
  2. Sequence: Is one option an earlier step that must happen before the other? When the question asks what to do first, the earlier step wins even if the later one is also necessary.
  3. Verification: Does one option include a check that the other omits? The option that catches its own errors is stronger.
  4. Reversibility and record: Which action is easier to undo and leaves a clearer audit trail? Responsible practice favors traceability.
  5. Authority: Does one option require a decision the person in the scenario is not entitled to make? Escalation or approval may be the correct answer when authority is missing.
  6. Directness: Which option answers the question that was actually asked rather than an adjacent concern? A true but irrelevant statement is still a wrong answer.

If all six tests tie, trust your first reasoned instinct and flag the item for review if time remains. Changing an answer without a new reason tends to move marks from correct to incorrect, not the reverse.

Exam note: "Both are reasonable" is not a dead end. It is a signal that the exam wants you to apply a prioritizing principle such as least privilege, first-step ordering, or verification.

Time management and flagging

Time pressure changes behavior. Candidates rush easy items, linger on hard ones, and arrive at the end with no room to check anything. A simple time contract prevents most of this.

Divide your available time into broad blocks and set a personal check point at roughly the halfway mark and again near the end. These are check points, not rigid rules: at each, ask whether you are on pace and whether any flagged items deserve attention now. If you are behind, make up time by answering straightforward items decisively rather than by abandoning verification on difficult ones.

Adopt a two-pass approach. On the first pass, answer everything you can and flag any item that is genuinely uncertain. A flag is a promise to return, not a confession of failure. Do not let a single hard item consume time that belongs to many easy ones. When you flag, record a one-word reason if the interface allows notes, such as "authority" or "verify," so that a second look is quick.

On the second pass, handle flagged items with a cooler mind. Fresh context often makes the right option obvious. Change an answer only when you can articulate a concrete reason: a missed constraint, a misread word, or a newly recalled rule. If you cannot state the reason, keep the original choice.

Reserve the final minutes for administrative checks: confirm that every item has an answer, that no option was left blank by accident, and that flags were resolved. Nothing is gained by finishing early, and much is lost by leaving marks on the table through carelessness.

Time-management practice Why it helps What to avoid
Two-pass approach with flags Prevents one hard item from stealing many easy ones Stalling until an item is perfect
Midway and near-end check points Keeps pace visible before it is too late to adjust Watching the clock on every item
Reason notes on flagged items Makes the second pass fast and focused Vague flags with no reminder
Change only with a concrete reason Stops second-guessing from erasing correct answers Switching on a hunch
Final administrative sweep Catches blanks and unresolved flags Submitting the moment you finish

One-page revision sheet

The value of a revision sheet is not that it contains everything. It is that it contains the few ideas that most often decide questions, in a form you can scan in a minute. Print this or copy it by hand; the act of rewriting reinforces it.

ONE-PAGE REVISION SHEET — CCAO-F Foundations

CHECK POINTS BEFORE EVERY ANSWER
1. What is actually being asked? (circle: first / best / risk / exception)
2. What constraints matter? (privacy, permission, accuracy, deadline)
3. Where does the problem live? (prompt, data, permission, process)
4. Any absolute words? (always, never, guaranteed, only)
5. Any answer that grants too much access?
6. Any answer that skips verification?
7. Among survivors: smallest responsible action wins.

DISTRACTOR FAMILIES
- Absolute language        -> is it really unconditional?
- Wrong surface           -> fix the layer where the cause lives
- Skip verification       -> add the check
- Over-privileged access  -> narrow the permission
- Polished-but-wrong      -> check the claim, not the tone

TWO LOOK RIGHT? APPLY THE LADDER
scope -> sequence -> verification -> reversibility -> authority -> directness

SAFE DEFAULTS
- Verify before relying on fluent output.
- Use the minimum data and the minimum permission.
- Ask or escalate when authority is unclear.
- Leave an audit trail; prefer reversible actions.
- Deadlines never remove the responsible step.

KEY PAIRS (do not swap them)
- Authentication = who.        Authorization = what.
- Grounding = supplied source. Verification = checking it.
- Prompt = instruction.        Permission = entitlement.
- Confidence = probability.    Correctness = supported fact.

TIME CONTRACT
- First pass: answer what you know, flag the rest.
- Midway and near-end: check pace.
- Second pass: revisit flags; change only with a concrete reason.
- Final minute: no blanks, all flags resolved.

Keep the sheet visible during practice. After a few sessions you will consult it less, which is the point: the structure migrates from the page into your working habits.

Worked scenario

To see the checklist in action, consider a realistic item.

A team wants an AI assistant to summarize internal customer feedback so that a product group can spot recurring complaints. The assistant produces a polished, confident summary. One analyst proposes pasting the entire feedback database into the prompt each time, including names and contact details, so the assistant has "everything it needs." A second analyst proposes granting the assistant a broad administrative account so it can fetch whatever it wants on demand. A third proposes taking the summary at face value because it reads well and the deadline is close. The question asks what the reviewer should do first.

Apply the sequence. The ask is about the first action, so downstream deployment details are irrelevant. The constraints are privacy (names and contact details), accuracy (a polished summary may still be wrong), and access (who may read the feedback).

Now classify the options. Pasting the full database exposes personal data far beyond what a summary requires: over-broad data use and a wrong-surface fix, since more input does not guarantee a safe summary. Granting an administrative account is over-privileged access: the task needs read access to a limited dataset, not full control. Accepting the summary because it reads well is the polished-but-wrong trap combined with skipping verification, and the deadline is a pressure device in the stem.

The responsible first action is to establish the approved way to handle the data and verify the summary against a trusted source before use. That means using only permitted data, removing personal details where the rule requires it, and limiting access to what the task needs. It also means checking the claims against the underlying feedback rather than trusting fluent prose. If the handling rule is unclear, ask the party responsible for data governance rather than proceed on assumption.

Notice what made the difference. The correct path was not the most sophisticated-sounding option. It was the one that minimized exposure, matched access to need, and inserted a check. Every wrong option was attractive for a different reason: completeness, convenience, confidence, or speed. Naming those reasons is what lets you discard the options quickly next time.

Common mistake: Treating "more context" as an unqualified good. Relevant, permitted, trustworthy context helps. Excess sensitive data adds risk without adding reliability.

Common mistakes

  • Reading the scenario paragraph before the question and absorbing details that do not affect the answer.
  • Missing qualifier words such as first, except, only, and should not, then selecting an option that answers a slightly different question.
  • Treating confident, well-written output as verified fact.
  • Choosing the option that grants the broadest access because it seems more capable or convenient.
  • Applying a fix at the wrong layer, such as rephrasing a prompt to solve a data or permissions problem.
  • Letting a stated deadline push you into skipping a review or verification step.
  • Changing a flagged answer on a hunch without a concrete reason.
  • Spending too long on one difficult item and rushing the straightforward items that follow.

Chapter summary

  • A short, ordered checklist beats improvisation under pressure: ask what is being asked, identify constraints, locate the cause, screen for absolutes, check access scope, require verification, then choose the smallest responsible action.
  • Know the pairs that are commonly swapped: authentication versus authorization, grounding versus verification, prompt versus permission, confidence versus correctness.
  • Recognize the five distractor families: absolute language, wrong surface, skipping verification, over-privileged access, and polished-but-wrong output.
  • Triage scenario questions by reading the ask first, marking constraints, eliminating by family, comparing on scope and reversibility, and committing.
  • When two answers look right, apply the ladder: scope, sequence, verification, reversibility, authority, directness.
  • Use a two-pass approach with flags, check pace at midway and near the end, change answers only for concrete reasons, and finish with an administrative sweep.

Checkpoint questions

  1. A scenario asks what a reviewer should do first when an assistant returns a confident answer that will be published. Which option is most defensible?

A. Publish it immediately because the confident tone indicates accuracy.

B. Verify the key claims against a reliable source before publication.

C. Rewrite the prompt to sound more formal and publish the result.

D. Grant the assistant publishing rights so it can handle future output directly.

  1. Which phrase is the strongest warning sign of a distractor built on absolute language?

A. "in most cases described"

B. "subject to the stated policy"

C. "guaranteed to be correct in every situation"

D. "after an appropriate review"

  1. A task needs to read one field from one record. Which option best reflects least privilege?

A. Give an account full administrative access to be safe.

B. Give the assistant permanent access to the entire dataset.

C. Give the minimum time-limited access to the specific field needed.

D. Give access to a colleague who already has broad rights.

  1. The stem describes sensitive data leaking because a query pulls more fields than needed. Which proposed fix acts at the correct surface?

A. Make the assistant's wording more cautious.

B. Restrict the query to only the fields the task requires.

C. Add a closing line thanking the user.

D. Increase the amount of context in the prompt.

  1. Two answer options both seem correct. Which is the best first tie-breaker?

A. Choose the option written in the most formal language.

B. Choose the option that mentions the most features.

C. Choose the option with the narrowest scope that still solves the task.

D. Choose whichever option appears first in the list.

  1. What is the main purpose of flagging an uncertain item during a timed exam?

A. To record that the item should be answered on instinct alone.

B. To return to it on a second pass without letting it block other items.

C. To justify leaving the item blank at the end.

D. To signal that verification is unnecessary for that item.

  1. Which statement correctly distinguishes grounding from verification?

A. They are the same process with different names.

B. Grounding checks a claim; verification supplies source material.

C. Grounding ties output to supplied sources; verification checks claims against a reliable source.

D. Verification only applies to written text, while grounding applies to images.

Answers and rationale

  1. B. Verification before publication is the responsible first action when accuracy matters and output is confidently expressed. A is wrong because tone is not evidence and it skips verification. C is wrong because changing wording does not test accuracy; it is a wrong-surface fix. D is wrong because it grants extra permissions unrelated to the immediate question and violates least privilege.

  2. C. "Guaranteed to be correct in every situation" is an unconditional claim and a classic absolute-language distractor. A is qualified with "in most cases," so it is not absolute. B is conditional on policy and therefore appropriately qualified. D includes a review step and is a responsible, qualified statement.

  3. C. Time-limited access to the specific field is the narrowest permission that accomplishes the task, which is least privilege in action. A is over-privileged and unnecessary. B is both over-broad in scope and permanent in duration. D borrows someone else's broad rights, which still exceeds what the task needs and obscures accountability.

  4. B. The cause is a query that pulls too many fields, so restricting the query fixes the layer where the problem lives. A changes wording without changing data exposure, a wrong-surface fix. C is irrelevant to the problem. D adds more context, which can increase exposure rather than reduce it.

  5. C. Scope is the first and most objective tie-breaker: the narrowest option that still solves the task is usually the intended answer. A mistakes style for substance. B mistakes feature count for correctness. D relies on position, which carries no exam meaning.

  6. B. Flagging lets you protect the rest of the first pass and revisit the item with fresh attention later. A is wrong because instinct alone is not the goal; reasoned selection is. C is wrong because flagged items should be resolved, not abandoned. D is wrong because uncertainty is precisely when verification matters most.

  7. C. Grounding connects output to supplied source material, while verification is the act of checking a claim against something reliable. A is wrong because the terms describe different activities. B reverses the definitions. D is wrong because both concepts apply broadly and are not divided by content type.

You now have a compact system for the exam: a checklist for decisions, a vocabulary for precision, a catalog of distractor patterns, a triage sequence, a tie-breaking ladder, and a time plan. The habit of verifying, minimizing, and pausing to ask is not just an exam strategy; it is the same habit that makes an AI assistant genuinely useful and safe in real work. Trust the process you have built, read each item carefully, and finish as you have practiced: deliberately, calmly, and one question at a time.

End of chapter 20.
